THE ONLY WAY.

Unless, therefore, one has expert knowledge or the service of such, buying a second-hand mount is apt to be somewhat uncertain pro- position. Unless substantial reasons are given it is usually fairly safe to give a courteous but firm "No, thank you" to the offerer of a great snip "—especially at these times, when second-hand machines are at a premium. And, on the other hand, it is wise to go to a good accredited agent for your purchase. Quite a number of dealers now specialise in used machines, and one at least to our knowledge thoroughly overhauls every mount before it passes .out of his hands, and gives a pledge of efficiency with it. Buying from the dealer may I mean- paying,, a little extra for the motor cycle. but it is surely worth while if one has his reputation as a backing of the " rightness " of the purchase. And this extra is often quite a small amount, -because it frequently happens that the machine on which you have your eye has been taken in part exchange for a new model,- and therefore, the profit margin can be kept within narrow limits.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
AFTER THE WAR.

A very difficult problem awaits solution when the war is over, and that is what is going to be done with the thousands of motor cars now being used for transport and ambulance work. I can't even hazard a guess at the number of cars now in actual use, but the number must be stupendous. Many of these cars will, of course, be unfit for further work when the war is over; many others will be retained by the military authorities. At the same time, there is bound to be a very large number of good, serviceable cars, and if these are allowed to flood the market at upset prices it is going to hit the manu- facturers very hard indeed. It is stated that overtures have already been made to the Government with a view to buying them up, and unless the legitimate motor trade moves quickly in the matter speculators will spring up who will make it their business to acquire these vehicles, repair them near enough to make them saleable and flood the market with cars bearing well-known names at prices far below those at present ruling for such good second- hand vehicles. The motor trade should act, and act quickly.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
KILLED WITHOUT BEING STRUCK.

Explesionp That Cause Stoppage

Of Blood Circulation.

One of the consequeaces of the present wai will be to revolutionisa the art of killing , human beings with gun•fire, this being th( final object of artillery, Ind not the wanton ' destruction af houses, buildings, cat-her:lrak aiad the making of mute or less larg‘. holes in the ground. On the various fields oi battle men have frequently been found stark killed exactly in the position they occupied when the shell which killed them exploded, the men showing no sign of wound. These men when they met their death were generally pro- tected from the direct action of the bursting shells. The shells in question are those fired by the French 75-mm; guns. There is no noxious gas known which is capable of produc- ing such an instantaneous effect, and certainly none is evolved from the shells, seeing that the high temperatures developed by the detona- tion of the high explosives now used for charg- ing shells, explosives which are trinitrated hydrocarbides (benzol, phenol, toluol, naphthol) of the aromatic series, can only lead to the giving out of carbon monoxide and cyanogen, two gases which aye but slow in their action. The men are killed by a sudden stoppage in blood circulation, and it is interesting to note in this connection an explanation put forward by Mr. R. Arnoux before the French Society of Civil Engineers after the reading of a paper, on May 28th, by Major Ferrus, on French, German, and Austrian guns. Mr. Arnoux re- ceived in January last, from a superior officer commanding at the Front a pocket aneroid barometer which had been rendered unservice- able by the explosion close to it of a German high-explosive shell. On examination, the speaker found the barometer to have been simply put out of order. It had ceased to act because one of the two transmission levers, connected with the hand, and which normally bears on the other lever, had shifted and had, instead, taken up a position underneath this other lever. This could only have been pro- duced by an abnormal swelling out of the aneroid system, due, as is manifest, to a con- siderable barometric depression; the little in- strument had acted as a minimum recording instrument. After shifting the two levers back to their original position by hand, and then placing the instrument under the bell of a vacuum machine, it was found that the two levers changed their position when the baro- metric pressure fell to 410 mm. (16in.) of mercury, .which corresponds approximately to the pressure at the top of Mont Blanc, the altitude of which is 4,810 m. (15,780 ft.

High Explosives More Deadly Than Shrapnel. The explosion, therefore, had led to a sudden barometric or static depression in the room where the instrument was placed—a distance of lees than 3m. (less than 10ft.) from the point where the shell burst, of at least 760-410 equal 350 mm. (13.8 in. of mercury, corresponding, from the formula) generally used in aerody- namics, to a driving velocity of the air of 276 m. (905 f t.) per second and to a dynamic pressure of 10,360 kg. per sq. m. (2,1231 b. per sq. ft.). Such a dynamic pressure, however produced, has for effect to hurl up into the air, or to crush on the ground, all tho living beings directly exposed to it. whilst in the case of those who are sheltered behind any kind of protection, the very sudden static depression of the surrounding atmosphere which ensues comes into play. In regard to these latter, the air and carbonic acid in solution in the blood, in a pro- portion which is the larger the higher the am- bient atmospheric pressure, are disengaged from the blood in the shape of minute gaseous bubbles as soon as the pressure decreases too suddenly from any cause- Those bubbles are driven into the small arteries under the influ- ence, of this, pressure exerted by the heart. If their diameter is greater' than that of the small arteries, they form so many gaseous plugs.. which instantaneously "stop the blood circulation. :They are produced the instant the' atmospheric depression takes place, and death occurs 'before 'there. is time for them to dissolve back in the blood when the ambient atmospheric pressure returns to normal. The sudden action required to bring about such a thing is a characteristic feature of the shattering nitrated explosives /low used, which combined power and instantaneity both far in excess of those possible with the use of black powder, The projectiles charged with these explosives were at first destined to destroy .all kinds of fortified works and to demolish trenches, and the discovery of their unexpected action upon living beings is quite as ,important as the discovery of the new explosives 'themselves. Their radius of action is less than the possible one of shrapnel, but they are more deadly than the shrapnel, for in their radius of action no living being escapes, whilst ,the shrapnel is dangerous only when one cf its balls or fragments strikes home.—" Engineering.'l

A NORTH DEVON TRIP.

(Queries and correspondence should be addressed to the Editor. Replies cannot be sent by post, And all queries will be answered in our columns.

Devonshire in the early autumn possesses a charm found nowhere else in England. Others may not perhaps agree with me, but I always con- sider that the best time of all the year to see this glorious county is early in October. A trip. which is particularly lovely at this season of the year is along the northern coast line, returning from Taunton, the starting point, by a more southerly route. Leaving Taunton by North-street, cross the River Tone and make for Langford Bridge and Bishops Lydeard ; thence to Seven Ash, Bicknoller, awl "Washford to Minehead, one of the most lovely spots of a lovely district. The views from Minehead across the Bristol Channel are superb. Leave the town by Park-street, and make for Holmcote, Porlock, Countisbury and Lynmouth, another celebrated "beauty spot." After leaving Porlock turn sharply to the left past the Ship Hotel, and enter a gate follow- ing a road through a private enclosure, which is, however, open to cyclists. The road is 44 miles long, when it rejoins the main road. Then follows a very hilly ride to Lynmeuth. Thence follow the road through Lynton, Paracombe, Combemartin, to Ilfracombe, with its magnifi- cent cliffs and superb views. From Ilfracombe to Barnstaple is a hilly but pleasant ride of 111 miles. Leave Barnstaple by the eastern road, following the telegraph wires along the main road. The ride back to Taunton, passing along the southern extremity of Exmoor Forest, is a most delightful one, and completes the round trip of 121 miles. On the way are passed South Molton, Bampton, Wiveliscombe, Milverton and Langford Bridge.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTRIC LAMPS FOR CYCLES.

It is somewhat strange that the electric cycle lamp has not gained a greater degree of popu- larity. Of course, I realise that the initial cost is much greater than in the case of oil or acetylene lamps, but even when money is not the only consideration the latter illuminants still hold their own. Perhaps now that electric lighting sets are beinr, more widely used for cars the cyclist will follow the motorist's ex- ample. There is no doubt that a good electric lamp gives a light equal, if not superior to acetylene, with very little sacrifice of weight. It has no oil to escape on to tyres, &c., to leak whenever it happens to be required, and no unpleasant smell of carbide! Once fixed on to the machine the electric lamp affords a maxi- mum of illumination with a minimum- of trouble. Of the two kinds now on the market, the accumulator and the magneto, the latter is generally considered preferable. Just as mag- netic ignition has 'superseded the accumulators of a few years ago, the Magneto lamp for car and cycle seems likely to displace its rival. The average magneto cycle lamp will give quite a good light, when travelling at a walking pace, and requires no attention whatever, whereas accumulators are always liable to run out at awkward moments when a lamp is absolutely necessary. A small torch is handy for use when the machine is stationary. Bulbs, the only source of trouble, last a remarkably long time, and can always be renewed with very little expense.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
TAKING A CYCLE BY RAIL.

It is not often that I want to take my cycle by train, but sometimes it does happen. Last week I had occasion to take my machine a com- paratively short distance, and it struck me' again, as it has struck me many times before, how the railway companies seem to adopt the same attitude towards cycles as they did to- wards canals; in other words, opposition plus neglect. First and foremost, charges are ex- cessive, particularly in the case of short journeys. True, several lines have introduced combined tickets for cycle and passenger for half-day trips into the country, but tares ar,3 still high. Another bone of contention is the limitation ef liability to damage over the sum of ten shillings. I wonder how many repair bills amount to anything between 9s. 6d. and Ps. lid! Although an extra ticket costing one penny, and covering all damage can always be pur- chased its sale is not pushed to say the least of it. Cyclists will do well to examine their machines after a sojourn in the left luggage office as such items as lamps, &c., often become detached through careless handling.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
SECOND-HAND "BARGAINS."

A writer in a recent issue of the Autocycle makes some pertinent remarks anent the con- siderable traffic which ig now being done in second-hand motor cycles. His advice having been asked as to the value of such and such a machine he is at pains to point out that it is a matter which is impossible to decide without actually examining the motor cycle, as so much' depends upon the manner in which it has been handled. It is well-known that two apparently identical new motor cycles of the same output batch will vary to a surprising degree in power—nothwith- standing the fact that they are made, assembled and tested in one batch. This being so, it is quite obvious that after twelve months' or two years' running the variation will be even greater, though it may so happen the pre- viously inferior mount is now the superior. It is even difficult to base the value of a motor cycle on its mileage, for here again one owner with considerate riding and care will have his machine in better " nick " after five thousand miles than other with only say, one-fifth of this distance to his credit. "You can't judge cigars by the picture on the box," ran an alleged comic

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 2, 1915.

believe that every human soul was of untold worth in the divine economy. When we con- sidered the untold myriads which had trodden this earth of ours, it was not easy to hold by the faith that each individual unit in the long procession of mankind had an inestimable value. It was quite impossible that any of us could have escaped the tremendous nature of this question, and it was surely impossible not to feel that in these days the question was set, us with a new insistency. Across the great tracts of France, where last autumn the Ger-, man hordes swept right up to the Marne in line with 'Paris, one might find many •a nameless grave; in many a churchyard where in times of peace some great monument stood over the tomb of a :single individual, one might find a simple cross marking' the common grave of a whole 'erowd of men;- between the trenches in the fighting line the ground lay deep in many a place with unburied bodies; we counted our armies by the million, and each man in them went his way with the knowledge that life was uncertain at the best. We need not be sur- prised if in many a soul there was a battle taking place, a battle between a kind of fatalism born of the sense of the insignificance of the individual and a faith that sought to hold by the infinite value which God set on every separate child of His. The simple fact emerged that the exigencies of this strange and tragic time were pointing again to the very fundamentals of religious faith, and compelling us to face the very basis of all religion. For religion was in its deepest as well as in its simplest reality the fact of our personal relationship with God, and the belief that what we were and what became of us were things of moment in His sight.

OPFICER (to boy of .thirteen who, in his effort to get taken on as a bugler, has given his age as sixteen) : "Do you know where boys go to who tell lies?" APPLICANT: "To the Front, sir."

to-night will be quite unusual as far as this pulpit is concerned, but I hope it may be pos- sible to gather it up at the finish into a spiritual message. Extraordinary Mixture of Men.

I think the first thing to• be said is that our armies in France comprise a most extraordinary mixture of men all fired by one spirit. One early gets the impression, and it is confirmed) the longer you are there, of a tremendous variety in type unified by a common purpose and motive. I suppose everyone ,here 'to-night has heard and perhaps told some of those stories illustrating how men of material resources are serving at this time in the humblest ranks of the British Army. Well, of course the fact is literally true that there are privates who have ' to make time to write home about their estates, and who are working and sleeping with men who held the lowliest stations in Society. It soon ceases to be a matter almost for comment that you should find the most diverse individuals jumbled together and quite probably finding the experience interesting enough. At the Y.M.C.A. centre where I It as stationed, we started a library. It may sound strange news, but it is perfectly true. It would not have reminded you of the affair in Museum-street. We got a number of books sent out from England, books of all description; they were too. Not a great number if .them, but just about 'as varied as the make-up of the British Army. It was wonderful how John Bunyan, and Dr. Dale, and Henry Ward Beecher, and Henry Prummend, and Mi's. Henry Wood, and W. W. Jacobs, and Mark Twain, all rubbed up against each other in'oui• library. It would have done them all a lot of good to do it in real life. Well, the day we got it going, there came a private who had heard of the enterprise. He said: "Have you got a Hebrew Grammar ?" Do you know what he was doing? He was scrubbing tables and making roads and carrying water, and doing orderly for a hospital tent, and he was a divinity student of one of the foremost universities in Canada. and well on to the finish of his course. It would be possible to go on telling Of instances like that all night. The men at the Front are just a multitude of the most diverse individuals coming from every kind of life and occupation here at home, drawn from all strata of Society, and yet all permeated by a spirit of unity Which makes them into a perfect whole through the possession of a common object and the in- spiration of a common cause. Easy-going Optimism Disappears.

And then if you ask how the spirit of the men at the Front impresses one, the answer is that it impresses by its courage and its cheerful- ness. You find courage and cheerfulness in face of an arduous task. It is quite certain that no one is across in France• very long without realising the tremendous task we are up against. Probably we are all feeling that at home more than we did in the early stages of the war, and the easy-going optimism which expected an early evacuation of Belgium and France by the German forces has now dis- appeared in this country. But however that may be at home, it is certainly true at the Front, that no one can possibly mistake the grim difficulty of securing an issue to the campaign in the West by actual fighting. Whatever may be done in that direc-

REV. lg. T. JAMES' RETURN FROM FRANCE.

THE STUPENDOUS TRUTH AND THE SITUATION.

FUNDAMENTALS OF FAITH.

Tragic Times _lmpel Us Towards

Them.

At the Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday, , the Pastor (the Rev. T. T. James) reoccupied the pulpit after returning from three months' service with the Y.M.C.A. amongst the troops in France. At his suggestion, it will be remembered, Mr. James forewent his annual month's .holiday, and at a special church meeting was granted leave for the purpose stated. Leaving War- rington early in June, Mr. James journeyed to Dulwich, where for a few days he was busily occupied in duty for the Y.M.C.A. with four brigades of the R;F.A. , He then crossed to the' Continent, and for a short interval remained right down at the base camp where he landed; but soon afterwards travelled eighty miles north to a great troop-centre, and here he has remained since, working in and around one of the Y.M.C.A. wooden "huts," which have now become celebrated (it would be safe to say) throughout Europe. In practically his first message from the Front—received by Mr. Ziba Armitage—Mr. James said: "The spirit of the men is splendid, and they are always brave and cheerful. Some of the badly wounded keep as bright as possible." Later he wrote: "There is a little burying ground here, where the wind plays through the pines, and we are often putting brave English boys there for their last sleep," whilst latterly he wrote: "We have had a month of hard work out here during August with some hot weather, which has been in strong contrast to the wet and often chilly days of July." The situation was "not far from a famous and once very beautiful city which has been laid low by German shell, fire," and the duties were onerous. In addition to the •c,onducting of meetings, interviewing the men, reading to them and writing their letters, counselling the sick and wounded, burying the dead, and systematically visiting three large field hos- pitals, Mr. James kept in close touch with his Warrington affairs, and maintained a wide- spread correspondence with his many friends. His talks and travels have afforded him a deep and intimate knowledge of the progress and conduct of the war; but naturally Mr. James observes reticence in speaking of such matters, and has contented himself with the exposition of principle. Similarly he does not mention the success which has attended his work

abroad, but we are able definitely to state that it ha a carried everything before it, and earned him the well-deserved popularity and respect of which the members of his church were so con- fident when they permitted him to go. - Enthusiastic as were the meetings which gave God-speed to the departing pastor, even greater have proved those at which he has been wel- comed back in safety, health and vigour. VALUE OF THE INDIVIDUAL.

The morning text was Luke xv., "What man of. you, having a hundred sheep, and hay- ing lost one of them, doth not leave .the ninety and nine in the wilderness and go after that which is lost, until he find it?" The preacher said this was not only a general picture of the heart of God, the love that would not let us go, but it was also an unforgettable statement oi: the value of the individual in God's sight. There was a profound significance in this swift sketch which placed the great body of the flock on one hand and the single member of it on the other.

How intimately the stupendous truth was related to the situation of the moment! He heartily 'wished it had been possible to come home to them with the impression that they were near the end of the long, disastrous con- flict, but it seemed to him quite futile to fore- cast an early peace. We were still living through days of crisis. The externals of this struggle were interesting enough, and provided the staple of our thought and conversation every day. The relative material resources of nations in deadly combat, the balancing of country against country, and army against army, the intricate and dimly perceived efforts of diplomatists on one side and on another, the economic burden springing from an exhaustion which wOild not .be made good for a generation to come, ,the actual progress achieved by sword and fire upon the field of, battle,, all these made up the viSible and apparent spectacle* with which we became too sadly familiar as the days went by. Very striking it all was, this out. ward aspect of war, and horrible enough in many of its manifestations. A man needed to be at a considerable distance from it all to continue to believe in the glories of war.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
To the Uttermost Cost.

Out at the seat of war one might find the actual warfare with all it entailed of endurance and courage and sacrifice , and suffering; and one might find an intense interest in the -cause for which sacrifice was being made, a sense of the wrong that had been done to a defenceless nation, and a patriotism prepared to pay the uttermost cost of loyalty; but if one went down deep enough one could find as well all the funda- mental problems pf soul being restated with a new note of urgency, and men wanting to be made sure of God, of God's concern for them, of the significance of the individual in the ultimate issue of things, of the fact of life beyond thb gates of death. _ _

And was this not true for us and him as well, and was it not also the problem of our

own personality? There was a sense in which science had made it easy to believe in God. How wonderful was the story of this universe of ours! What a magic story had evolution disclosed to us! All this shaping and planning, all this ceaseless energy and unfailing law, all this growth and development through the ages: it was not difficult to believe, that in the end the whole thing should be perfected and completed. It must be so or God was wasting His time. The perfect whole was 'already in the mind of the Eternal. But to believe that every single link in the chain, and every individual in the race, was something of infinit& and, eternal value, was not the easiest but often the most difficult of tasks. We might be finding it so as we confronted what was happening day by day.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
Problems Of Human Mind.

But he had not come to talk, about the ex- ternals of this conflict; when one was' in the midst of it the externals were, all impressive .enough, but by,-„and by:. One became- impressed :by the. great fact that one was in the presence of unseen, and often unstated :problems of the human Mind. :It was one thing .to view a huge army.and think about its material power as a fighting .machine, as an engine for aggressive or defensive purposes; it Was another thing to realise that the huge army was made up of separate individual personalities, each turning ,over the problems and questions which a strange and startling experience,- presented., It ,was one thing to ealculate:lbsses-from time to time as so many deductions to be made from the effective numbers which a country could put:, into, the field; it was another tihng., to realise that the • great problem of pain and- suffering had been Set every disabled and wounded man in terms of personal experience, and that somehow he had got to solve it for himself. And so he wanted to speak about the great fundamental problems of the soul which were all being stated with a new insistency in this hour, of tragedy, and which no man with his eyes open and his heart in the right place c.nild avoil nieAirr;-. . With a New Insistency. Tn ordinary times it was not easy always to

Love That Left No One Out.

- And out there where men knew what it meant to stare death in the face, one could feel a hush sometimes when they read how Jesus said that not a sparrow fell to the ground without the Father knowing it. They had so often been ready to dismiss the statement with a smile. Out there men did not smile at it! It was perfectly safe to say that religion was never, laughed at; realities were far too near for that. And when one read the words of Christ, if men did not believe them they sat there and wished they did. And one could see something in men's faces when they read how Jesus said that God was like a shepherd who lost one sheep out of the whole flock and went to look for it; for it told them that the best and purest of the Sons of Men came with a message for each, the love that left no one out. And though the Blessed ILord never argued about these 'things, but simply, said them, yet.komehow He had got more 'people to listen 'and believe them than anyone else in the wide world.

The Primary Questions. This, was what he meant by "religion and 'the war." He did not mean the question of the effect of the war upon the Church, though it was a qdestion intimately and indissolubly related to all they had been saying; nor yet the question of the reconciliation of war and Christianity, whether they considered that to be possible or not; but the simple fact that the experiences of these strange days were driving the soul back Upon the primary questions: "Is this Owl's-world; am His child, does it matter to Him what I d 9 or what beeomes of me, is the life given to 111 Q something of indestructible and imperishable value in His sight?" And the faith that answered," Yes" had got the making of the future.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
MEN AT THE FRONT.

Their Occupations, Ambitions, And Thoughts.

Preaching to a large congregation in the evening, Mr. James took for his text 2 Samuel, x. 12: "Be of good courage; and let us play the man for our people, and for the- Cities of our God." The preacher said:7

" Ue men at the Front "—I want to speak about them. You will understand that this is not intended for a sermon in the sense of the exposition of a text, but I put these words selected before what I am going to say. because they seem, though spoken long ago, to be a fair statement of the spirit of the men who hate gone out from oUr own country and are at present fighting our battles in foreign lands. The fact is that a great deal of what will be said

tion will be only achieved by the greatest exer- cise of the qualities of endurance and deter- mination, and it is bound to be at a tremendous cost in human life. Yet so far from optimism having disappeared from the men who are on the scene of actual conflict, it is always there, and s with a fuller realisation of the heavy demands that Are` being made and must be made upon them, their spirit remains as high as ever. People at home may sometimes get into a panic, but out there courage and hope- fulness are always dominant.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
Humour and Lightheartedness.

And the cheerfulness away there is a wonder- ful thing; it might almost be called the light- heartedness which is prevalent. It almost comes as a shock at first to hear the fun and frolic and laughter that can pervade these men. • It would be possible to write a book •on "Humour at the Front." I do not think you can understand it, or sympathise with it unless you have been there. But then you can begin to understand it; you can understand how the grim tragedy . day after day will drive men mad unless they insist on seeing the lighter side of things wherever it is possible. A sense of humour is one of God's gifts in the hardship and grimness of this business to keep a man's heart from breaking. If I am speaking to-night to anyone who has been in the trenches he will know what it means to thank God for the man who can make a joke. There is a cheerfulness which is the refuge from unthinkable things, the delivery from madness, the escape from sheer stark collapse of the mind.

Pathos Which Amazes.

And then I want to speak about the courage in the midst of suffering. You do not want me to harrow your feelings with descriptions, and I certainly do not intend to do so. The, appear- /ance of•the men whoiwere brought in with their bodies branded by liquid fire was beyond any- thing that we would care to describe. You would rather hear how wonderfully, right there under canvas, medical skill was directed to its mission of saving and healing wherever possible, and how soon the dull, tired faces of the wounded began to wear a smile of happiness. There are some things which are stamped in- delibly on the memory. One is that of a day when a concert party from England reached the point where we were situated, and in the Y3I.C.A. hut six hundred men who had stared death in the face and come back wounded from the trenches, listened to seine of the old English songs and sang themselves when the chance arrived. You may sing "Till the boys come home" and find it perhaps nothing extraordin- ary as poetry, but when you look into the faces of men to whom home has become a mere per- adventure, there is a pathos in it which amazes • yo.u.

Nor shall I forget how the same day in a grew hospital tent forty stricken men were brought ii

on their beds, and all around the wounded ho were well enough made a circle, line behind line of blue. clad figures, and how beyond that we ' could see the 'green hills of a stricken country, with the blue sky above it all, and the tears ran down men's faces as they listened to a woman's voiee sing "The Hills of 4pnegal," so that when it was over you could Wear nothing but the flapping of the tent until all these broken ones of the battle field burst into such applause as they could give. Sometimes you discover how conditions can give soul to a song. Did you read Patrick Mac Gill's description of the trenches the other day, and of how the lads mile out of them, marching back to their billets

after the fire and the shot, singing: Here we are, here we are. Here we are again? - ~

The most tawdry stuff, truly, sung in the smoke and fume of a music hall; but somehow there is a soul in it sung in the cmoke ai.d itme of a battle field, by men going back out of the ,mouth of death, who know quite well that many a lad is missing after all. The courage and the cheerfulness in the midst of suffering is a wonderful thing, and sometimes one can only thank God and marvel at it. The Wonderful Y.M.C.A.

And then I want to speak to yo.o in these last moments about the courage and strength which spring from a religious faith. The Y.M.C.A. has done a wonderful work both at home and in France, and certainly not an insiuiü. nt ptrt of it has been the provision of a counter-attrac- tion to the canteen, the setting up of centres at home and abroad where men could spend such spare time as they had, where they could pro- cure food and drink, find recreation and social fellowship. I would like here to-night to pay my tribute to the wonderful way in which the Y.M.C.A. has planted its organisations through- out the length and breadth of this country amongst the British troops, and in France right from the main base up now to the fighting line 'itself. And I would like. to emphasise the fact that it has not only done great service in the provision of opportunities of a social and recreative character, but above and beyond that it has opened a wide field for definite religious and spiritual work. Within the walls of its buildings you may find varieoypes of religious. gatherings, from the large 'services attended by hundreds of men, to the little circle which meets for prayer from time to time. I have seen men back from the shot and shell finding time to come to an intercession service for those at home. Intercession services at home ought to need less announcing when we realise these things.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
"Of a Unique Nature."

I think it is true to say that there is a very wonderful religious awakening taking place amongst many of our lads abroad. The best thing for a man to do who goes out there for religious work, if he is a preacher, is to tear up all his old sermons before he starts, or at any rate to take the precaution of leaving them at home. He will find they are no use out there. I do not mean that the simplicities of the Christian Gospel are not the great require- ments there: they are the only truths of any use. But Ido mean that he will find himself in a new situation, dealing with conditions: of a unique nature, and he will have to learn to set whatever message he has in the line of a strange experience and unusual surroundings. Then indeed it is possible to discover a great spiritual opportunity,. Alen who _are face to face with the great realities are ready to 'listen to religious truth, and many who have for long enough been indifferent to the spiritual get an awakening of the soul. It is an experience different from any other to speak to men who have stood side by. side in the trenches, and faced with each other the horrors of this ghastly thing, and there is often an answer in their hearts to a religious appeal, and a demand too for the deepest things, quite arresting in its nature. lam not saying that this is so with all of them, nor that all the men who will return are coming home "converted,' but that a widespread religious awakening has come to many of them is a fact that is inescapable. And these lads, many of them, will be coming back to us at the end of the war. They will not all be coming back—l wish it were possible indeed. Bt4 many of them will he uoming hock to cur cities and our churches; they will be coming back with this new found religious experience. 'lt rests upon those who are at home, it rests upon the churches here at home to see to it that they find here that atmosphere of spiritual reality, thtlf vivid sense of spiritual life, which will nurture and maintain the faith that they will bring with them. It will be a tragedy if men who have found God on foreign shores discover that those at home have not a keen and vigorous spiritual experience. It is the work of the Church of Christ to seek and culti- vate that vitality of power which will 'make

them fit and ready to -welcome those who will return.

Mr. W. IT. Payton presiding at the organ, the choir effectively rendered the anthem "Be Glad And Rejoice," the solo part was taken by Mr. S. Davies.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT THE HUTS ARE LIKE.

Striking Address After P.S.A. Welcome.

An open and well filled meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. on Sunday also gave a hearty welcome to their President, Mr. James. Mr. B. Farrar occupied the chair, and the lesson was read by Mr. H. Osborne. In her usual splendid and rich style, Mrs. Smallshaw sang: "But The Lord Is Mindful" and "Nearer, 'My God, To Thee." The Chairman' said it gladdened one's heart to see so many there to welcome one who had been near and dear to them by his life and works for a long time now. The three months of his absence seemed a long time to look for- ward to at the commencement, but looking back it did not seem so long. That was the very thing about all experiences—when one looked one saw mountains of difficulties, but looking back it did not appear much. Mr. Farrar then mentioned the receipt of letters from the following brothers with the troops: Sergeant J. Oxley, R.G.A., in France; Sapper G. C. Pye, in France; Private A. Knowles, 214th South Lancashires (machine gun section), in Surrey; Private Harry Kent, in Frante; Private , Joseph Bond, of the Terri- torials, now in a Hertfordshire hospital recover- ing from recent wounds; Sergeant. A. Spencer, 10th Lancashire Fusiliers, in France; and Lance-Sergeant F. Andrews. Private Leather (a member on leave), Private Hartwell, of the King's Royal Rifles (a visitor) were welcomed to the meeting's midst. THE ALL-IMPORTANT POST BAG.

Mr. James, who was well received, thanked the members for their welcome, and went on to refer to the letters just read. It was a great thing, he said, when the lads who were away could write to them letters, which could be read in their midst: because he could tell now from experience that they did think of the folks at ham% and it was good for them to feel they were remembered. As he had said before the post bag was the •great institution. Yet, perhaps, sometimes they did not take the trouble to write; if only they could see it they would write, 'for it was worth their while, and did a great deal of good out there. Of course it was a splendid thing that the lads were able to write such long letters to them; for it was hard to do it under the circumstances, and a letter often represented as much trouble and inconvenience as it took to write a dozen at home. They really represented the enthusiasm of their brothers, and, their continued hope that the home. work -would-prosper.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
"Temperance Drinks, Thank God."

Speaking of the Y.M.C.A. work, the speaker said lints :had , been. erected , in, all parts of France wherever possible; A hut was a big' building which held five or six hundred. At one of the " main " huts was a platform, and at the other a counter where refreshments and drinks—temperance drinks, thank God—were supplied, and all sorts of wonderful things. "It is surprising what men require !" added Mr. James. (Laughter.) The British 'Tommy' can eat a meal at any Moment with- out warning, either before a meal or after one.. Only. let him see one and he can eat it! so the Y.M.C.A. counters are always busy." At the other end also was a quiet little chapel, .- so that:me:li could go' in and 'sit. there :it any time dining the day; it. waa always; open. Sometimes the Communion of the Lord's Supper was held; and very impressive it was. It was a great'thing wh n a man knew that the Y.M.C.A. was open there for him to go in and be quiet! Beyond the counter were some places where the Y.M.C.A. staff, slept at night. Not Only Refreshment Counter.

But it was not only the refreshment counter that they were after, it was much more than that. More than £l,OOO of notepaper had been provided and used by the men in writing home; and one of the things upon which the Y.M.C.A. was determined was that the Censor should be kept busy—becauSe the men would write home when the materials were there at hand.

Then their Y.IVI.C.A. was a great spiritual centre as well. There was a wonderful spirit amongst the men out in France, especially among those who had been in the trenches or were going into them; and there was ground for religious instruction which it would be quite imposAble to produce at any other time: Of

course he did not pretend that all the men were like that. But he had worked and moved about among great crowds of men who had known what it was to stand shoulder to shoulder, know- ing that any moment might be their last, and feeling that they were just staring the grim spectre of death in the face, side by side, and might be called—either or both of them— through the gates. There was something about that experience which simply brought men's hearts together in a way that no other ex- perience could produce, and a great many of them had discovered their way back to the faith they lad believed in years ago, and they know they were always safe in the eternal keeping. DREAMING ON THE THAMES..

Dreaming on the Thames in the lovely summer sunshine, Dreaming to the music of the emerald water'o flow, Where the willows whisper softly in the noon - shine And the swans come sailing softly to and fro.

Dreaming as the churches sweetly rise and slowly Slip behind the woodlands, fade and 'pass away, 'Past the rustic places once held strangely holy. Holy still to some of us floating'doWn to-day.

Dreaming 'mid the flowery lawns which coma down to the water Where the roses redden Find the overs rest; , Dreaming half forgetful of the strife anti slaughter Where fierce War is claiming England's first and best.

But the distant vision of the camp put yonder

Wakes me from my reverie: let me dream

again, Once again forgetful of the distant thunder, And the blood and tumult and the world of pain.

Once again the waters gurgle softly round me, Meadowsweet is waving, callow cygnets pass; War's remembered nightmares now no longer

wound me, Heaven seem, half reflected in the river's glass.

Might I sail for ever on thy gentle bosom, Beautiful old river, through these meadows green, Soft blue skies above me, all the flowers in blossom, All the pleasant landscape silent and serene.

"War's black night will vanish, strife and pain . be over," So thy waters whisper to my heart to-day 1 "I shall wait to welcome happy maid and lover When the strife is ended "—so thy waters saY•

"All my flowers shall blossom and my sylvan' # reaches Stretch out arms to welcome those who shall return " Beautiful and"gracious the old river's speech is "Over them out yonder tenderly I yearn.

"When the world grows wiser and all wars are ended, I shall gleam and sparkle, I shall sing and shine; Dear to Mother England are those who have defended Her eternal honour—hers, and yours, and mine.

"For their ' sons and daughters through long generations I shall wait to murmur in their loving ears Noble tales how England stood among tlie nations,

Made anew through sorrow, sanctified bY tears

" I, and you, and England, who have grown together"— So the waters whisper—" shall together staPd' One amid the sunshine, one in stormy weather", I, and you, and England, proudly hand In hand." , .

So I sail contented through the trees and rost, Where the owans slip softly down the silver

stream, Listening to the music till the journey closes In a golden gunsei—was it all a dream? ARTHUR BENNETT.

I_AYMME

Fewer Lamplighters.—With the approval of the Council, the General Purposes Committee have empowered the gas manager to discharge two lamplighters and to retain the two whom he considered would be the most useful.

Private W. Worrall.—Early this week the Cheshire casualty lists (wounded during April and May) included the name of Private W. Worrell, Rush Green, Lymm, of the 2nd Cheshires.

LYMM TERRITORIALS.

Writing a week ago to Miss Bessie Cross, Private Percy Clare (Mining Section) says :---,_ "This is the .first letter.you have had written 'in ink since I have been out. You see they have opened A reading and writing room, so it has made it a lot better for us when writing letters, and we can pass a few happy hours away when we come off duty. We came down from the trenches to-night and had our tea; and it is after tea that we can come here to pass the night away, indeed, we have a piano ,going all night. It is fine; it puts me in mind of the Y.M.C.A. in England, and we are not far from the trenches—well you can tell, for we walk up every day to our work. Sometimes at night we work eight hours and have twenty-four off. We go to work to-morrow night at five o'clock and come off at twelve; and then we go in the night after twelve, and so on. "Well, our company has started leave."

===== 0002601_19151002_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JAMES LEAH.

Old Volunteer Visits Home After Being Gassed.

Private James Leah, of the Transport Section ; sth Cheshire Territorials, arrived home at 3, The Rookery, on Saturday night.; and his leave ends on Monday afternoon, when he will return to the depot at Chester. It will be remembered that Private Leah was slightly gassed about the middle of July. His case affords a typical example of the terrors of gas. On Thursday afternoon "The Examiner " representative heard how he met his misfortune. "One or two of us were standing against the fire with the cook," said Private Leah, "and suddenly our eyes began to smart—although we could see no change at all in the atmosphere. Then it got in our noses, and a ' funny ' taste came in the mouth. In a day or two the others had to go to hospital, but came out shortly as they were not affected badly. It was about a week afterwards that the effects of it came on me in the same way as on them—a headache and then all my head began swelling, and I could hardly breathe. Now I have chest and bad in- ternal troubled. I reported to the doctor, and he sent me to a hospital not far away, where I spent the night. The next two days and three nights I was in another hospital farther away, and then moved to Le Treport until August Ist. Everywhere I met with the best attention. Next I came to the Ist London General Hospital for a fortnight, then to Epsom, and on Saturday I came home."

Asked what he thought was one of his most exciting experiences at the Front, Private Leah tiaid that on April 17th he was "taking the horses out of the range of the enemy's fire, when a shell came and he got blown off his feet. felt a bit shaken, that was all; it was the shock."

Being engaged on transport work, be has not seen a great deal of fighting; but all he saw appeared to be going in our favour. After the shelling of Ypres he saw a few women and rchildren lying in the streets who had been killed ootright by the shells.

In peace times the Private is a farm labourer for Mr. Harry Gibbon. He is married and has four e.hildren. In November, 1904, he joined the Volunteers, in.4hich, and the Territorials., he has remained since.

LYMNI RATES.

fdJillinlllllllllllllllllllllllll

===== 0002601_19151002_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
To tiic Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—At the present time, when economy is being urged upon all classes, it should not be out of place to draw attention to the state of affairs existing in local government generally. It behoves individual communities seriously to overhaul their expenditure and to consider if ways and means cannot be found to reduce, rather than increase, the already heavy taxes levied upon them. Particularly should this be done in districts which are not rapidly increasing their popula- tion, for in such areas any advance in taxation must ,merely ' add to the individual burden,: unless industrial expansion increases the 'rate- able value.

Lymm is a typical instance which may be taken as an illustration. During the past seven years the poputation has remained practically stationary, the very 'slight increase shown over that period being negligible, and the total in- crease of rateable value, if any, very slight. On the other hand, the position of rates and taxes is shown in the following table, the District Rate and Poor Rate being separated:—

District rate. 'Poor rate. s. d. s. d. 23 12 23 12 26 14 26 1 4 28 1 6 210 1 6 33 18

1909. 1910. 1911. 1912. 1913. 1914. 1915.

The increase shown in the District Rate in the past seven years equals therefore 44.44 per cent., and in the Poor Rate 47.21 per cent. It would have been interesting to note exactly in which departments the increases have taken place, but these figures the average ratepayer does not re- ceive on his District Rate demand note. The 'Poor Rate- demand note' ; however, does give such an analysis, which can be preserved as record. The rather weird..system, which seems peouliar. to Lymm, of retaining, the original de- mand note, when thO rate is paid, and,issuing only a receipt, is ncit conchicive to the keeping of a concise record. It should be'abolished; Fur- ther elaboration is not proposed, as the sole purpose of this very brief statement is to draw the attention of the general body of ratepayers to the constant increase year by year of the rates, and especially the heavy addition of sd. between 1914 and 1915 to the District Rate. If a similar, or even possibly a larger, advance is called for in the coming financial year, it will be a, serious matter for all concerned. The recent enormous increase of Imperial taxation, with the certainty of further imposts, will force many people seriously to economise. This has already shown itself amongst residents by a demand for smaller houses and a curtailment of travelling expenses. ,

For the shopkeeper, unable to transfer his business or to further reduce his expenses, while at the same time facing a reduced turnover, it will create a very grave position. It is, I think, a patriotic duty for all con- cerned to see that not one sovereign is spent during , the next twelve months or two years, except for the barest 'necessities, and I believe the present inflated rates offer a fair field for rigid economy.

PRO BONO PUBLIC°. Lymin, 29th Sept., 1915.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 2, 1915.

STOCKTON TIIL 1a F

Present At Parochial Committee.—At the meeting of the Parochial Committee, held in the Council Schools on Tuesday evening, Mr. F. Pearson presided, and there were qlso pre- sent: Messrs. J. Whitley, 11. J. Westbrook, W. Mclntyre; J. Hallows, A. Bottomley and C. W. Hallows, with the Acting Clerk (Mr. W. E. Hough), the Surveyor (Mr. A. Stanley Wright), and the clerk to the Parish Council (Mr. E. Bradshaw).

Concerning Victoria-square Signpost.—At a meeting of. the Parish Council on Tuesday even- ing, the Chairman (Mr. W. Mclntyre) reported that a County Council official had been down and had inspected the _signpost in Victoria- square. Although, ,so the official stated, the lettering on some of the plates was the large. :t which the Surveyor's departme.at had in sto.tk at the present time, he quite agreed that the signpost could be improved, and promised to bring the matter before his committee. With regard to danger posts, he was perfectly willing to suggest that one should .be erected at each end of West-avenue. Reilresentations would be made to the responsible committee, and he (the Chairman) had no doubt that the wishes of Stockton Heath would be met.

Volunteers' Long Route March.—Led by a drummer and a fife player from Orford Bar- racks, the members of the Stockton Heath detachment of the Knutsford Battalion of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment indulged in a ,ong route march on S!...nday. Nearly fifty n number, and in charge of-Platoon-Commander Z. Warburton, they fell in on Victoria-square at 9.15 in the morning and marched to Moore Station. There they entrained for Acton Bridge, From whence they marched through Crowton, Hatchmere and Delamere Forest to Moulds- worth, where lunch was served and where a halt of an hour and' a half's duration was called. Falling in once more,' the men -next reached Alvanley, where They were joined by the Frodshtim detachment, under Mr. W. N. Hutchings, and with whom they marched on to Frodsham. The Stockton Heath men then ,at down together at tea at, the Bear's Paw Hotel, and afterward entrained for Daresbury, from which place they continued their journey afoot, reaching home •at 8.45 p.m., after an exhilarating and enjoyable "field day."

ONCE OR TWICE A YEAR?

How Often Should Parish Meetings Be

Heki ?

A meeting of the Stockton Heath Parish Council was held under the chairmanship of Mr. W. Mclntyre on Tuesday evening, when the principal business for settlement was as to whether parish meetings should he held annually only, or every half-year, as hereto- fore.

The Clerk (Mr. E. Bradshaw) reported to the Council that he had submitted the question to the "Local Government Chronicle" ans.! the "Poor Law Officers' Journal." In the ques- tion which he had addressed to the mitors of those two papers he pointed out that at a parish meeting some time ago a resolution was passed that in future two parish meetings should Le held each year. At the last meeting of the Parish Council, however, it was reported that the attendance at the parish meeting wits very small, and one of the members considered that, in the circumstances, only one meeting a year should be held. The member further contende:l that the resolution passed at the Parish ine?t- ing that two meetings a year should be held was of no effect, and that if the second parish meeting was summoned by the Clerk, acting only on the resolution referred to, such meeting was illegal, and any expense incurred thereat was likely to be surcharged. To make his point clear, he said that if the Chairman was un- willing to summon the second meeting or two, members of the Council were unwilling to do so, then six ,ratepayers must give notice for a meeting to be summoned. Any meeting sum- moned in any other way would not have legal standing. _ _ _

===== 0002601_19151002_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
Expert Opinion.

To the Question raised in the letter, added Mr. Bradshaw, the "Local Government Chronicle had replied that the resolution of the parish meeting was directory only. It did not interfere with that section of ,the Local Government Act which provided that the date, time and place of a parish meeting might be fixed by the Parish Council. The Clerk of the Parish Council had no power to summon a parish meeting on his own responsibility. Notice of such a meeting should be signed by the Chairman of the Parish Council or the other conveners of the meeting. A notice not singe, as directed in the Act, would be irregular. The view taken by the "Poor Law Officers' Jour- nal" was similar, the editor adding that the resolution that two parish meetings a year be held was unnecessary, if not ultra vires, though it was not likely that the auditor would sur- charge any expenses incurred in summoning a meeting in compliance with the resolution.

Automatic Meetings A Waste Of. Time And Money.

Mr. A. Bottomley said it was clear from the answers—as he had contended—that the resolu- tion of the parish meeting had no standing at all. Two members of the Parish Council, acting in concert, could call as many parish meetings as they liked, and the Chairman of the Parish Council 'could call as many as he liked. Six ratepayers, acting together, also had power to call a parish meeting. In view of that, it was a waste of time and money 'to summon auto- matic half-yearly, meetings, at which scarcely anybody turned up. Knowing that any six per- sons in the parish could, if so desirous, summon a meeting, it was foolish of the Parish Council to take into consideration a resolution which was only directory and summon a meeting when there was nothing to do and nobody was interested in it.

Mr. H. J. Westbrook said he thought they would be quite right in intimating to the next parish meeting their opinion that they did not consider mom than one parish meeting per annum really necessary— Aethe same time, if the parish Meeting passed a resolution in favour of the holding of two meetings yearly, lie would hesitate very much before. he went against their wishes.

Parishioners To Have Their Say.

It was decided to call the next parish meet- ing for October 19th, and thereat to introduce the views of the council on the question of one or two meetings,per annum.

The Clerk -Said he would mention in the notice calling the meeting that a proposal to discontinue the half-yearly meetings would be introduced.

Mr. 11. J. Westbrook : Perhaps that will bring them up! (Laughter.) Mr. A. Bottomley : Can't we provide refresh- ments. We sliould be sure to have a good meet- ing if we could. (Mere laughtei.) Eventually Mr. A. BottornleY gave notice of his intention to propose at the next parish meeting that the half-yearly meetings hitherto held be discontinued.

THE SEMI-D lIIK STUEETS.

Resident Craves Boon 01 More

COUNCIL POWERLESS IN MATTER.

The Parochial Committee, at their meeting' on Tuesday evening, spent some little time dis- cussing the lighting restrictions at present in force, in the Stockton Heath district, the matter arising out of a letter written to the Clerk by Mr. W. Harvey Evans, of 130, London-road, Stockton Heath. The writer referred to what he called "the dangerous state of the road from •Victoria-square to London Bridge after dark." There was, he added, only one light on the east side, and that was partly shaded. On the west side there were two lamps up to Orchard-street, but neither was lighted at night, and it was a difficult matter indeed to reach home safely. If the two lamps were lighted. even on the reduced scale of the lamp on the I the opposite side of the road, he was sure Ile • inhabitants of the neighbourhood would be very grateful. Winter was apprbaching, when the conditions would be still worse.

..The chairman (Mr. :F. Pearson). said that the question of the provision of an extra lamp ivas a matter for the Lighting Committee. Mr. J. Hallows said that as a member of the Lighting Committee he, would prefer that the matter was considered .by the full_ Parochial Committee, for, in his opinitm, if'an additional light was provided' in London-road they would. soon • find that they had .to consider the claims

of the residents in Grappenhall-road and other roads for more lamps. Appeals From All Parts Of Parish. Mr. J. Whitley said that he had been approached by people from all parts of the parish and asked whether he could not do some- thing in the way of getting more of the street lamps lighted. He said he would go into the matter, and from a conversation he had had with Mr. Haddock (the gas engineer to the Warrington Corporation) and other authorities, he gathered that they must adhere to the regu- lations now in force, whiph provided that there should only be one lighted lamp where pre- viously there were four. Owing to the numer- ous air raids which had taken place in other ' parts of the country at night-time, it was thought better that conditions should remain as at present. • Mr. A. Bottomley : But in this case there are exceptional circumstances. If we are of opinion that a light should be ther and we ask the Warrington Corporation to provide one, and they refuse, then, if any accident should occur in the future the responsibility will be on the Corporation's shoulders and not on ours. Mr. J. Whitley: Any question of additional lighting would have to come before the military authorities. Mr. A. Bottomley : Then let the military authorities be responsible for any damage that may occur. Mr. J. Whitley: I question whether they would allow it. The Chairman: We cannot consider the whole question of the lighting of the parish at the present moment. To-night it is simply a ques- tion of a request made by one parishioner. Why The Lights Are Few And Low. Mr. H. J. Westbrook asked, whether it was not a fact that the present diminution in light- ing had not been brought about by the Parochial Committee, but by the military authorities, or the police. The Chairman: That is so, precisely. Mr. H. J. Westbrook: That being so, I don't think we can go against the regulations. Our reply to Mr. Evans should be that we sym- pathise with him very much, but can do noth- ing for him as we are under orders from a higher authority. Mr. A. Bottomley : If the Members of the Lighting Committee are satisfied that this is a particularly dangerous spot, and that it ;s advisable to have a light there, I think it is our duty, so that no accident shall happen owing to that deficiency, to point the fact out to the powers that be and ask them, in the cir- cumstances, to allow a light to be provided. It is all very well to carry out regulations, but if they are going to be the cause of accidents it is for us, as a local authority, to mention our fears to those who have the power, but who do not come into contact with the people or with the conditions in the district as we do, and consequently do not appreciate the position as we do.

Would If They Could, But -! The Chairman pointed out that the point at istsue reopened the question of every street in the place. Personally he wished they could see their way to oblige Mr. Evans, but he was afraid they could not. Besides the road in which Mr. Evans lived was one of the best in the parish, and was by no means a dangerous one.

Mr. H. J. Westbrook proposed that a letter be swritten Mr. Evans informing him that whilst the Committee sympathised with him in the matter they could do nothing as the diminution in the public lighting had been brought about by order of the military authori- ties.

Mr. J. nallows seconded, and it was carried.

LATE MRS.. ALBERT BROADY.

In the presence of a large number of mourners and sympathisers the mortal remains of the late Mrs. Albert Broady, of Wilderspool, were laid to rest in St. Thomas' Churchyard on Sun- day afternoon. The deceased lady, who was 48 years of age, had been in indifferent health for many months, though latterly she had seemed to improve considerably, and her death occurred with painful suddenness at about midnight on Thursday of last week. Prior to the interment a deeply impressive service was conducted in St. Thomas' Church by the Vicar (the Rev. Jas. Collins), the lesson being read by Mr. F: Pearson, and the choir singing the well-known hymn, "On the resurrec- tion morning." Mr. F. Rose officiated at the organ. The chief . mourners were: Messrs. Albert Broady (husband), John Twiss, Edwin Twiss, Frederick Twiss, Henry Twiss. and Herbert Twiss (brothers), James Carter, Henry Broady, Thomas Broady and Frederick Broady (brothers-in-law), Harry Broady, jun. (nephew), Thomas. Melbourne, John Melbourne, James Melbourne and William Melbourne (cousins), William Mclntyre, John H. Mclntyre, Fred Timperley, William Timperley, Frederick Pear- son and R. N. Foster. Among others present in the church and at the graveside were Messrs. John Fletcher, Stan- ley Carter, T. Jackson Lee, 11. H. Ferguson, ' John Hallows, James Richardson, Fred Berry, Thos. Jackson, R. H. Smith, C. 11. Hughes, F. Haslam, and C. 11. Hatton. Messrs. Frank Mclntyre and Wm. S. Mclntyre (nephews), JOB. Blinston and John Blinston acted as bearers. Many beautiful floral tributes covered the grave, the senders being: "Albert," "Jack. Sarah and family," "Edwin, Fred, Jennie and Bab,"" Harry and Agnes," "Herbert and Lizzie," "James and Lily," "Harry, Annie and family," "Will, Polly and family," f` Tom and Lucy," "Fred and Annie," "Auntie and cousins at the Foundry," "Uncle Alfred, Fred and "Tom and Bell," "Jim and Clara," Pearl and Harry," "Billy and Edith," "Madge and Charlie," "Annie and Mabel," "Joyce and Mary," Alfred and Norman," Mr. and Mrs. Woodcock, Mr. and' Mrs. Timperley. • PADGATE.

Social Evening and Presentation.—The open- ing social of the Wesley Guild for the winter session was held on Monday evening, presided over by the Rev. A: J.4Morton. Speaking for a few Moments on the work of the Church, he wished each member to have an increasing sense of individual responsibility during the coming winter. We do not want any slackers in Christian, work, he said, but we want real good hearty services, both morning and evening. A very interesting event was the presentation of a gold wrAtlet watch to Miss E. Bennett in recognition of the valuable services she has rendered to the Guild for many years as general secretary, a position which she has been reluctantly compelled to relinquish. Mr. Morton, who made the presentation, said in all his experience he had never come across a Guild that was blessed with such an energetic secretary. In reply, Miss Bennett said she was overcome with the kindness displayed by the members, and she should always have many happy memories of the good times she had had dur- ing the period she had acted as secretary. The soloists were Miss C. Hankey, who sang "Sunshine and Rain" and 'Angus Macdonald," and Mr. J. Angus, who contributed "The Skipper" and "Queen of Angels." Miss Hankey and Miss Duckworth were the accom- panist. Refreshments were provided, and gam" were indulged in during the evening.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
THRIFT Arm TAXATION.

Mr. Wilfrid Rowland on Economy by Compulsion.

On Sunday at the Bewsey Adult School, Mr. Wilfrid Rowland, bon-. secretary of the National Thrift Campaign, gave a lecturette on Thrift and Taxation.

lie said that thrift was not a class virtue. Bravery belonged to the peer and to the poor' man—modesty might belong as much to the university professor as to the peasant maiden. Thrift is a virtue that may characterise rich and poor alike. Thrift is not primarily money- saving. A poor man might spend his whole income mid: yet be thrifty if his expenditure was wise. The word economy meant originally household, management. The thrifty man was the man of imagination —the man who could see things in proportion and live in: thought beyond the coming week. The need for careful expenditure was not at all widely appreciated, even under the present national conditions, and people were far more ready to shrug :their shoulders than to put their shoulders to. the wheel in the veryouphill task of education. •

On a recent occasion a local employer had been talking to his workpeople about the bad times that are bound!to follow the war, and had urged them to make all the money they could and to save what was .possible. When he had finished a girl laughing & brushed everything on one side with a remark that showed that she meant o enjoy herself to the full, and the future coulrl "go hang." Such light-mindedness might spell disaster.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
Coming Burdens.

Fresh taxation was just about to be imposed— neither too soon nor too heavily—for the nation was now better able to- bear heavy taxation than it would be during the depression which would follow the war. Petople were going to be hit hard, and some sections of the population very hard—notably the men with incomes from. £l7O to MOO. These people would not only feel the burden of indirect taxes on food, in COITLIMbiI with the lower-paid* wage-earners, but they would have to face a heavy income tax._ _ _

The rate of increase inincome tax would work out very unevenly. The man with £2OO would bear an increase of 180 per cent., while the man with ,t3OO would bear an increase of 80 per cent., and the man with .Zl,OOO would bear an increase of 40 per cent. Those with £5,006 and more per annum would find their percentage of increase to be less. From sheer necessity the poorest class of income tax payers had • already begun seriously to economise, and one effect of the present Budget would be to compel' people to save fur- ther in certain directions, The stiff tariff on imported luxuries, like motor cars and watches, was partly designed to check expenditure on un- necessary things, and to retrace purchases from. abroad. It might even have been more useful if the British Government had prohibited the purchase of luxuries (except under special licence), as France and Russia had done on cer- tain articles.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
Economy An Art.

It was a time not so much to preach to one another about the virtue of thrift in general, as to exchange experiences as. to how to econo- mise. After all, economy is an art, which has to be acquired by toil: It does not came by the light of instinct. The losses of loved ones had made people through a common suffering realise the horror of war. There might be still more bitter experi- ences to come when Poverty would walk hand in hand with Death. When civilisation emerged from the present struggle her task would be to find some new and better way of settling inter- national differences. It would be a task for a super statesman. •

A NEV KIND. OF "FIRST-AID."

Novelty For Lovers Of Dumb Animals.

Quite an interesting novelty has made its appearance,_ being a " first-aid " ointment devised especially for the cuts, sprains and skin diseases of our dumb friends. It is really Zam-Buk with all its well-known healing properties in a different or stronger form adapted for horses, dogs, sheep, cattle and pets. Veterinary Zam-Buk, a it is called, is reddish in colour and is sold in red packets as distin- guished from the green packets of the ordinary Zam-Buk. It is a little cheaper than thd house- hold preparation, in that it is not affected by the new war tax on patent medicines. It is really a fine healing, soothing and antiseptic preparation which has, evoked a chorus of un- stinted praise from farmers, stock-keepers, fanciers and lovers of pets. General Botha's famous charger was cured by ' this Zam-Buk of some obstinate sores which veterinary surgeons had treated for some time in vain. In the farmyard, stable, kennel and poultry-run, Zani-Buk provides for the first time an ideal and handy "Vet."

SIR ARTHUR CROSFIELD.

Sir Arthur Crosfield, Bart., who will be accompanied by Lady Crosfield, will pay a visit to Warrington next week to fulfil several important engagements. On Monday after- noon Mrs. John Crosfield will unveil at the Town Hall the bust of her late husband, which she and the family have presented to the town. Sir Arthur will formally hand over

the gift, and the Mayor will respond on behalf of the borough. Sir Arthur and Lady Crosfield will also attend several ward tea meetings held by the women workers at the war sewing elastics.

PEARSON AND KNOWLES GLOOMY:

The directors of the Pearson and Knowles 'Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., have had another exceptionally good year, as will be found from - the annual report, which we publish in an- other column: Dividends of eight per cent.' and five per cent. in the two preceding years: are followed this year by a dividend of 7t, per cent., besides large assignments to the regerve and other funds. The peculiar char-- acteristic of the reports that periodically come from this great firm is the sombre view the directors always take of the future, though the present be ever so bright. With a pro- fit this year of £122,000, against £98,000 last year and £llB,OOO the year before (the eight per cent. year), they still look forward and say:

The present position of the iron and coal trades, in common with the general business of the country, is precarious, and surrounded with difficulty, and the future it is impossible to foresee.

PINK FORM VOLUNTEERS.

At the Police Buildings Recruiting Office clerical work in connection with the National Registration Pink Forms is now in progress. Unfortunately :that progress is slow. Recently appeals were issued for volunteers to help in the work; any spare hour which a lady, gentle- man, boy or girl might have between 9.30 a.m. and 9.30 p.m. could be spent most patriotically in the special room allotted for the work. Since the issue of that appeal three weeks have now passed, and it has to be admitted that the result is a disappointing one. At first there was a good response, the volunteers including (besides ladies and gentlemen) schoolboys on holidays, and even wounded members of the 4th South Lancashire Regi- ment then having quitted hospital to pay brief visits home! To all these who gave their ser- vices when they could Colonel Mocatta is deepli grateful.

Recently, however, these volunteers have, numerically, dwindled so low that it is neces- sary for public attention to be drawn to the fact. The work is of vital importance, and Expedition should be the word of the moment. Should the present rate of progress continue the completion of the register will be greatly delayed, and considerable harm may ensue. The only qualification necessary for a volun- teer is good hand writing; and *here must be no small number of ladies and gentlemen— especially ladies—who could help. Warring- ton has responded so excellently to other war appeals—many less urgent than the present one—that•ohe-feels -ewe this request will not have to be repeated.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
WORKING MEN'S CONCERT.

To-bight the Parr Hall will be the scene of one of those concerts which have for a long series of years opened the local concert season and provided the Infirmary with a constant and substantial source of income. In the hands of the Working Men's Infirmary Com- mittee,. the entertainment has always been a success in every way, and it is to be hoped that this year, although conditions are so abnormal, the financial result will be satisfac- tory. Some idea of the venerable character of this committee for the propagation of good deeds will ,be found in the fact that this will be the twenty-seventh occasion upon which Mr. T. J. Down's band has appeared at this particular concert. The other artistes are taken from amongst the town's best vocalists, and Mr. Payton will be accompanist..

===== 0002601_19151002_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
BRAVO, THE BASSES!

There was a peculiar little sentence in the annual report of the Musical -Society. The chorus this year numbered 159, against 164 last year, "so that," says the hon. secretary, "with the exception of the basses—of whom a considerable number had entered the ArMy —the strength of the chorus was practically the same as in the previous year." As a matter of fact, while the basses have de- creased from 38 to 27, the tenors have in- creased from 20 to 23. What is the reason to account for the fact to which -the hon. secre- tary draws cinch pointed attention? Are our gruff-voiced ones more bellicose by nature than those of milder tmie ? It is so on the stage— your dark bass was ever your hero-villain, and your fair, light-hearted tenor has the pre-emption upon the role of Romeo and his descendants. Or do the tenor voices last longer than the heavier ones and so carry their class on beyond the military age? Or —but why speculate further? The fact re- mains that a "considerable number" of the basses of the Musical Society have entered the Army. We hope they will join in a chorus that will make the Kaiser weep.
