PEARSON AND KNOWLES.

Reserves To Meet Difficulties Of The Future.

GERMAN COMPETITION.

Sir J. S. Harmood-Banner, M.P., chairman of the Board of Directors, presided at the forty- second annual meeting of the shareholders of the Pearson and ,Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., held at Warrington, on Wednesday. The attendance included the following directors:— Messrs. Johri J. Bleckly (deputy chairman), W. Peter Rylands, Herbert S. Bleckly, John Bleckly, jun., and Major H. Harmood-Banner, with the SecrOary (Mr. A. Brotherton).

POINTS FROM THE ANNUAL REPORT.

The report, which appeared fully in last week's "Examiner," contained the following points:—

The profits for the year ending 30th June, 1915, were. £122,359 ss. 2d.; the balance brought forward from last year 01,842 Is. 5d.; together £144,201 Gs. 7d. Dividend at the rate of G per cent. per annum on the first and second preference shares. Dividend on the ordinary Shares of 71 per cent, for the year, malting with the interim dividend a: total distribution of £62,386 9s. 2d. Recommended that £47,500 be added to the i'eserve fund, raising it to f 450,000; that the sum of £8,254 Is. 5d., which represents the ex- penditure on capital account for the year, be written off, leaving a balance of £26,060 Us. to be carried forward.

SIR J. S. HARMOO3 BANNER'S CONGRATULATIONS.

The Chairman, moving the adoption of the report and accounts, said when they met last year they had had only a short experience of the war, and it was .difficult to appreciate the position in all its hearings. They were led to believe that the war might mean a. possibility of capturing Germany's foreign trade, and that it would be possible to carry on business as usual.. Twelve Months had passed, and put an end to any such suggestions, and all of us feel that while it was important, if possible, to keep up the export trade so as to earn money to pay for imports, yet the most important matter is the energy and determination with which everybody worked to further the success- ful issue of the war. . (Applause.) He and his colleagues, the managers and the employes, had bent all their energies in this direction. First of all, from their works over 3,100 men had ioined his Majesty's Forces. We had had heavy losses to deplore, but also gallant deeds to record; and one man at Moss Hall, named Kenealy, won a Victoria Cross. (Applause.)

They would probably ask why the directors starved the shareholders by declaring no interim dividend to the ordinary shareholders in March last, and increased the transfer to reserve by so, large a sum as £47,500, the largest transfer ever made in the history of the company. As regarded the first question, trading to the 31st December was most unsatisfactory.

They were luckier than most manufacturers in not having to shut down the works, and began at once to feel the benefit of their investment lin the Partington. Steel and Iron Company, Limited, from whom- they were able to obtain stocks to fill up the void produced by the war with Germany, and to continue the business:

" No doubt," said the Chairman, "you will have all excused the omission when you have received the satisfactory dividend of per cent. which we are now paying out of the full twelve months' trading." (Hear, hear.) "Why have we transferred so large a sum as £47,500 to nerve "instead of increasing the dividend? Several reasons have induced us to adopt this figure; principally, we have to face the great uncertainty which will arise about business at the close of the war, and we con- sider that we ought to build up a strong reserve to face the position at .that time in case of difficulty. - He thought he might reasonably congratulate the shareholders on the splendid property they possessed in their holding of the ordinary shares of the Partington Steel and Iron Co., Ltd. That company was projected and pro- moted by the board with the sole object of safeguarding the supplies of raw material for the parent company against the dangerous un- certainty inseparable from deperfdence on Ger- man importations, and it was a matter of satis- faction to the directors that this object had been amply achieved, especially in this particular crisis. (Applause.) In the coal trade, production had decreased over ten per cent., whilst wages and the cost of material was largely increased, with the result that profits were less by one-half of the profits of 1913, and £lO,OOO less than the profits of last year.

There- ought to be no jealousy (though it was too often- encouraged) between employers and employed, and he could only hope that the spirit of friendliness which had distinguished this district might continue after the war in deal- ing with wages. He would like to appeal to the men to improve the statistics of absenteeism, which comes to a proportion of 16 per cent. Some men work as much as seven days per week, whiLst others were only doing their three of -four days. Coal was an essential element of the war, both physically and #nancially, and required the. extreme effort of every citizen at these times of stress who was eager to do his best for his country. "I am sure," said the Chairman, "you will be the last to complain of the tax on war profits, whether it be, as at present, 50 per cent., or whether it has to be increased to 100 per cent. Sacrifice is demanded all round, and thousands who are shedding their , blood are makings greater sacrifices than we are; but barring any unforscen accidents, I should quite hope that we might reach the basis on which we are this year paying income-tax, namely, the basis of 1913, when we distributed a divi- dend of eight per cent. I think we should hardly give you as much, because of the neces- sity of improving, our reserves, and what pro- portion of the eight per cent. you would have to spend as actual cash I cannot tell, as 'income tax may absorb a large proportion of the profits available for dividend. •

"All we claim as regards taxation is that it should be fair and just all round. (Hear, hear.)

COMING DOUBLE DEFEAT FOR GERMANY.

Mr. John J. Bleckly, seconding, said he fully corroborated what the Chairman had said as to the difficulties they had had to contend against throughout the past year. Their. finance was disorganised by the moratorium. Though its imposition relieved the enormous strain upon the .banks, and obviated a panic, its operation was exceedingly inconvenient in ordinary busi- ness at the time. In the manufacturing depart- ment they wore all but stopped by a sudden and complete collapse of all their ordinary sources of supply of raw material, which they had hurriedly to obtain in altogether new directions. Workmen in large numbers" patriotically answered their, country's call for recruits, and by so doing rapidly depleted their mills and workshops, so that it was with great difficulty that they kept the works on at 75 per cent. pro- duction, and it was fortunate that they were able to do this much. Altogether the year's ex- perience had severely tested their organisation , and resources, and :but for the loyalty and energy of their staff the results, of the year's working might have produced something very different.

Last year he ventured to express the opinion that the result of the war must be to cripple, if not to destroy, Germany's preponderance in the iron and steel trades, to be replaced by a large expansion of British works, and he .was more and more convinced of the correctness of this view. When the " Tommies " and the Territorials had done their duty the captains of industry must /take their position. But in order to take lull advantage of the opportunity when • it came, they must meantime huSbanct their resources and strengthen their financial Teerves. It was to be ready for such a future develop-

===== 0002601_19151002_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. RYLANDS ON THE DIVISION AND

USES OF PROFITS.

The Chairman moved that the usual dividend at the rate of 6 per cent. per annum on the first and second preference shares for the half-year ending 30th June last, and a dividend on the ordinary shares of 7i per cent. for the year to the same date—all less tax—be paid forthwith. Mr. W. Peter Rylands," in seconding, said all reflecting and patriotic men must feel it to be improper that private individuals should derive undue personal advantage out of the war. Private individuals were, of course, entitled to reward on the principle that the labourer is worthy of his hire, and in this unregenerate world, reasonable reward was still a stimulus to endeavour. In considering the ,question of war profits, however,oin its broadest aspect, as affecting the great concerns who have to fight the commercial battles of the country in the markets of the world, such as the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co. and ll:viands Brothers, it must not be forgotten hat in their case, at all events. financial strength in times of peace was analogous to guns and ample munitions for our Army in the present terrible conflict. (Hear, hear.)

We may rest assured, continued Mr. Ryland, that when peace returns Germany will seek once more, with. the assistance of all the resources of the State, such as may remain, to recover her former commanding position in the world's iron and steel trade, and to find an outlet for her enormous capacity of production. I speak feel- ingly upon this matter, because in the wire trade, for which I am more directly responsible to our shareholders, the competition ot Ger- many in the past has been one of extreme severity, and we may be sure that in the future her efforts to recover lost ground will be renewed with every adventitous assistance that German commercial ingenuity can devise. When that time comes it will fall to the lot of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron C. the Partington Steel and Iron Co., and Rylands Brothers to take a strenuous part in fighting our country's. commercial battles, but without any assistance from the general taxpayers of the country, and any extra profit that can be made in this time of war should, it appear. 4 to me, properly be devoted to assisting us in that con- flict (Hear, hear.).' The country- vi ill share in our success if we succeed, and will suffer in our failure should we fail. If, therefore, this ques- tion of war profits had to be considered only in connection with such firms as those whose fortunes we are discussing to-day, it would seem to be an actual advantage to the State to leave war profits untouched, provided they were not distributed among the 'shareholders. From the point of view of the Government, however, the question is very complex, and there are undoubtedly vast numbers of instan- ces where war profits, if untouched, would acerue to the personal advantage of individuals, and as it is impossible to make fish of one and fowl of another, we mnst not criticise the pro- posals of the Government to tax, and to tax heavily, war profits, by whomsoever secured. At all events, a substantial proportion of such profits may be retained, and if, we are fortunate in securing any extra profits during the year in

front of us, I hope the shareholders will agree with me that but a small proportion should be distributed, and that they will support a policy of appropriating as much as possible to strength- ening the financial position of the company on the lines recommended by the directors in their report, to the shareholders to-day. (Applause.) The resolution was unanimously adopted.

HARVEST FESTIVALS.

Continued from Page 7.

COLBORNE-STREET BAPTIST CHURCH.

Veteran Wycliffe Pastor Occupies

Successful harvest thanksgiving services were held in connection with the Golborne-street Baptist Church on Sunday, when the special preacher in the morning and evening was the Rev. John Yonge, formerly minister at the Wycliffe Congregational Church, and now living in semi-retirement at Stockton Heath. The veteran preacher's two appropriate discourses were greatly enjoyed by large congregations, and altogether the services' were of a specially hearty and apposite character. The evening anthems were "0 Lovely Peace" and "The Lord is My Shepherd." Throughout the day the choir, under the able leadership of Mr. Her- bert Barnes, acquitted themselves with great credit. The church organist, Mr. Sam Rickers, presided at the organ and rendered suitable vol- untaries. There was general agreement that the tone of the services had 'never been higher. Both the preached word and the praise were in harmony with the present, times. In the afternoon the Rev. H. T. Dixon, of Penketh, gave a very instructive address on "The Wonders of the World." In his opening remarks he mentioned among the wonders the flowers, fruit, corn, sea, mountains, &e., and argued that the day of miracles was still with us. ,He said the parable of the feeding of the five thousand was evert being repeated, for the seed sown in the springtime was greatly in- creased before the harvest, and the juice of the grape was ever a repetition of the Master's turning the water into wine. Turning to the farmer, the speaker said he did his part—the sowing, the weeding, and the harvesting—and thus co-operated with God, Who gave the in- crease. In like manner they were all called upon to work with the Divine, use this wonder- fill, beautiful world to prepare them for that which was more wonderful still. During the service Miss Kershaw sang "Nearer, my God, to Thee," and the choir, under the leadership of Mr. Barnes, rendered the anthems "Every ono that thirsteth," and "He shall feed His flock."

The results were about equal to those of last year. On Monday a partial sale of the fruit was held, but some of the best fruit was sent to the Whitecross Hospital for the wounded soldiers. Other fruit and bread were sent to the sick and needy of the congregation.

ST. JAMES' CHURCH, LATCHFORD.

Fokmer Curate Re-occupies Old Pulpit.

A special and successful service in celebra- tion of the safe ingathering of the harvest, was held at St. James' Church, Latchford, on Thurs- day evening, there being a goodly-sized congre- gation. The church was tastefully decorated with seasonable produce. Added interest at- tached to the service from the fact that the preacher was the Rev. E. M. Swift, a former curate of the parish—and consequently very well-known to many of the congregation—and now curate of St. Paul's Church, Crewe.

The service was conducted by the Vicar (the Rev. 11. B. Firth), who was assisted by the Rev. C. F. Waller (curate). The lessons were read by Mr. W. H. • Stansfield. From the musical point 'of view the service was most at- tractive. in character, the choir rendering the anthem" Thou Visitest the Earth," by T. Mee Pattison, as well as leading the singing of a number of popular harvest hymns- Mr.. E. Davies officiated at the organ. _

The festival will be continued on Sunday, when the preacher at Matins will be the Rev. G. D. Wray (vicar of Timperley), whilst at evensong the pulpit will be occupied by the Vicar.

THE LATE FATHER SWARBRECK.

This he resigned about thirteen years ago and came to St. Alban's Church, Warrington, where for a few years he remained; afterwards taking charge of St. Benedict's. Since then he had been largely instrumental in improving the schools; and it is only two months sine the imposing new St. Benedict's Church, ereceted in Orford-lane at great cost during his labours, was opened amid great ceremony by his Eminence Cardinal Bourne, of Westminster. Nine sisters of Father Swarbreck are nuns. In the, ministry of St. Benedict's the late Father's colleague has been Father G. C. Jack- son, who was to have transferred to St. Alban's in a week's time.

FATHER N. V. WILSON.

Leaving Warrington Alter. Twenty

Years' One-rous Service.

Somewhat unexpectedly the news has been broken that the Rev. Father Neville Vincent Wilson, 0.5.8., is in a week's time to resign the rectorship of St. Mary's, Buttermarket-street, ,and , leave Warrington. lie will proceed to another and a less onerous appointment at Brownedge, near Preston.

Not only for local Catholicism, but also for Warrington as a whole the change entails a heavy loss: for during the twenty years of his residence here, Father Wilson has, in a quiet and faithful way, contributed much to the public welfare, particularly in matters educa- tional.

Ampleforth, Hereford and Liverpool.

, Both his parents' • were convert,s to the Catholic Faith. His father was a clergyman of the Church of England, but about the time of the Tractarian Movement gave up his living and became a Catholic. Born at Fordham, Cambridgeshire, in 1852, Father Wilson, at the age of twelve, went to Ampleforth College, near 'York ;, and after completing his studies entered in 1871 tho Benedictine Novitiate at St. Michael's Monastery, pear Hereford. Here having remained for the usual four years of discipline and study, he returned to his Monastery at Ampleforth, and was employed on the teaching staff for about ten years—during three of which he was also Prefect of Discipline over the College boys. In 1884 he was sent to St. Peter's, Seel-street, Liverppol; whence loter he was removed, and for about twelve yenrs worked amongst the large industrial popula- tions of Durham and West Cumberland.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
Achievements At St. Mary's.

It was in 1896 that Father Wilson was placed in charge of the Mission of• St. Mary's, the largest parish in Warrington; and during the ensuing years his church has been able to boast many new and fine achievements. When hav- ing been here only two years he established the Convent and introduced the Nuns of the Holy Cross and Passion. The labours of himself and his clergy have been greatly aided .by the Sisters, by their admirable work in the schools and parish. In 1907, at a cost of several hun- dred pounds, the beautiful tower was com- pleted and its peal of bells installed; whilst quite recently the new .Parish Hall and club- rooms have been erected opposite the church at a cost of about £2,600..

• -A -careful preacher, an; assiduous worker, and a tactful and far-seeing adviser, Warring- tonians of many shades and class,es have found in Father Wilson a real friend. Guardian and Educationist.

In about 1598 the Rector of St. Mary's became a member of the Board of Guardians, and con- tinued to hold office until 1904. Then he was compelled to relinquish the post because in the meantime—twelve months previously—there had been instituted the Education Committee, and he had been elected theron; and the dual duties, in-addition to his parish work, he found too much for him.. - With the Education Conintiti,e.o Father Wilson 'has 'since continued, serving right from the commencement upon both the Attendance aid School Management !-tub-corninitte:. Alth14;11 not figuring prominently in' public debate, he has-been one of the most zealous 'attenders and conscientious workers in connection with the committee.

In 1900, when his Alma Mater was created an Abbey by the holy See, Father Wilson was elected by his brethren a member of the Con- ventional Council, whose function it is to advise and assist the Abbot; this office he has held since. Amongst a number of unofficial positions he is a trustee of the Thomas Morris Charity.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
FATHER THOMAS A. HIND.

New Rector Of St. Mary's A Native Of Warrington.

In Father Wilson's place will come Father Thomas Austin Hind—at present an assistant priest at St. Peter's, Seel-street, Liverpool. He is a son of the late Mr. Roger Hind, who lived at 1, Manchester-road, and owned the world- famed Kitchen's weighing machines. Father George Ephege Hind, of Merthyr Tydvil, is his brother.

FATHER BASIL PRIMA VESI ALSO LEAVING.

Thirteen-Year Connection With Town Severed Next Week.

Father Basil Primavesi is also nearing the eve of separation from St. Mary's; indeed, next Thursday he leaves the church which has known him so long—and the Catholic community for whom he has Striven so hard and among whom he has been so deservedly popular—for Liver- pool, where, at St. Anne's Church, Overbury, Edgehill; ho will officiate as assistant priest. It is thirteen years ago since he came. to Warring- ton as assistant priest, that being his first ecdo- sinstical 'appointment.

Father Bas- Primavesi is a native of Swansea,

During the week ended September 25th. theta were 42 births in Warrington against 53 in the corresponding week last year; and there were 23 deaths against 17 last year. No fewer than five of the deaths were from pneumonia, five were from consumption in various forms, three from heart disease, and cne each from diarrhcea and scarlet fever. There were 18 fresh cases of the last named disease.

Strengthening the Temperance Cause.

A meting of tie Executive Committee of the citizens' Temperance • League was held on Wednesday, Mr. T. Dale (vice-president), being in the chair. The open-air pledge signing cam- paign was reviewed, and very satisfactory reports by members of the committee were given, the Secretary reporting that pielges had been signed at every meeting. The executive were pleased to receive the report, and after deciding that the various societies, speakers and presidents be thanked for their services, ex- pressed the hope that the meetings would be productive of much good, and thus strengthen

The Pudding Lady.

"The Pudding Lady is—cominj!" That is the mysterious announcement of the Thrift Campaign Committee. Of course, the initiated will know that "The Pudding Lady "is none other than Miss Petty, who originally worked in the St. Pancras district of London, going in and out of poor honies and helping house- wives to cook with whatever utensils happened to be at hand. If there was no rolling pin, a bottle would be promptly requisitioned. Miss Petty is a unique. combination of scientific cook, social reformer, professional lecturer and, above all, bright and sympathetic woman. She is evidently -just the one to give advice on war- time cookery, which is an art every woman ought to learn ;ander the discipline of the Coalition Budget.

The Father has, thanks to his linguistic4tecomplish- ments, been able to be of considerable service to the Belgian refugees who have been quartered in the town, and also to the body responsible for their entertainment. He will leave many friends and admirers behind him when he migrates to Liverpool, • for his innate courtesy and great kindliness of heart have endeared him to all classes, both within and Without the church to which he ministers.

Run Over By Motor.

Shortly before four o'cloCk on Saturday after- noon, Frederick James, of 14, Park-road, Widnes, was cycling along Liverpool-road, War- rington, in the direction of Warrington. Whoa opposite Cannell-street he overtook two funeral hearses; behind him was a motor car driven by Mr. James Brooks, of 36, New-street, Seacombe. Just as the cyclist passed he appeared to get his wheels in the car-lines and fell over on his right side. The motor went over his body, but at once stopped and conveyed him to the Infirm- ary—where the house surgeoii found him un- conscious and in a serious condition.

On Thursday he had progressed suffcientlY to return home.

Funeral of Old Warrington Tradesman

The death took place, at his residence-1-5. Chatham-road, Rock Ferry—on Friday of last week, of Mr. Thos. Gilbert Jolley, who was a member of a well-known Warrington family, and some thirty years ago carried on busizess as a butcher in Bridge-street. The deceased gentleman was 61 years a age, and he was latterly engaged in cattle dealing, being par- ticularly wll-known in the Cheshire live stock markets. Born at the old Seven Stars Hotel. Bridge-street, Mr. T. G. Jolley was the son of the late Mr. William Jolley, and was a brother of the late Airs. Francomb, a former Mayoress of Warrington. The funeral took place at the Warrington Cemetery at noon on Monday, the Rev. J. H. G. Hollingworth officiating.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
WATCH THE TRAINS.

Numerous Alterations For The Present Month.

SLOWER EXPRESSES.

Yesterday war conditions and the change of the season caused a considerable re-arrange- ment of the railway time tables. At the end of every summer season there is a material reduction in the nuraber of trains, especially to pleasure resorts. In the case of some of the trains that are retained the times are somewhat altered. Some of the London and North-Western Railway passenger expresses to Liverpool, Man chester and Scotland (including Warrington) leave Euston five or more minutes earlier than hitherto, and are due at their destinations either at the same time as before, or, in a few cases, five or more minutes later. This apparent "slowing down" of the ex- press services is merely a recognition of the fact that under present conditions the actual running times of these trains do not coincide with the time-table hours. Military traffic has added to the weight of trains and as a result lateness of arrival has become so regular as to make the existing time-table misleading. It is hoped that the new time-table will be a more accurate index to the actual running times of the trains.

The trains from Warrington to Euston aro in some cases similarly affected. The midnight express from Euston to War- rington remains unaltered, either as to time of departure or arrival. _

Intending passengers should study the time- tables carefully to save inconvenience._ _ _

The Warrington A.B.C. Railway Guide for Oetober is now on isale at all newsagents.

VIVID PERSONAL NARRATIVES.

• .Last Saturday the Allies in the West made • fierce assault upon the German lines. The Itrittsh attacked south and north of La Bassee und aj Ifooge; the French, in co-operation with us, went forward round Sonchez. Large gains were made. South of, La Bassee, the western outskirts of Hulluch—east of Ver- melles; the village of Loos---east bf Grenay— fell into our hands. The adVance in this quarter swept over the German trenches on a front of over five miles, piercing at some points to a depth of 4,000 yards. The French had even greater successes. Al- together the Germans lost we're than 100,000 mien killed, wounded and captured,, besides many guns, which do not appear as .yet to be fully counted. • •

===== 0002601_19151002_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PART OF WARRINGTON.

Our Territorials And negulars Prominent.

Warrington ,played its part in the great advance. The names of local men figure in the casualty lists of most of the regiments engaged. So far as the Territorials are con- cerned the 'Battalion does not 'appear to have been engaged as a whole—except on subsidiary but highly important work—but our bomb- throwers and the machine-gun section were in -file thick of the fray and have suffered to some extent, though at the time of writing—Friday eight—to what extent is not known. . As on many previous occasions, rumour has leetn busy spreading the most alarming reports and friends of men at the .Front would do well if they waited for authentic information before -unduly upsetting themselves by . what they hear.

OFFICERS KILLED AND WOUNDED

Second-Lieutenant Cheers, who was attached to our Territorials from the 3rd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment, was killed. Second-Lieutenant Stanger, an, officer of the Teriltorials, was wounded. Neither of these gentlemen is a Warrington . Wan.

A FALSE RUMOUR

It was rumoured that Sergeant Widocks, of the machine gun section, was in the casualty list, but on Thursday his wife received a post- card from him saying that he had gOt safely through." The Mayor's son has likewise heen named as among the casualties, but up to yesterday has friends had heard nothing to that effect.-

FEW KILLED; MANY WOUNDED.

It will be seen by the casualties we publish. there have been few killed, but many wounded. where is an impression that thhs was the general experience in this particular lighting.

A Hurried Message From Corporal

Skelliorne,

corporal Skelhorne—brother of Sergeant A. kelhorne, the well-known Warrington foot- hailer—who is serving with the Territorials, -writing to his sister, who resides at 219, Knuts- tord-road, and dating his letter Monday last, says :—'`l suppose you will wonder what has become of me, especially as you know where I am. and Will by now have learned .through the I)4pere of the events Of the laSt few dais: I Was not able to get a letter away earlier 'What I said about sad hearts at home has, you see, come true! . From what I hear the Germans would not take any 'prisoners. Well, it makes all the more for them to answer for."

• LATCHFORD RIFLEMAN CONIES THROUGH.

Rifleman James Cartwright. of the Rifle Brigade, came through the Great Advance" 'hattle unscathed. The following morning he wrote to his wife at 120, Slater-street, Latch- tord "Just a line to let you know that I received your letter and. 'The Examiner' on the night of the 24th. I daresay when you received my last letter you gueSsed that we were going into action. Well, thank God, I have come out of it 'alive—as. we 'consider we are all lucky that did come out alive. I shall never forget the 25th of this month if Ilive to ise a hundred. The sight was awful, and our leattalion has caught it;. but farther along the line they have had a great suc,c.ess as yon will no doubt have seen in the papers. . . I think although we got it pretty hot fre have taught Fritz a lesson on our front; but, my word, at what a cost! . . You may thank God that I got out .of such a hell upon earth as we were in en the 25th."

===== 0002601_19151002_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED AT WIN WICK.

Some Of The heroes Of Big Effort

Arrive.

On Monday evening and again on Wednesday evening large batches of wounded soldiers fresh from the Front arrived in special Red Cross trains from the South of England at Arpley station, Warrington, and from there were con- veyed in motor ambulances and care to the Lord Derby War Hospital at Winwick, as the County Asylum has been temporarily re-christened. In- cluded in the trainload who arrived on Wednes- slay were a number of 'men who had received their hurts in the big thrust forward which &as been developing so successfully for the Allies during the past few days. Many of the wounded warriors were able to walk—some requiring the aid or sticks and the support of willing MA.M.C. men—to the cars in waiting to take them to their destination, but *any others had to be conveyed in 'stretchers to the ambulance cars, the nature of their in- juries and their physical condition necessitating lnost careful handling. Both on Monday end on Wednesday large crowds gathered out- side the station to witness ,the arrival of the wounded men and to give them a cheer of thanks and enCoaragement.

PRIVATE W. H. FELL.

Old Latchford Footballer In Base Hospital.

"Admitted into hospital, wounded" and. "Rave been sent down to the base." By leav- ing these two lines incact a field postoar4 which reached Warrington during the week, rrivate William Daniel Fell, of the itb South I anca- shires, conveyed to his wife, who resides at 4, Cumberland-street, Latchford, and to his father and Sister, Mr. and Miss C. Fell, of 716, Knuts- ford-road, Latchford, the fact that he Was in- cluded among the wounded in the fierce _fight.

Private Fell is thirty years of age and, in addition to his wife, his family consists of three children, the youngest of whom is a baby only twelve months old. Prior to offering for active eservice in the early days of the war, Private Fell was employed as a compositor in the printing department at Messrs. J. Crosfield and Son's works.

Lord Kitchener Visits the " Boys." , Writing home to Warrington on the eve of the big fight he saia " I trust I shall come safely out of the big 'do "that is coming off.. . Of course, you know that a party of us Terri- torials are attached to the throwers. To-day Lord Kitchener came and in- spected them and us, I was standing quite close to him. He thanked the ' boys ' for all the good work they had done, and hoped they would do as well in the future, as there was some hard work to he done. That sounds rather pleasant! We go into the trenches on Friday night, and I hope the Lord will spare me and pull me through quite safely. From all one hears this is &in* to lat; another Sixteenth of June affair!" „ -

PRIVATE W. D. FELL.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
Shells Dropping Like Rain.

On Wednesday morning the followAg letter was received from him: "I have pulled through all right. We managed to do our work in the trenches on September 25th. And what a terrible day it was! It wns like hell let loose. Shells were dropping just like rain, and how we pulled through was a miracle. You have

PRIVATE A. WILSON. Wounded.

no ne('d to worry about my wound, for it is not much, compared to, those some of the other fellows ,have received; I saw some terrible wounds. . . . ,

===== 0002601_19151002_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
Risks The Bomb-throwers Take.

"There were three of us guarding a bomb stores in the first German trench, when word came along that bomb-throwers were needed in the first line, so off went the three of us with a bag of bombs. We had to run across open ground, and, it was terrible. However, we managed to get where we wanted to, and we had been holding the trench about five minutes when a shell blew the back of our trench on top of us, completely burykg five of us—three Highlanders, another bomb-thrower and my- self. It *as a hoiril* sensation.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
Rescued By Highlanders.

"Four Highlanders from Ilia next traverse came, and got two of us out. I don't think they got the others out as there was a. German, sniper watching us. To get to the dressing station we had to climb over the parapet and run for it, as there was no communication trench, and our men could not dig one, because the shells were flying too thick. We took four lines of trenches. . . . It was a lot worse than the fight ,on-June 16th."

===== 0002601_19151002_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
" Took Four Lines of Their Trenches."

Writing later to his brother, the Private said: "Lance-Corporal Freeman was buried with me. - I was in -the first lino over the parapet, and it was like hell! How we got through I don't know, but we took four lines of their trenches, and advanced about 600 yards. We were drawing all the attack while the French broke through. The High lenders and ourselves did our share as bomb throwers, and by the look of the papers, I think we got them on the run. We were in the thick of it, and some of the Highlanders fairly praised the South Lancs. bomb-throwers for their good work. We took a good mahy prisoners, and the German shells blew up some of their own men as ive were taking them back to our trenches."

===== 0002601_19151002_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
Seyenteen-year-old Soldier.

Private Wilson, who is only seventeen years of age, was educated at St. Barnabas' School. He belonged to the Territorials before the war. Prior to, " offering " for the war he , had been employed by Messrs. J. Crosfield'and Sons, Ltd, in their galvanising •department. When at home he attends St. Barnabas' Church a :id Sun- day School.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
A Missing. Brother.

Private Norman Wils%m, brother of Private A. Wilson, has been missing since June 16th. when the Territorials male a Charge open the German trenches. Ani' news of him will Le welcomed by hie -parents. , Another brother, Bombardier Frederick Wilson, joined up twelve months ago; and is now serving in the vicinity of the Dardanelles with a. detachment of the R.F.A., and Private John Wall, Rollestbn-stfeet now in training with the 3/4th South Lancs. Regiment, is a step- brother; • Private Wall was formerly employed in the engraving department at Messrs. Cros- field's.

A. F. Marrhani, chaplain. P.S.—I hear ,e is being moved to England' at once, so you viii be glad to hear he is near you. He will 4,end you his address as soon as he knows his destination."

KILLED.

RIFLEMAN T. O'DAY.

"Died Doing His Duty Like A Britisher."

Written from a hospital behind the firing line, Mr. and Mrs. Thos. O'Day, of 38. John-street, have received a letter from a .comrade of their son's, informing them that the latter—Rifleman Thos. O'Day, of the Rifle Brigade—has given his life for his country. The writer of the letter was Rifleman M. Rogerson, and his missive, which was undated, ran as follows :

===== 0002601_19151002_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
Corhracio'Breaks the News.

"I am very sorry to inform you that your son Tom has been killed in action. He was only a few yards from me when he was struck by a. bullet in the head. Shortly before this hap- pened he was asking me, if I got home, to call on you and tell you that he was going on Well. Of course, poor lad, he did net know what was going to happen. I hope it will be some consola- tion to you to know that he died doing his duty, like a real Britisher. I am now in hospital wounded."

===== 0002601_19151002_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
Patrol Lcader in Boy Scouts.

Rifleman O'Day was in his 19th year, and he joined the Rifle Brigade on September 2nd last year. He received his education kt St. Bene- dict's School, and was a regular worshipper at St. Benedict's Church. After leaving school he went to work in the 'box-room at Messrs. Cros- field and Sons' Soap Works. For a couple of years or more prior to "joining up" he was a member of the' Crosfleld Boy Scouts, latterly holding the rank of patrol leader.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====

RIFLEMAN JOSEPH OAKES.

Veil-Known Footballer _ Wounded IR

The Stomach.

Mr. and Mrs. William Oakes, of 136, Knuts- ford-road, have not heard direct that their on Rifleman Joseph Oakes' of the King's Royal Rifles has been wounded in the great advance. The news was received in a letter from Rifle- man James Cartwright (of the Rifle Brigade), who wrote home to 120, Slater-street: "Joe Oakes has been wounded in the stomach, and I believe he is going on all right. I do not know what has become of other Warrington lads yet." The parents will be glad if anyone can supply further information. Aged 31, and a native of Warrington, Rifleman Oakes was educated at St. James' School, and in peace times ,is employed as a tester at Messrs. Richmond's, this work, however, he relinquished thirteen months ago and enlisted, crossing to France early in the New Year.

Wounded.

Three months ago he was slightly wounded and spent a week in hospital. A smart and well-known footballer, the Rifle- man has played for the Warrington "A" team, and also for Runcorn and Cadishead clubs. Besides a third one, he holds the Warrington "A" and Cadishead medals.

RIFLEMAN WILLIAM MESON'.

Fletcher, Russell's Moulder I)ipw • Dangerously Wounded:

"The British Hospital, Hotel Bellevue, Wimereux, near Boulogne-surMere.—Dear Mrs. Heesom,—Your son, Private W. Heesom. has asked me to write and let you know that he is in hospital at the above address. He has been wounded -in the right shoulder; and is, I am sorry to say, dangerously ill. He will let you know again soon how he progresses.—Yours truly, Sister Bennett." On Thursday morning this letter reached 1.18, Knutsford-road, and broke to Mr. and Mrs. A. HeeSom the news 'of their son's misfortune. Later in the day arrived from Winchester a telegram which said the Rifleman was danger- ously ill from gunshot wounds in file abdomen, and lay at Rawal Pindi British General Hos-

pital, Wimereux; they regretted that permis- sion to visit him could not be granted." After leaving St. James' School as a lad, Rifleman Wm. Reef:porn entered as a moulder Messrs. Fletcher, Russell's; and there remained until the war outbreak—when he enlisted. Since then his parents have not seen him, as throughout the fourteen month 4 which have elapsed he had no leave. In early January he went to the Front, and has been a regular writer home. Aged 26, and a Warrington-born man, he took an interest in football, but did not play, himself. His brother Arthur is in the same regiment, and in March was wounded at Newts Chapelle. He' is now back in the:firing line.

- PRIVATE W. BACKLER.

Shot In Leg And -Fell Into Trench.

Private William Backler, of 82, Evelyn-street, Warrington,- one of four soldier brothers, has been wounded, and is at present in the No. 3 General Hospital, where he appears to be mak- ing a good recovery. Writing to his family recently he recouintrd the story of how he received his injur,-. "I have,'" he wrote, sent a field car.? home say- ing how well I was getting on ar.d only a few hours later I had to be carried on a stretcher; from which you can see that we dc,n't know from one minute to another bow we shall fare. . . .

===== 0002601_19151002_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
How It Happened.

A Small, party of u were .working together .repairing an old German. trench. It was about a hundred yards behind.tlie firing line. In the course of our duty we were walkiTg n the top of the parapet and the bullets kept whizzing last us. But we had to take no notice .4 h.,m; one has to chance his hick as to whetly3r tne hits .him 'or not. Suddenly I felt a snarl) ain in my leg. I walked on a bit, but I very S,Wil fell into the trench. . . . Our ambulance men are very quick in getting one away. It had just gone midnight when I was Wounded, and I-was in 'the Red .Cross .train and a hundred miles down the line in about eighteen hours." Three .Brothers Serving.

Private BaCkler..is .married and is 27 years of age. - His, parents, Mr, and Mrs.. Wm. Backler,.. live at ,07, Welleld-street, and his three brothers. who are ,serving are Privates Charles Ernest .Backler, Fred John Backler and Gunner Thomas Edward Backler. The first two of the trio are, like the wounded man, serving with the Warrington Territorials, whilst the last named is with the Royal Field Artillery, which he joined -during the first few days of the war. In , addition 'to: her. -four gallant sons, Mrs. Baelder 'has a brother-in-law and four nephews serving in the Army.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
Brother's Narrow Shave.

Her son Charles,l.rnest, had a narrow escape from being bUried.alive a short time ago, owing to the side of the trench, in which he was stationed, being blown in by a bursting .81101. Like his brother William Charles, is married, and in peace times made his' home at 24, Lloyd- street, Warrington. Fred has not yet gone to the Front, being in 'training with the 2hdi4th (Territorial) South Lancs.•Regiment. Before volunteering for active service, Private William Backler .was employed in the L. and N.-W. Railway Co.'s local engine sheds.

LANCE-CORPL. A. E. FREEMAN.

Thrower.

Wounded Before.

Aged 26 married; and the father of two chil- dren, 0 Lance-Corporal. PRIVATE W. AIKENHEAD.

Wounded Rifleman Expects To Go To Base.

Mrs. Susan Aikenhead, of 3, Rigby-street, off Academy-street, has received a field card from her son William, who is serving in the Rifle Brigade, announcing that he is in hospital wounded and, at the moment of writing, was expecting to be removed to the base. He added that be is going on well. The card was dated September 28th. The wounded man is 28 years of age, and is single. Prior to the war he.made his home with his mother, and other members 'of the family, at the address given above. He was latterly employed at Messrs. Richmond and Co.'s works at Grappenhall, and altogether he has been in the service of the film a matter of about four- teen years. He is a moulder by trade.. He received his education at the Heathside School.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
An Old Volunteer.

Private Aikenhead joined the Rite Brigade about a month after the outbreak of war. He was no stranger to military regimen, because for seven years be served in .the old Volunteers, giving up. civilian-soldiership when that body was metamorphosed into the present Terri- torials, the exigencies of his employment necessitating thot step. Footballer and Bowler. Private Aikeirhead is 'ell-known and liked by his former workmates; .and since it I:3eitilie known that he had met with a mishap tlr.7any. sympathetic ,messages have reached his raether

time, he had made himself so affable that he was liked by all and would have been promoted Corporal in a few days had he been spared. 1 also took a great liking to him and. will miss him very much as he was so willing- and_help- ful. Aged 18, Bombardier Wilkirisot was bona at St. Helens, and at the age of seven came with his parents to Warrington, where he became a scholar of Heathside School. Next he went to work for Messrs. W. IL Smith and Sons, news- agents, Central Station; but after a short time became a blacksmith at Bridge Foot for Mr. Burrows.

In the meantime he had been a Boy Scout, and upon attaining the necessary age joined the Warrington Territorials. Three years ago, however, he transferred to the Regulars—the Royal Field Artillery—and upon the war out- break was called up. Less than a t fortnight later he proceeded to the Continent, where he had since remained.

Previously Hurt.

In .the battle for Hill 60 he sprained his leg and was sent to hospital at Le Havre, later proceeding to the adjacent rest camp. From his advent in Warrington Bombardier Wilkinson attended the Holy Trinity Church "aiid Sniidair—SChool.; Ond'wa;9 a fallitd member' of their choir.

accept my sincerest sympathy with you in your great loss."

In a Dug-out At The Time.

Rifleman E. Burke, the comrade referred to above, wrote:—" It is with the greatest regret and sorrow that I take it upon myself to con- vey to you the sad ne„ws that your husband, Corporal Roberts, is dead. Ile was killed by .a high explosive shell on Wednesday morning, September 22nd. The battalion • were in the trenches, and your husband was in a dug-out at the time of his death. He was killed in- stantly, .and I dug him out at , the time, and carried him down to the brigade burial ground; and he was buried in the afternoon. I cannot express my sorrow in words. I am. a Warring- ton boy myself. Although your husband only joined my company fourteen days previous to his death, yet, in that short time, we became very chummy. He was respected by all who knew him. Please accept my deepest sym- pathy in your terrible bereavement. May God comfort you in your trouble."

===== 0002601_19151002_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
One ..Of Five Soldier Brothers.

Corporal Roberts, who was twenty-seven years of age, enlisted in September of last year, and was wounded on the 16th of June.. As a boy he attended the National School, Church-street, and, before enlisting, was employed by Messrs. Locker's, wireweavers, Church-street. Four of his brothers and a brother-in-law are serving in the Army. Two of the quintet have been wounded, and another is on his way to the Dardanelles.

BOMBARDIER STANLEY WILKINSON.

Killed In First-Line Trenches Just Before Promotion.

Bombardier Stanley Wilkinson, of the Royal Field Artillery, has given, his lifp,at the Front. In the following letter, dated September 23rd, the, news was received by his parents at 28, Horseifiaricet-street.

• "I, :On-behalf of the N.C.O.'s an men of your son's sub-section, have to convey to you our sincerest sympathy in the loss of your son Stanley, who was killed in action this after- noon, and will be buried to-night. He was with the Major and two more N.C.O.'s (one of whom was kiled, too) in the first line trenches. Although he had been with us but ,a short

of last year—to be precise. He was then 47 year of age, his 48th birthday being due on December Bth. Up to the time of enlisting Private Berry was working as a painter for Messrs. Fletcher, Russell Lind Co. Ile had been no fewer than 28 years with the firm and was regarded by them as a capable and trustworthy employe. Prior to going to Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co. over a quarter of a century ago, he. was employed for a xrumber .of years by Messrs. Rylands Bros. Private 'Berry will be well-known to the. older generation of •local Rugby football enthusiasts, for he used to play in the forward line for the town club and was accounted a speedy and extremely useful man. As a matter of fact he played for no fewer than thirteen- tonsecutive seasons for,, the i.Wirrington Club, .and it is somewhere about that number of seasons since he doffed the shirt and the shorts and took his place amongst the spectators. Keen Angler And Bowler. •

Private Berry was educated at the Heathside School. He was keenly interested in Friendly Society work, and for close upon twenty years had been a member of the "Morning Star" Lodge of the Ancient Order of Shepherds. One of his most cherished possessions was the emblem of the Order, presented to him when he had "passed through the chairs" of his Lodge. Private Berry found great delight in angling and at one time was a prominent mem- ber of the Warrington Anglers' Association'. Of later years Private Berry had gone in for bowling and had become an enthusiasticavielder of the woods. He did most of his bowling on the Corporation green near his home, .but he had frequently figured in the Liberal Club • team.

In politics Private Berry was ,an unce,ra- promising Liberal, and in the past he had done valuable spade work for his party as one of the committeemen for the Howley Ward.

RIFLEMAN JAMES BERRY.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
His Last Letter Home.

The last letter which Mrs. Berry received from her husband was dated August 26th, and in it he- said that he was all right. "It is vex.y quiet here," he added. "There's just a bit of sniping going on, and now and again a big tom:booms: One is all right in the trenches ‘.44:5 long as he does not show himself too often above the parapet. The German sharpshooters hardly ever miss. One of our lads got shot the first day in the trenches through being a bit too bold. He would keep putting his head above the parapet and the Germans got him at the finish. He was shot through the head. . . . . . . .We expect to 'be coming out shortly. They always take us out. and bring us in at night, because it would be too dangerous to go out in the daytime—they would shell us un- mercifully. They gave us a lively time at night when we are in the trenches. Bullets fall all about you—it's very hot _ . When we

come out of the trenches this time they say that it will he a month before we go in again."

"Thank God He Died Like a Man."

As the days—lengthening in fact into weeks —rolled by since Mrs. Berry received the letter referred to above and .no official, or ()the?, cor- roboration came to hand, she began to hope against hope that a mistake had been made, and that her husband, after all, was safe, even if not sound; for she accounted in her own mind for the sudden cessation of his letters, which hitherto had come most regularly, by the thought that he wa4 wounded and in hostiltal. On Saturday last, however, arrived a letter written by Rifleman Yule—a particular chum of her, husband's—which seems- to put beyond citiestion the fact that the gallant old Warring,- ton footballer, who took his Part so well on, many a playing field, has made "the great sacrifice" on a more glorious field, and has now to be numbered with the patriots whose memory is undying. "I dare say," wrote Rifleman Yule, "you will have been notified of your husband's death, but I thought I would just Imp you a line to let you know how he I did not hear of it until I came out of the trenches yesterday, and it happened over a week. ago. He did not suffer any pain, for death was in- stantaneous. He got shot while 'out digging with a party at night. He lies at rest in a little cemetery just behind the firing line. He told me, if anything did ever happen, to write to you, hoping that you would bear it bravely and not fret a deal. I know it is hard, but I would honour his wish and thank God he died like a man."

===== 0002601_19151002_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
A Fearless Rugby Forward.

" Buff " Berry, as Rifleman Berry was affectionately known to his intimates and admiring Wilderspool Rugby devotees, was of genial disposition and eas a forward on the football field was fearless and robustious, mak- ing up anything he lacked in science by the forcefulness of his play. In the. days when Berry was one of the idols of the crowd, Mr. Wm. Dillon was mtain of the Warrington first team, and the players included such well- known men as Harry Ashton, Frank Turner and Fair Turner.

ORDINARY OINTMENTS DiSPLACED.

IWHEN Zam-Buk is promptly applied to ascut or laceration of the skin it ensures disinfection of the wound, and destroys the numerous germs whose rapid reproduction is so dangerous. If poisonous microbes have started a festering in a cut or sore, Zam-Buk immediately lessens the pain, subdues the suppuration, abates the inflammation, and hastens complete recovery.

Zam-Buk is purely herbal and effectually displaces common fatty salves and ordinary antiseptic dressings. The usefulness and unequalled efficacy of Zam-Buk is proved daily in numberless cases of Eczema, Bad Legs, Ringworm, Piles, Poisoned Sores, etc. Zamßuk's timely aid is also keeping our gallant soldiers fit for the firing-line by saving them from reporting " sick " with sore feet or simple cuts, wounds or bruises.

HARVEST THANKSGIVIa

MORE FESTIVAL SERVICES IN LOCAL CHURCHES AND CHAPELS.

Special services of thanksgiving for the Se() ingathering of the harvest were held in a num- ber of local churches and ohapels on Sunday last, whilst others have boon held during the week, and yet more will take plaee.to-morrow. Specially largo congregations have boon the order at all the soli-iv:es so far held; a foot which, in some measure, is due to the wide- spread realisation of the added importance of the home harvest in these dire days of war.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
PARISH CHURCII.

The Rev. A. J. Bratt And The German

Tares.

There was a very large congregation at the Parish Church on Thursday evening to hear the Rev. A. J. Brett, a former pop`ular curate of the parish, and now vicar of Nether Witton, Mor- peth, preach the opening harvest thanksgiving sermon. Those parts of the church which lend themselves to it had been tastefully decorated. Specially bright was the musical portion of the service, the harvest hymns and the chorus "Achieved is the glorious work" from "The Creation" (Haydn) being finely rendered under the direction of Mr. F. M. Darby, the organist and choirmaster. The trio, "On Thee eaCh liv- ing soul awaits," was sung impressively and per- fectly by Master N. Davies and Messrs. J. 11. Draper and J. B. Poole. The Rev. A. J. Bratt took for his text St. Matthew xiii. 38, "Th. good seed are the chil- dren of the Kingdom, but the tares are the children of the wicked one."

It had, he said, been argued that if God was a iust God He ought to, have interfered on the side of right to prevent at the outset the terrible 'results that this war had brought about; but ream had been given a mind in order that he knight know right from wrong, and had the war been ended by a miracle, God would have taken from man his prerogative of judging for him, self. No one could read the literature of the last generation in Germany without seeing how the tares had been deliberately sown, tares that had eventually predominated and borne down the fruits of the good seed that undoubtedly existed in Germany. The children of the land had bekti taught, even from the kindergarten, through the elementary and secondary schools, up to the uni versity, the doctrine of blood and iron, which bad developed into the ideal that might was right. Gentleness, humility, and consideration for others, the elements of the Christian ideal, had been scornfully rejected for a perverted and cruel ideal, which hkd resulted in the present awful conflict. In the end right must prevail, and when it had prevailed and the war had been brought to a successful end, England must eon- tinue to stand true by her Christian ideal and work for its success. We must continue to teach to our children the true Christian virtues, the rejection of which for a harvest of tares had led to such disastrous results in Germany. The Rector and the Curates, the Revs. T. Vickers, J. M. Buelannster and and g. G. Siam- ton, took part in the service.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
01:K1N6 MEN'S MISSION.

LIM Preacher- And Mystery Of Seed-Time.

A pathetic, note in the celebration of the harvest thanksgiving services in connection with the Working Men's Mission Church, Bank Quay, was the fact that the services were con- ducted by a blind minister, in the person of the Rev. J. S. Morley, of Manchester. Formerly a Congregational pastor, the reverend gentleman continues to maintain an active interest in church work by preaching from time to time In various parts of the country. The church had been tastefully decorated for the occasion, special attention having been Oiven, by a will- .ing band of workers, to the communion table and pulpit. • Mr. Morley's morning text was taken from Psalm lxv. 11: "Thou crownest the year with Thy goodness." Life, he said, was a harvest in miniature. People were constantly engaged in sowing and reaping; and as in the case of the sowing and harvesting of the fruits of the earth they looked to God for His help, so in the spiritual life man was required to seek , for assistance from the same source. It was at harvest time that they recognised God's good- ness, and His constant care over His people from year to year. Not only' did He provide thqm with whal was really essential, but He also gave them other gifts, that might be looked upon as of a non-essential character, but which

assisted materially to help and cheer His people on. life's pathway.

A TASK BEYOND MAN.

The church was crowded at the evening ser- vice, during the course of whioh the choir gave a splendid rendition of the anthems, "The desert shall rejoice" end "The Wilderness." Speaking upon the difficulties associated with tke sowing of the seed, Mr. Morley said that the hours of anxiety, the times of doubt and the expenditure of what seemed useless labour, were all forgotten at harvest time. Sowing and harvest went hand in hand. Seed was a remark- able thing. It was a gift of God. Whatever knowledge science might bring to assist in any way man could never produce a seed. It was the same with life. It was full of mysteries, which could only be explained by the Creator. But in all lives there were the seasons of sow- ing, and many seeds wore scattered in various directions from which, although the sower was surrounded with difficulties, a harvest would result. The preacher went on to speak of the seeds of good influence, freedom, justiee and lore, and concluded by urging his hearers to continue in the good work in whieh they were engaged, and though many times discouraged, never to give up, but to look forward to the harvest time, when after sowing in tears they would undoubtedly "come again rejoicing, bringing their sheaves with them."

SUCCESSFUL MUSICAL SERVICE.

GERMANY'S DEAR FOOD.

High Prices -Cause Serious Anxiety In Berlin.

The manager of the Berlin markets, Herr Spieoker, publishes in the "Berliner Tageblatt" a long article on the increase in the price of foodstuffs. He says it cannot be denied that prices are very high, and that they are causing serious anxiety to the Government. In tabu- lated form Herr Spiocker compares the prices prevailing on September Ist, 1914, and on September Ist, 1915, according to the official market figures. Bull beef (Ochsenfleisoh) has increased from 79 and 90 to 107 and 122 marks (shillings) per 50 kilogrammes (about lcwt.). A contributing cause of tho dearness of meat was especially the deficiency in made-up fodder (Kraftfutter) and the !complete stoppage of imports from abroad.

The cows, the writer continues, give not only less buts poorer milk. Thus for the production of 11b. of butter a greater quantity of milk is used. A very great drought has also been ex- perienced. The meadows, particularly in the middle of Jul-, were in a very bad condition, and only recovered later after rain, so . that agriculturists were unable to give green fodder in place of the substitute. Eggs on September Ist, 1914, were ten ,pfennings (one penny) each; on September Ist, 1915, they were 14 to 16 pfennings (three halfpence). Butter increased from 2.40 and 3 to 3.80 and 4.20 marks (shillings) per kilogramme (21b.). Herr Spiecker shows also considerable increase in the prices of veal, mutton and pork. Hardly any fully fattened meat comes on the market, and the supplies are mostly of medium quality, but they are, nevertheless, sold at tremendous prices. The making of preserves was not advisable. Experienced workmen were lacking, and at least 30 per cent. of the goods have been spoiled. The writer proceeds to say that a harvest of 50 • million tons of potatoes is • expected, while normally the population uses from 14 to 15 million tons of eating potatoes. The pricca for this important food stuff are therefore too high. The poorer classes are hardly able to purchase it, in view also of the high 'prices of meat. Ho urges the Government to reduce the prices. He says that there is an excellent harvest of fruit, and cheap fruit has been available for the masses. Only for plums are prices very high. Poultry is very dear. This is due to the diminution of stock consequent on the lack of suitable fodder for feeding chickens.— " Standard."

untrimmed straw and fabric hats came from Austria and Germany, and perhaps the intention is to prevent the revival of that trade. That would be legitimate, but it means that the work- ing women will have to pay more for her head- gear, seeing that the British manufacturer will I be able to charge higher prices for his wares. If the tax is aimed at the luxurious foreign " confection " it is hardly worth the small revenue it will produce. If a woman pays ten or twelve guineas for a hat in Paris, she may as well pay the Government three or four more before she can bring It home. That is right enough, but' such instances are - few, and will hardly pay to collect the duties, which will involve the overhauling of personal luggage at the ports to see that no one is smuggling hats.

Evening Dress and the Theatre. As far as the theatre is coneernld, evening dress may be said to have disappeared. Rarely now are more than half a dozen women to be seen in the stalls in the regulation full dress. In the big stores and shops of London, the space once allotted to exquisite toilettes is now devoted to outfits and comforts for soldiers and sailors. The disappearance has, however, been gradual; at first the gravity of the struggle entered upon in August of last year was realised only by the few, the majority carried on business and pleasure as usual. There is now a marked change, and elaborate toilettes are felt to be out of place at home and abroad. When black, or black and white are not obli- gatory, soft shades of rose and blue are chosen. Many a the new evening dresses are suggestive of the Watteau type, and rather bunchy draperies caught up with flowers are a feature of skirts made of diaphanous materials.

The Revival of Fringe. The afternoon frock has Ibecome of great im- portance, for it takes the' place of the tra- ditional evening dress at places of amusement, and at the charity and other functions, now so few and far between. For its confection taffetas is for the moment the chosen fabric either in bine or black, and veiled with soft black silk net. it forms an ideal gown. The crispness of the taffetas is softened by the effect of the transparent net, the light folds of which are kept in place by a narrow- piping of the silk. Fringe in silk, and to match the taffetas In colour, is used to finish flounces, and even placed at the hems of plain skirts. This trim-

taing i 3 131:1CTOW in width and of the lightest description. It finishes also the edges of bodices and sleeves of the bell type. Velveteen, more especially the chiffon make, is combined most successfully with taffetas for smart day frocks. Velveteen is a purely British production which deserves to be es popular OS it bids fair to be this winter, for it is inexpensive, durable and handsome in appearance.

This Season's Bags. Pockets, it is said, will bullish the handbag, for those that find a place on this winter's out- door coats are spacious enough to hold all the etceteras that women have for so long carried in the übiquitous bag. The knell of thet blouse has been sounded a hundred times, yet every season it takes on a now lease of life, and in- creases in popularity. The reason is not far to seek, vie., it is too useful to be dispensed with. The slime remark applies to the bag, which changes its shape or renews its youth in some other way, every season. But the demand for it never slackens, and it will in all probability be as popular two or three years hence at the present moment. This season's bags are dis- tinguished for the beauty of their clasps and framework; they are dark in colour, chiefly blue or black, and are fitted with all kinds of useful things. The linings are very beautiful, and often of a delicate tint. Those in moire and faille are sometimes embroidered in eolours at the top, which adds considerably to the cost of the article. Rings and ribbon work are chiefly employed, and this addition is well within the capacity of women who are at all clever pith the needle. Women and Tobacco.

Cigarette smoking among women is not new confined to Bohemia, but has advanced into the wider and prosaic eireles of well-to-do classes. Smoking in the street lee not yet beaome general, but women enjoy their eigarettes in publie unabashed, and many who have acquired the tobacco habit have boldly adopted the holder and mouthpieoe. Makers of smokers' aecessories have not been slow to mark the changes in feminine smoking habits, with the result that dainty spesialitiee for women are to be seen In all the tobacconists' windows. Numbers of men have abjured tobacco in any form for the duration of the war, and surely women would do well to emulate this sensible example, especially now that Mr. McKenna has made cigarette smoking more expensive.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Barn House, Whitstable. The quaint old town of Whitstable was in- formally visited by the Queen it few days ago. At the time of the Coronation the Marys of Britain presented Her Majesty with a sum of motey, which was devoted to the purchase and maintenance of a picturesque dwelling situated on the outskirts of the town of Whitstable, and called the Barn House. Parties of about fourteen working girls ere received there for a short holiday by the sea every summer. Quite a feature of Whitstable, or rather its near suburb, Tankerton, are the bathing huts that stretch for a considerable distance along the shore. The Queen provided one for the use of the girls at Barn House, which from the be- ginning of October will be used as a eon- valeseent home for soldiers. The object of her Majesty's visit was the inspection of the house, which is exeeedingly• comfortable, and will doubtless be as fully appreciated by the wounded men as it has been by the girls for whose benefit and pleasure it was acquired and fitted up.

Women's Supreme Council. The Russians are said to be lacking in powers of organisation, but thoir women have con- elusively proved that the?' possess the invalu- able gift. On the outbreak of war a Supreme Council was formed of women only, which was to all intents and purposes e second govern- ment. It reeeived statutes from the Tsar, and at once began its work, viz., the controlling of twenty million women for war services. Up till the present it has raised end spent thirty million pounds sterling, but the value of its achievements cannot be computed. The magni- tude of this 'work is little undervtood outside Russia, but it ranks first among the triutrtphs won by women in the titanic struggle now deeimating the manhood of Durolje. The Flot Tax.

HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

Light gymnastics will cure all bf ronnd shoulders if persistently practised, with deep breathing and plenty of froh air.

Scotch Eggs.

Ingredients: 4 eggs, 1, egg and breaderumbs for coating, la. of sausages, frying fat. Boil the eggs for 15 minutes, and place them at once in cold water. When quite cool, shell them: ria,ve ready the sausagemeat, divide it into four equal portions, and roll one piece of meat round each egg, taking care to preserve the egg shape. Coat with egg and breadcrumbs, and fry in deep fat. Serve on a hot dish gar- nished with parsley.

Eggs and Cheese. Ingredients: ilb. of cheese, 1 tablespoonful of breaderumbs, 2 or 3 eggs, seasoning. Butter a pie-dish and lay the cheese, cut in thin layers, on the bottom of the dish. Break the eggs care- fully„and lay them on the cheese. Sprinkle with pepper and salt. Over all sprinkle the breaderumbs, with a little grated eheese, if liked. Bake for about fifteen minutes. The top of this dish should be a nice brown colour when cooked.

Eggs and Tomatoes. S or 4 ripe tomatoes, breaderumbs, 2 eggs,, seasoning. Butt;er a dish slice the tomatoes thinly, and place in the dish. Sprinkle with breadorumbs, pepper and salt, and lay a few small pieces of butter on the top. Break the eggs gently on to the crumbs, pour over all a very little milk, and bake for about twenty minutes. Serve hot.

Egg Creme. Ingredients: 3 or 4 eggs, 3 teaspoonfuls of cream, seasoning, 1 teaspoonful of vinegar.— Boil the eggs for fifteen minutes, and put into told water. When sold, remove the yolks and cut the whites into rings. Mash the yolks with a spoon, add cream and seasoning. Mix vinegar in gradually. Pile the mixture high on a dish and arrange the white rings round it. This may be eaten with toast, but is delicious served with cold ham.—" Rome Cookery." Beef Pieces And Macaroni.

The suecess of this dish depends very largely upon the slow cooking it receives. Take 11b. of beef pieces, remove any superfluous fat, and dip each piece into a mixture of flour, pepper and salt. Place them in a stewing jar with a cover. Dust over the meat with a tablespoon- ful of minced onion and a very little flour; add a teacupful of water, put the cover on the jar, and stand it in a cool oven. Let it cook for five hours. Have ready lb. of macaroni boiled in slightly salted water; drain it; add a small piece of butter and a little pepper and salt. Make a wall of this in a hot dish, place the meat in the middle, and serve with snippets of toast. To Cook Bananas.

Take ripe sound bananas. Allow two or more to ei eh person. Peel, and out in half length- ways. Use a clean frypan and a small lump of good butter—fresh is best if possible—if not some elarified bacon fat will do equally well. Melt the butter, put in bananas, and cook very covered with a plate or saucepan lid. Add little more butter if required and. pass a

knife under once or twice to prevent them sticking. When quite cooked remove plate or lid and fry a nice brown. Do not add water. Bananas cooked in this way, as well as being very economical, possess all the essential elements which constitute a nourishing and wholesome food. It is a very suitable dish for the nursery breakfast when eaten with bread. Wheatmeal Porridge.

If any difficulty is experienced in the diges- tion of oatmeal porridge, wheatmeal porridge will prove a most excellent substitute. This finds a place already in many homes, alternating once or twice a week with oatmeal. The economy of these foods, in this time of national stress, cannot be too highly estimated, but their value is probably even greater as an asset to the health of the people. Bring sufficient water to the boil in a pan for the required amount of porridge. Let the amount of meal be in propor- tion of Boz. to the pint of water in the pan. Moisten the meal in a small basin with cold water, making it smooth with a wooden spoon. Stir the moistened meal into the pan, stir briskly until the porridge boils, and lift the pan to a part of the stove where it will boil gently, put on the lid, and cook steadily for forty minutes or one hour. Add salt to taste, boil up briskly, and stir well before dishing. Serve vary hot with good sweet milk or cream.

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information In Popular Language.

- Fresh Air. - Night air is not poisonous. Shutting the windows brings bed Inek. Sickness is the worst kinclof betl luck, there- fore open the windows.

The Simple Met. A, yet we don't seem to hare found an infallible system of diet suitable alike for all ages and conditions of life. The stomachs and livers of people appear to differ as much as their noses, and there is some truth in the saying that "one man's food is another man's poison." Nevertheless, there are certain axiom- atic, guiding principles with regard to our daily food which we can lay down without appearing to be unduly arbitrary or dogmatic. It is a fact that the simpler and plainer the system of diet, the less difficulty there is in adapting it to large claeses of individuals.—" Good Health." Fruit for Child of Two Years Old.

Ripe bananas may be given provided care is taken to remove the stringy outside part, and, as a .rule, they are better mashed up with a little milk; soft, juicy peaches or apricots, ripe melon, and sweet grapes, all skins, stones and seeds being carefully removed. A slice or two of rips, apple is good as a finish to each meal. Strawberries, raspberries, and currants should be withheld till the child is older, al- though one or two of the first-named, if they are "peeled," as it wore, so that the seeds are removed—and this is easy enough if they are big ones—do no harm. Fruit juice, of course, is extremely good for babies at all times, and stewed fruit, apples and prunes especially (provided any skins or rough pieces are re- moved with care, for a child whose digestion is delicate especially) are also very good, as well as oranges (a particularly good fruit for childfen, although not, of course, so available in summer time).—"Queen." Health Laws For Children, The Juvenile Health Crusade advocates the following Ten Health Commandments for Chil- dren. The children are asked to adopt these health laws and sign a statement to that effect:

"1. I will try to have my bedroom window open at the top and bottom, at night as well as by day, whish will assist in preventing a eold. "2. I will try to have a bath at least once a week.

"3. I will try to keep my head, face, hands and finger nails as clean as possible. "4. I will clean my teeth and rinse my mouth every morning on getting up, and at night on going to bed. "5. I will always wash my hands before eat, in,g food. " I will lways eat slowly and chew ray food well.

"7. I will try to breathe through ray nose by keeping my mouth shut. "8. I will not cough or sneeze without turning my face or holding a handkerchief be- fore my mouth..

"9- I will not spit upon floors, stairways, or sidepaths. "10. I will love and raspeet my parents, try to say a kind word or do a kind action every day, and be kind to all dumb animals."

A SONG OF AUTUMN.

Translated from the German. The dry leaves are falling,—

The Cold breeze above Has etript of its glories The sorrowing grove. The hills are all weeping- The field is a waste; The songs of the forest Are silent and past;

And the songsters are vanished—. In armies they fly To a clime more benignant, A friendlier sky.

The thick mists ere veiling The valley in white; With the smoke of the village They blend in their flight.

And lo! on the mountain The wanderer stands, And sees the pale Autumn Invading the lands.

Then, sorrowful wanderer, Sigh not, nor weep; For Nature, though shrouded, Will wake from her sleep.

The Spring, proudly smiling, Shall all things revive. And gay bridal garments Of splendour shall give,

PARISH CHURCH CHANGE.

To Make The Holy Eucharist A Separate Service.

Under the caption Sunday morning services, the following appears in the current issue of the Warrington Parish Church Magazine: "We propose at the harvest festival service and on the first and third Sundays in the month after to eonneet the sermon with Matins, and to make the Holy Eusharist a separate serviee without break. The withdrawal of the major part of the congregation has always been a grief to ns., We have done our utmost be induce communi. cents to stay for worship, but 'without avail. Many kayo said the servise is too long and the strain of singing both at Matins and the Eneharist toe great, and as we have desided te separate the two services, for they are quite distinct, and to have short Matins and Sermon at 10.80 and the Celebration of the Holy Eucharist at 11.31, shoral on the first Sunday and plain on the third. We hays also thought it well to have an early Celebration of the Holy Communion every Sunday at Y as well as S. It is not the number of communicants that has led us to add this, but the desire to put it in the power of every one in the same family to com- municate early if they so wish. and as they should wish to do on the Lord's Day. We trait our eliort to help will be mush appreciated."
