NEW BOOKS.

Rabindranath Tagore. A 13iographieal Study. By Ernest Rhys. London: Macmillan & Co. ss. net.

Mr. Rhys' task, in writing this book, was a very difficult one. Mr. Tagore is a poet who has attained an enormous vogue in his native country, India, and who, through the wonder- fully beautiful translations into English which he has made of a few of his works, bids fair to achieve a great reputation among lovers of poetry in this eountry. But much of Mr. Tagore's work remains untranslated, and, as Mr. Rhys admits, even the works that are translated are said, by those competent to judge, to be finer in the original when they are accompanied by the Indian music to which they aro usually sung. It is not an easy task to present a study of a writer whose native tongue is unknown outside his own country, and of whose works only a. few are known: yet Mr. Rhys is so keenly sympathetic, he is so deeply penetrated with the spirit of Tagore's writings and teaching, that notwithstanding. the difficulties, his book is a very valuable commentary on the published translations of Mr. Tagore's work, and should do much to- wards elucidating some of their difficulties. To anyone unfamiliar with the form of Tagore's translations from his own writings the first impression seems to be one of strangeness, but this soon gives way to appreciation of its extreme beauty and the happiness of the imagery which he employs. His English ver- sions have been compared to some of the finss. passages of the Old Teetarnent, and the com- parison, in our opinion, is extremely apt. The subjects Mr. Tagore chooses for his writings are very varied, and he uses as mediums verse, prose and drama,. He treats of the philosophy of life in one volume, and, in another, he writes of children, and we will quote one exquisite example of his art from this book—" The Crescent Moon" :

Bless this little heart, this white soul that has won the kiss of heaven for our earth, Ho loves the light of the gun, he loves the eight of his mother's face, He has not learned to despise the dust and hanker after gold. Clasp him to your heart and bless him. He has come into this land of an hundred cross-roads. I know not how he chose you from the crowd, came to your door, and grasped your hand to ask his way. He will follow you, laughing and talking, and not a doubt in his heart, Keep his trust, lead him straight, and bless him.

The book called "Sadhana," is a, volume of addresses and lay-sermons which were delivered in America and England, and contains the message whieh Mr. Tagore, as a representative of the East wishes to give to the West. Mr. Rhys has the advantage of personal acquaintance with Mr. Tarore, and the glimpses ho gives us of the impressions made on Mr. Tagore by the eivilisation of the Western nations are very interesting. " .he spoke." says Mr. Rhys, "with alarm of the temper of the great nations and the life of the great cities like Paris and London, whose love of luxury, need of sensation, and craving for exoitement were up against every finer instinct he cherished. When he spoke of the forces in the western world which he thought must become disruptive and lead to trouble, and stretched out his hands, it might have been the moral map of Europe, with its teeming continent and restless atoms, that lay spread out before him. The major energies. as he viewed them, were not constructive; they did not make for the world's commonwealth, and by their nature they must come into conflict sooner or later." This was said before the war, and in recalling it, Mr. Rhys quotes a passage from " Sadhana " bear- ing on it. "Whenever some ancient civilisa- tion fell into decay and died, it was owing to causes which produced callousness of heart and led to the cheapening of man's worth: when either the State or some powerful group of men began to look upon the people as a mere instru- ment of their power: when, by compelling weaker races to slavery and trying to keep them down by every means, man struck at the foundation of his greatness. Civilisation can never sustain itself upon cannibalism of any form."

One can, perhaps, partially realise what the present war must mean to a man with the spiritual perceptions of Mr. Tagore. There is an extremely interesting section of the book devoted to the educational establish- ment which Mr. Tagoro runs at Shanti Niketan, which shows how deep and great is his interest in the future of India.

There is a. number of excellent photographs, which add greatly to the interest of the book.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Asylum Experiences. The advertisement for male attendants at the West Riding Asylum, Wakefield, set me mentally picturing that institution, with which I happen to be familiar. Some reader will no doubt be ready with tha obvious joke when I state that I have been at that asylum four, times. The lunatics from one district in which I lived some years were all accommo- dated there,' and it was customary for the Guardians'in that Union to go over and inter- view them at least once in twelve months and to invite representatives of the local Press to accompany them. I had heard that persons on the staff could not remain very long in •such an environment without themselves being affected, and on the earliest opportunity I questioned an attendant as to the truth .of this. • He had himself been at the work eight years, and there 'were others who had been Much longer, and he assured me that the popular notion had no foundation in fact. On another occasion I noticed an inmate who was walkineto and fro alone, and who seemed to be. carefully avoided by everybody. I men- tioned it to the' doctor, suggesting that I might have a word with the'lonely one. .".But," was the warning reply, " you'll only • get a snub for your pains. He's sure to re- sent the liberty with great show of indigna-, tion. He's convinced that he is the German Emperor!" Again, having lingered in the day room *and become temporarily isolated, I hastened along a corridor to rejoin my party, and' was escorted by a crowd of lunatics, some of whom snatched at pencils sticking out of My vest pocket, while others shouted in my ears most horrible ribaldries and blasphemies. I was forcibly reminded of Bunyan's pilgrim's progress through the Valley of the shadow! Married at 76.

I recently took occasion to refer to Mr. John Hartley, the Yorkshire dialect writer, who is a native of Halifax, and whose acquaintance I there made as a young man. He was one of my father-in-law's most intimate friends. In my former paragraph on the subject, I men- tioned that Mr. Hartley's second wife—the " Mally Grimes" of his writings—had passed away. r now note with interest that the venerable author, who will next month attain his 76th year, was last week married at the Birkenhead Registry Office to Mrs. Annie Spencer, who was born at Manchester in 1858, and who left Vancouver 8.C., about a couple of months ago to thus link her fortunes with those of Mr. Hartley. The Bride.

think it worthy of remark that I also know the lady quite well. She was a school mis- tress in a district in which I edited a news- paper some twenty years ago, and often con- tributed original poetry of more than averau merit. This was neatly written on slips daintily attached with ribbon or done up with prbtty wild flowers, and signed "Annie McCandlish." During the period in question, and after her removal, Mrs. Spencer wrote and published much over that signature—perhaps her maiden name, but at all events invariably. her pen name. She was, and in all likelihood still is, a member of the LancaShire Authors' Association.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0194.txt | included=true | included =====
Remarkable Dream.

The " terrible" dream narrated last week by Uncle Ned somehow brought to mind one told by Mr. Charles Garvice, the novelist. It is to the effect that, while in the midst of a series of short stories for a popular magazine, an excellent plot was revealed to him in a dream, but was erased from his mind when he awoke. He was in need of precisely that sort of material, therefore he resolved to will him- self "to dream the dream again the follow- ing night, and to wake up in the middle of it." We have the assurance of Mr. Garvice that it came off all right! Economical Cookery.

Our good Allies the French have always been noted for this. It is ,well to remember at the same time that they regard cookery as an art; and not as a mere domestic necessity. The re-opening of local schools for teaching it set me thinking of the subject generally. I well remember Professor Smithells of Leeds University, observing that the whole art of domestic management is not comprised in making a digestible suet pudding, or in pre- paring a family dinner at so much per head, though if every girl of marriageable age under- stood so much about domestic economy there would be a happier, and perhaps soberer Eng- land. There are good cooks who yet have so little general sense that they will allow milk to stand near an evil-smelling drain, and take no trouble to have the latter made unobjec- tionable. No doubt the teaching in such classes as are this week being opened here for the winter takes due account of matters of this ' soft. It was that same professor, too, who once in my hearing commented on the shallowness of kitchen knowledge. "The oven has been funny to-day," "Something • went wrong with the cake," "The bread would not rise," is the sort of thing echoed from the kitchen. In the past the average woman has' not been taught to connect cause with effect. "Though one must admit there were many more important things than mere domestic arts," said Professor Smithells, " no 'one who had any experience of life could have failed to observe how muca the comfort and happiness of life depended upon the proper management of the home." If the business that supports the home were run on the same rule-of-thumb principles as the latter frequently is, there would soon be acquaintance with the bank- ruptcy court. A broader scheme of education is needed for girls, superimposing the domes- tic arts upon a trained faculty for observation and deduction. That is gradually being recognised and realised, and the schools for instruction in cookery, millinery and dressmaking just re- opened at two or three schools in this borough are assuredly on right lines.

Scots' Proverbial Wisdom. An advertisement in another column cites "an interesting Scotch proverb." The national pawkiness is further manifested in " Soon is a gude house pulled down," in "A horse hired, never tired," and in " Speir at Jot& Thief, if I be an honest man." Another highly significant and characteristic one is " Silence grips the mouse." The Scotch variant of our " Satan finds some mischief still for idle hands to do "is " An idle brain is the Devil's workshop." Their " Anger's more hurtful than the wrong that caused it," while morally justifiable, is demonstrably true physiologically. One Scotch proverb hath it that " Meat feeds, cloth deeds, but breeding makes the man." The line is severely drawn at anything like flunkeyism or snobbery, how- ever, as witness "A nod frae a lord is a breakfast for a fool." Proverbs in General. .

- • - --- - For several. years I have been collecting such gems of crystallised wisdom, and my stock includes a, wonderful variety from the Far East. One ,Of the oldest of these well says, "In the Inn of Decision men sleep well." " Fortune will call at the smiling gate" is a Japanese way of expressing a truism and en- forcing a• lesson calculated to serve here at home just now especially. I have a book pub- lished so long ago as 1749 containing lots of proverbs with meanings. appended. Thus, under " Birds of a feather flock together" we have this—" Every fowler knows the truth of this proverb; but it has a further meaning than the association of irrational creatures; it intimates that society is a powerful attrac- tive; but the likeness is the lure that draws people of the same kidney together. A covey of partridges in the country is but an emblem

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 2, 1915.

DICKENS AND PEACE.

When Human Nature Is Opposed To Ideals.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE BATTLE OF LIFE."

In these days when mankind is apt to deplore that the peace ideal, which it had believq was almost within its grasp, has been rudoly shattered, and to "feel as if the world bad been pushed bask • five hundred years," a follower of Dickens may extract some comfort from the fact that the "Apostle of the People" was himself somewhat unconvinced regarding the possibility of perpetual peace. Of its desirability, of course, he was—as shown in a previous article—most profoundly convinced; and never ceased to entertain the hope that one day it might be realised. Towards this end he would throw into the balance all the weight• of his genius and influence—realising that, be the object attained or no, at least nothing but good could come from his efforts. Yet that this attainment was probable he did not emphasise a personal belief. ,To make this discovery many may have been surprised, because in the novelist's‘ works and other writings there could be found statements which might not lead one to that conclusion. It must, however, be remembered that "be did not run on any specific line or lineage of ideas; his themes are as numerous as his portraitures" (vide Mr. W. Walter Crotch), and one can only• take what appears to have been his most recurrent mood. Moreover, people in general do not believe that happiness and strife can go hand in hand; and (they say) since Dickens advocated laughter he must have been fighting for a realisable ideal—joy through peace.

practical result not entirely independent of divine agency, yet the more . firmly he became convinced that a perpetually optimistic out- look upon life was unnatural and. false, the more consistently he refrained from the un- interrupted painting of a summer sky over which thp clouds of disillusion were bound to roll sooner or later. Ahl let us make no claim On life's incognisa,ble sea To too exact a steering of our way!

is Humanity's perpetual cry. , Yes, Dickens knew it, and he knew that it will always be so. One has but to turn to his allegorical epitome "The Battle Of Life," in- vested as it is with that steaming atmosphere of joy and jollity which pervades all the Christ- mas Books, to realise in some degree the bitter hopelessness which must have absorbed him whenever, in the sanctuary of his own true thoughts, he could cast aside the rose-coloured glasses and 'face facts as they were. From that it does not .of course follow that his writings are insincere; only that one side of his philosophy iseleft at normal while the other is exaggerated—for the good of mankind. Never did he attempt to underrate the pathos of life ' 'no one," he said of what was perhaps his saddest book, "can ever believe this narrative in the reading more than I believed it in the writ- ing."

It is strange, but it is true. When Bismarck and Jules Favre met under the Walls of Paris in 1870 (says Forster), the Prussian waiting to open fire on the city, whilst the Frenchman was engaged in the. arduous task of showing the wisdom.of not doing it, Von Moltke was seated in a corner reading "Little Dorrit." And now more novels of Dickens are to be found in the German trenches than of any other writer of any nationality! Why? Do our enemies find therein excellent stimulus to their warlike' propensities? That cannot be; and the reason must remain a mystery. Perhaps he appeases their guilty consciences. At any rate, in "A Tale Of Two Cities," though deploring the methods by which the revolting French peasants are driven to realise their splendid ambitions, he never suggests that there was an alternative course, and merely concludes :

I see a beautiful city and a brilliant people rising from this abyss, and, in their struggles to be truly free, in their triumphs and slefeats, through long years to come, I see the evil of this time and of the previous time of which this is the natural birth [note the restriction] gradually making expiation for itself and wearing out.

Therefore that bloody revolution—" the worst of times "7 -was even more (being a means to an end) "the best of times.". This was the pivot upon which turned all his creed: Although war, might be inevitable, life was not quite futile.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
LOOKING ON BRIGHT SIDE.

But how did Dickens advocate laughter? G. K. Chesterton says that he devoted his genius in a somewhat special sense to the description of happiness. The fact is that he not 5o much preached happiness as that he relegated sorrow to its proper position and purpose—and, as Robert Lynd has it, perhaps one turns to Dickens most readily because he refuses to ignore death and yet keeps his hold on the comedy of things. At least, so he did in his maturer years; we know that his earlier work was devoted rather to the humorously- grotesque than to the happy, but then he had as yet hardly realised what true happiness was, and could only make as serviceable a cloak as possible from the cloth he had. Always he wrote from personal experience, and although carefully diminishing the sadness of life he was by no means oblivious of it. Many years before, the iron had entered into his own soul when he was set to wash bottles and stick on labels amid companions from whom he shrank in despair; and we know that for years after- wards—until, indeed, the district had entirely changed—he could not bear to walk in the neighbourhood of Hungerford Market. In "A Child's Dream, of a Star," too, Ile tells us how the early death of his dearest companion made his life a pilgrimage to the grave. And all this preyed upon his mind. As carefully as he suppressed it in his earlier work, so as he grew older and happier the pathetic note was more insistently struck. "Bleak House" marked the turning-point, and "Hard Times" emphasised it with unmistakable severity. Simultaneously the gaiety, becoming rarer, became also truer, and (alas!) in only one instance relapsed to the old fantastic and inimitable wildness. -

That, however, by the way. Experience had taught him that life is—and, in view of human nature, can only be—a continual strife against cause and circumstance; and that inevitably physical warfare will be involved. Above all he was human, and the attraction of human nature never ceased to draw him with its potent cords. Had this not been so he never could have acquired that vital grip upon the heart of a world-public. And, as Mr. Squeers says in "Nicholas Nickleby," "she's a rum 'un, is Natur'. Natur' is more easier conceived than described." In "Oliver Twist" the novelist ironically emphasises "what a beautiful thing human nature may be made to be; and how impartially the same amiable quali- ties are developed in the .finest lord and the dirtiest charity-boy." In his own com- position, with strong traits of idealism there was combined a robust common-senVe which warned him that there must be contained in others that natural pugnacity of temperament which was so marked an attribute of himself, and that everybody would not give it vent in a manner so splendid as he did. True it is that Dickens was a born fighter. In his early days, we are told, he was though delicate and frail "ever swift to range himself on the side of the oppressed and to combat the oppressor" : He, too, a man that knew all moods but fear, He, too, a fighter! Yet not his the strife That leaves dark scars on the fair face of life. He did not fight to rend the world apart, He fought to make it one in mind and heart, Building a broad and noble bridge to span The icy chasm that sunders man from man.

The warlike disposition, as a matter of fact, was essential to his greatness; "lie alone of modern writers did really destroy some of the wrongs he hated and bring about some of the reforms he desired," and you cannot do. that without fighting. It was precisely the same spirit which prompted him to make his last book his most ambitious one, despite the fact that he was writing it in declining health and amid innumerable distractions. And better it has been that, circumstances forbidding Dickens the usual physical indulgence of this tendency, he was compelled to stimulate his brain and give it an outlet through that channel. Herein mankind gained as, it realises—" as powerful an exponent of practical Christianity as ever led an apparently forlorn hope in the eternal crusade against evil." Upon the influences which accounted for the novelist's choosing the nobler mission when the whole course of his early life might have been expected to embitter his outlook and warp his nobler instincts beyond hope of readjustment, we need not enter. Suffice it that they existed.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0199.txt | included=true | included =====
WAYWARD HUMANITY.

Unfortunately one cannot have .omelettes without breaking eggs; and in this case Dickens's belief in the practicability of the peace-ideal was the eggs. Nobody realises the weak points in a doctrine so well as he who advocates it. Logical conviction, Dickens had found, is as often as not worse than useless when opposed to natural desire; and the traces of Civilisation's elusive epochs are only too frequently kicked over in the unreasoning pas- sion of mad moments. So that although he held it the business of a man. of letters—as, indeed, of any man—to develop our knowledge of character consciously aiming at some

===== 0002601_19151002_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
In that same concluding chapter he says:—

Along the Paris streets, the death-carts rumble, hollow and harsh. Six tumbrils carry the day's wine to La Guillotine. All the devour- ing and insatiate Monsters imagined since imagination could record itself, are fused in the one realisation, Guillotine. And yet there is not in France, with its rich variety of soil and climate, a blade, a leaf, a root, a sprig, 'a pepper- corn, which will grow to maturity under condi- tions more certain than those that have produced this horror. Crush Humanity out of shape once more, gnder similar hammers, and it will twist itself into the same tortured forms. Sow the same seed of rapacious licence and oppression over again, and it will surely yield the same fruit according to its kind.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
UNCERTAINTY OF PEACE TREATIES.

There is one other point: Should the ideal state of peaCe .be attained, would the " pre- carious equipoise" in which it would be main- tained allow it to be worth while?. Everything is so unreliable in these days of broken treaties and stupendous hypocrisy—and accidents will happen to the best-regulated machinery. Perhaps it is better for the world to remain as it is, making the best of a doubtful business. How well Dickens was reconciled. to this! "Try sometimes [says Alfred Heathfield in 'The Battle of Life '] to forget this battlefield and others like it in that broader battlefield of Life, on which the sun looks every day." "Really, I'm afraid that wouldn't soften his opinions, Mr. Alfred," said Snitchey. "The combatants are very eager fend very bitter in that same battle of Life. There's a great deal of cutting and slashing, and firing into people's heads from behind. There is terrible treading down, and trampling on. It is rather a bad business."

" I believe, Mr. Snitchey," said Alfred, "there are quiet victories and struggles, great sacrifices of self, and noble acts of heroism, in it—even in many of its apparent lightnesses and contradic- tions—not the less difficult to achieve, because they have no earthly chronicle or audience—done every day in nooks and corners, and in little households, and in, men's and women's hearts— any one of which might reconcile the sternest man to such a world, and fill him with belief and hope in it, though two-fourths of its people were at war, and another fourth at law; and that's a bold word." . . . "Well, well!" said the Doctor [Jeddler], "I am too old to be converted. . . . I began, as a boy, to have my thoughts directed to the real history of a battlefield. Sixty years have gone over my head, and I hkve never seen the Christian world, including Heaven knows how many loving mothers and good enough girls like mine, any- thing but mad for a battlefield. The same con- tradictions prevail in everything. One must either laugh or cry at such stupendous inconsis- tencies; and I prefer to laugh."

And there the matter can be left. Presum- abb., the novelist's chief use for peace was that it facilitated home reform—" in love of home the love of country has its rise" he says in "The Old Curiosity Shop." In 1854 we find him mourning the war: "I fear I clearly see that, for years to come, domestic reforms are shaken to the root." On another occasion he said of war: "There are seasons when the evils of peace, though not so acutely felt, are im- measurably greater." And yet—as Mr. J. Cuming Walters has pointed out—Dickens was born in a period sick of war! That period, however, was also trained to war; which may account for the novelises declaring:—

It is because I dread and hate the miseries of tyranny and war, because I would not be soldier- ridden, nor have other men so, that I am not for the disarming of England.

How times change! Now it is contended that —at least to some extent=-England must dis- arm. Can it be that the cause of Peace is making progress? Well, Hope will not trim her lamp the less brightly for him and his because of this' impulse to their struggling fortunes; and trust me, reader, they deserve her light and need it sorely.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
REST.

When I crawled into bed last night, A thousand cares, it seemed, beset me; I longed to just drop out of sight, To die and let the world forget me; My dearest wish- was but to close My weary eyes and drift away To leave behind me all my woes And sleep until the judgment day,

This morning I awoke to hear New voices calling me to duty; My vision, blurred before, was clear, I found the world arrayed in beauty; The hopes which I had thought were vain And had in sorrow cast aside Returned to give me strength again And point the way to paths. untried.

How foolishly we often fret, When little troubles come to taunt us; How often we decide to let Poor, little difficulties daunt us; How often cowardly we swerve And weakly shamble down the hill, When but a little rest would serve. To keep us bravely mounting still.

IN NORTHERN RUSSIA.

How Life in Villages Complies With Wav Conditions.

THE PEASANT WOMEN.

BY HAROLD MAYBURY

(Author of " A Russian's Impressions").

Night has falleu over this land of immeasur- able distances, a night-such as only those who

live in the Northern lands of Europe ever see. It is no darker than an English twilight. From the Gulf of Finland a slight breeze is blowing, making the yellow fields of waving corn rustle and sway; so that its sighing adds to the multi- tudinous'noises of the night. A bat flies through the gloom and soon is lost to view. From the marshes a distance away comes the shrill cries • of storks and quails with opasion.ally the melan- choly howl of a wolf afar 'off in the pine forest. The Russian peasant women- and boys have fin- ished their day's toil in the fields, and now are outside their " izbas or wooden. dwelling- houses, for only at eventide can they take their pleasures and make merry. Borne on the even- ing breezes is the sound of "balalaika" and melodeon played by a. Couple of aged Russian peasants, together with the rich and full sing- ing of the youths brought up and reared amongst the pine trees and forests of this Northern land. Inside the " izbas " a few naphtha lamps burn dim, casting ghostly shadows o'er the scene. Groups of peasant girls clad in their picturesque costumes of Many hues, with coloured kerchiefs around their heads, exchange confidences and talk of their men folk far away in the firing line. Some few are performing peasant dances, Whilst others are partaking of tea from a huge " samovar " near by. Many of their elders have. already retired to rest, or are praying before their Holy " Ikons " .for the success of .harvest or for those of their own kith and kin fighting for the Emperor in \this present mighty struggle. No wonder' these Russians are so romantic and emotional, for the perfume of the pines, the en- chanting odour of the flowers with the dew of nigyt upon them, the shadows, the music and singing, the earth bathed in silvery light, and the air wondrous and cool, full or softness, all tend to make them as such.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
FEW MEN WHO HAVE REMAINED.

There are few men of military age in the village, for the call of the Empire has been too great for them. Throughout the day the familiar ring of their axes is no longer beard. The tall trees in the Northern forest do not now bend and fall beneath their Mighty strokes, for instead of the wielding of the axe these, rough peasants are mastering the use of rifle and bayonet. Even in this small Village the toll of war has made itself .manifest. In the fields from early morn until sundown, peasant women

have worked throughout the summer months, tending the oxen, sowing potatoes, and prepar- ing for the coming of harvest time. Now the fruits of their labours is nigh, and in each " izba " prior to retiring to rest, before the sacred pictures of the Madonna, they utter their prayers of thanksgiving for the success of their toil. The music has died down and all is nigh silent except for an occasional laugh and jest• from one of the more boisterous of these fair daughters of the soil.- They are all making their way towards it large and pretentious building situated h. little distance down the road: It is the abode of their employer, and there they wend their way to wish the good " barin "• and his wife a last . good-night and to offer their prayers for safety until the,dawn appears. This is one of the customs of the peasants of North- ern Russia, they always visiting their employer in groups before retiring to rest. There one saw the mistress of the house and _ .

her husband, standing at the entrance of their dwelling receiving the blessings of the peasants at the close of another day. Many bring with them offerings Of wild flowers, others small sheaves of wheat, oats, barley and rye, As a token of the handiwork of .Nature and human labours. Over all lies the- soothing fragrance and freshness of a summer's night. The thatched " izbas" look like sheets of beaten silver by the light of the moon, and the gardens and orchards of this Russian village one imagines as the abode of wood nymphs and fairies, which tales the peasant children are never tired of listening to. The lights grow fewer, and all is silent, Presently out of the stillness of the night the sound of a bell rings out loud and clear, causing the birds to flutter restlessly in the trees and the oxen in their stalls to stir uneasily in the .midst of their slumbers. It is the watchman making his round of the village to assure the inhabitants that all is well, and that • they can slumber in peace. None go,to sleep tintilhis voice is heard.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0205.txt | included=true | included =====
GOD WILL PROVIDE.

The Russian peasant's chief terror is fire; that is why the watchman makes his nightly journey through the village. Clad in a blue • smoak, top boots of red leather, and peak cap, in summer, and a great sheepskin coat and fur hat, in winter, his figure is a familiar one in all country places. After his, task he returns to his appointed place, there to rest in the open, ever alert and watchful . for the care of the vil- lagers. At the least sign of fire his bell rings out noisily warning the -inhabitants of danger. It is only at dawn that his vigilance ceases, for then it is that his duties are over. Although many miles from, the war zone and busy cities, hardships are being endured and sacrifices made on behalf, of the common cause of liberty and justice. The burden and anxiety of 4making both ends meet owing to Many of the peasants being at the war is largely borne by the women. in this. Northern land. With true patriotism have they taken the place of their menfolk in the agricultural districts, • there to . work from morning until night in the fields in readiness'for the harvest. " NiChievo " (nothing matters), "God will provide." This is how they look at it. Moreover they are helping to maintain their more unfortunate brethren, from those districts where the horror Of war has laid its -heavy hand. Several in the village 'have arrived from the country places round Riga, two even are Polish peasants from Warsaw, all of them arriving without money or means of sustenance. It is the same in all the small villages in this North- ern land. The spirit of hospitality is strong within them, for to those in distress there is ever the open door of the peasant. Robbery is unknown in these parts; so the " izbas " are never bolted and barred th the stranger. Oft- times in the summer months aged resin-tappers from - farther north journey to the larger vil- lages. If it is nightfall or late afternoon, and no one is resident in the " izba" one has only .to push open the door and malt oneself com- fortable and there partake- of what food there is in the house. In nearly all " izbas" is to be found a bowl of " stchie " to which the traveller May help himself, the only return being the offering of a blessing on the house. The peasants' trust in Providence is surprising. Many of them do not`know the reason for their country being at war, for news travels slowly across this mighty, expanse of empire, The ,Holy. Father willed 11," they say, "and the Emperor called our men to fight. They have gone' and we re- main. The harvest must be gathered in, for soon the winter snows will lie over our valleys and plains, then only is there rest from our labours." As soon as the sun casts its long shafts of light across the Eastern sky the peasant women are awake, and all is bustle and excite- ment. Another day has'dawned, and all Nature is astir. There can be no idleness, for -it is a 'fight against time. Ever with the peasants are the thoughts that in a little while • will the summer have flown, and in its stead the dreary winter months, ith its howling winds, ice and snow, casting desolation o'er its now fair and fertile plain's. Verily indeed are the peasants in this 'Northern clime enduring hardships whilst their menfolk are fighting for the empire. Some day, not Yet-, the Thisiian race Of people will bee3me the foremost nation of the world,, and it will come when the idea of the overwhelming vastness a territory is shaken from the minds of its people and the pesan.4 come into con- tact with city life,

1815-1915.

Talk With A Warrington Centenarian.

EARLY RAILWAY DAYS.

BY ARTHUR MOUNFIELD.

The year of Waterloo belongs to the far past. A century of rapid movement has made it so dim and remote that it comes as a Shock to realise 'that we are joined to it by the span of a life—a long life, it is true, but still a single lif e. r. James Chater, with whom we enjoyed a talk on Monday last, was born amid the buzz that marked the downfall of Napoleon, was a grown youth when Stevenson ran his. first locomotive, has seen the world encircled by railways, .by the telegraph, telephone, and " wireless," saw the infancy of steam trac- tion, and. has lived to see the 'maturity of electricity and • petrol. A list of the social changes which such a life has covered makes a centenarian seem a survival from another age and realm. It is interesting and it is weird. It reveals as .nothing else does how fast the world is moving. Mr. Chater's life has known none of the vicissitudes which such a whirling century would suggest. It has been placid and com- paratively.uneventful. The trade of a cabinet maker (interesting and conducive to longevity if anything is) found him occupation and has kept his interest right to the end. I learned that he is, a cabinet maker of the second gen- eration (a further inducement to longevity), his father having -sustained such a business for

SAW WELLINGTON AND SCOTT.

He began his carper on September 26th, 1815, in the High-street of Sunderland, in a house next to the " Grey Ilorse." An out- standing memory of his youth .was the visit of the Duke •of Wellington and Sir Walter Scott to the town in 1827. Arches spanne! the streets, ladies in white stood to make a welcome, and when the horses were taken from the carriages to allow the Duke to be pulled through the streets by admirers, he gave a hand .at one of the ropes. He travelled in an open truck upon Steven- son's first -railway from Stockton to Dairling- ton, but tells .of an earlier railway from Sun- derland to Ryhope, on which the conveyances were pulled by ropes. On one occasion he was one Of a patient crew who waited for the trucks to begin- to move: Patience was needed on all occasions on ancient railways. In this instance it failed to have its reward. They waited long without feeling the slight- est tug or movement. Theii a discovery was Made. The engine had `started and wound its customary fathoms of rope, but someone had omitted the formality of attaching the hook to. the waggon, and the rope went on the journey alone. I gathered that Sunderland, in the bad old days, levied a toll upon all who crossed its famous iron bridge. This came out in a skit, which my facetious and reminiscent friend of a century and 'a day sang for me. 1.-Tp the High-street I marched . The fair bridge for to son; And when walking over it A- fellow stopped me; Says he, "My good lad, • You've a ha'penny to pay, For everyone. does it That passes this way." "

My ha'penny I paid, •O'er the bridge I did go, And, I just took a peep, At the sailors below: Some were up the rigging, . And some down below, Others pulling at ropes • And crying "Yo, •ho."

CHORUS:

Well done,- Sunderland, canny hard, Sunderland Sunderland's surely a wonderful town.

A tale that stuck in 'a youthful memory and that •lingers to old age belongs to the sinking of coal pits by a Mr. -Pemberton. The shaft sinking- had been carried to 'a depth which left some of the men incredulous, and one of them declared that they would never find coal. Mr. Pemberton made the caustic retort, "If you can't get coal, then go ahead and get , cinders.". But they found- coal, and the pits still flourish. It is curious to relate that Mr. Chater was regarded .in youth as of delicate and unprom- ising health. A fine old oil painting was shown to Inc of a,sister who declined and died at the age ,of twenty-four. But early fears were falsified in his case, and old age finds him actively cheerful and undisposed to quarrel with Warrington or its climate. A sideboard completed in his 95th year (all the More a triumph because made from packing cases) adorns the living room, and on Monday last was resplendent with a bouquet of roses, very thoughtfully bestowed by Mr. -and Mrs. Fulton-Smith,

Like every home-loving. Englishman, he would like to see once more his native town.. The fates may deny this, but another wish less difficult to fulfil vas met on Wednesday by the kindness of the Mayor and Mayoress. He wished to see the completed Warrington Budge, and had the satisfaction of surveying it from the Mayoral motor car. • Mr. Chater-can still write without the aid of glasses, and gives no special trouble to his daughter, into whose .care a kindly fortune has placed him in. his.. mature years.

A girl without an appetite can afford to marry a poet, Much happiness is due to ability to forget tin, pleasant things.

POLAND'S HOLY PLACE.

An Incident Of The Great Upheaval On The

Eastern Front.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
A FAMOUS PICTURE LOOTED.

By ARTHUR

• We have been told that among the war pic- tures circulating in Germany is a penny post- card which bears the legend " The famous picture of the Virgin and Child captured from Czenstochowa by our gallant army." At the top of the card is a portrait of the Kaiser and beneath a picture of a tender-faced Virgin and Child.

Behind this crude postcard lies a story, of

which one 'would. gladly be spared the telling. We tell it as far as we have learned it, because so little .of the story of Poland's woes is ever likely to reach us.. Behind the bald official statements of a great Russian retreat and, the speculations of war experts based thereupon lies the tale of a national upheaval probably unparalleled in the history of the world. A Christian nation. has invaded another Christian nation; millions of panic-stricken people are swept before an army which defiles all that it touches and makes a mad carnival at the expense of religion and of every- thing else that civilised nations have agreed

to honour. The woes of Belgium are small in magnitude when compared with the sorrows of Poland. Such a colossal evil the patient autumn moon has never looked upon. The mention of the picture postcard on which the Kaiser's face is flaunted with the Madonna and Child will serve to open one tiny peep-hole upon Poland's hour of an4aL-1.1.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OLDEST PICTURE IN CHRISTENDOM.

The town of Czenstochowa has been the resting-place of a dim old picture reputed to be the oldest in Christendom. Tradition has it that the artist was Saint Luke the Evangel- ist, and for anything that experts can suggeit to the contrary this simple painting may date from the very earliest days of the Christian era. In the second century it is reputed to

have been in Jerusalem' whence it travelled to Constantinople, finally finding a resting-place at Czenstochowas, where it has been an abject of veneration for nine centuries.

Not that its career has been, an untroubled one. ; It bears upon the faces of the mother and Child marks of Mongol arrows, , and its escape from destruction during the Tartar wars was. a narrow one.. But the scars do btit endear it to the simple minds of Poland, .and they tell each other with a touch of native superstition that the marks of sorrow upon the faces will disappear upon the day when their land regains its freedom. The picture itself shows no mark of the artistic genius. It has fervour, but only the fervour of a devoted Christian mind. It is small, and is almost black with age' and the two soft faces that fill it only rise from the sombre background after the beholder has gazed upon it for a moment or two. Viewed with the artist's eye and without the help of Christian imagination the picture would not bo called beautiful, Itit coming from a dim and far-off age it makes an appeal which the heart cannot resist.

Czenstochowa has delighted in the honour of being its custodian. It has framed this little picture in a frame that blazes with diamonds, rubies, emeralds, topazes and pearls, _set upon a background of gold. Its

possession makes the town " the heart of Poland," and the picture is " the heart of

its heart." To. the church that enshrines it pilgrims'lravel hundreds of miles. They visit it ,as pilgrims visit Mecca or the Holy Sepul- chre. Of the scene inside the church when the curtain is withdrawn .a vivid description _ _ _

is given by the Right Hon. .W. F. Bailey in a " Fortnightly Review " article, and we quote it in order that the story that lies behind the Kaiser's postcard may stand in its true set- ting.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
PILGRIMS AT THE SHRINE OF POLAND.

" The streets are thronged, the snow has been trodden into marble solidity, and the town • sparkles in the chilly sunshine. Cross- crowned spires and copper-capped turrets float fairylike ' against the pale azure of the sky. From all sorts of sheltered nooks and crannies, where they have passed the night, • cowering under their shaggy , sheepskins, country folk creep forth to meet whatever fortune the day has in store for them. Round the Holy Place .small church that enshrines the relic which is revered by the Eastern and Western Church alike—a fact to be remem- bered—round the home of the oldest picture in the Christian world, a surging crowd has already collected. But the church doors are still shut. Backwards and forwards sway the multitude. A thousand tired, eager faces are lifted to watch for the swinging back of the

"Is there Freedom left in England

The first of these two poems was written b, to Mrs. G. K. Chesterton, wife of the wet] poem.

THE aUESTION.

(By an Englishwoman in Germany). Are there bluebells still in England? Is there left a happy place Where they grow in all their glory, Nodding with a careless grace?

Are there hedgerows still in England? ...AR untrimmed and growing wild, Honeysuckle and dog roses Dear to every English child?

Is there joy left in old England? Or have children grown so stern. Thinking only of their duty And the lessons they must learn?

Is there freedom left in England? Is my England England still, Not to be coerced or driven, Save by choice and of her will?

Oh. my country! oh, my country! How I tremble, how I fear When I see the awful bondage That has fallen on us here.

May God spare you, oh, my eountr : Stamped upon. you is the worth Of a nobler far dominion Than of force upon the earth.

Forge no chains for Children's children; Stilt let thy law bp liberty; 'England, mother country, girded By the broad zone of the sea.

ORIGIN OF MOVING PICTURES.

Present Marvellous Productions Came From

A Simple Experiment.

The beginning of moving pictures was in this wise: Sir John Herschel, in the year 1826, asked his friend, Charles Babbage,,how he would show both sides of a shilling at once. -Babbage re- plied by taking a shilling and holding it to mirror. This did not satisfy Sir John,' who set the shilling spinning upon the dinner table,' at the same time pointing out that if the eye_ is placed on 41 level with the rotating coin both sides can be seen at once. Babbage, was so struck by the experiment that the next day he deecribed it to a friend, Dr. Fitton, Who imme-

===== 0002601_19151002_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
MOUNFIELD.

barriers. Boom ! Soft, rich, full of sonorous sweetness, the great bell of Czenstochowa tolls 'out its welcome, and at its tolling every head is uncovered, and the sacred sign is made by thousands of fluttering hands. " Then very slowly the heavy doors unclose and the entire throng bows to the ground. Aristocrats, officers, tradespeople, peasants, bend side by side. For through the dusky gloom of the interior can be seen the glittering rows of the tall wax candles which burn per- petually before the heavy silver and gold em- broidered curtain veiling the 'heart of the heart of Poland.' The organ breathes out a few cho*s as very quietly, three .or four abreast, the pilgrims enter the building. And when every square foot of the church is occu- pied, the doors fall to again, and those With- 'out must await their turn for admittance.

"Like a deep whisper growing ever fuller and clearer, like the far-off murmur of the sea, the music swells out, and a mysterious Slavonic chant soars up towards the lofty rose and sapphire windows, up higher still into the golden mists of the roof. Such Music! It seems to carry with it, as does the ocean, the 'whole awful burden of humanity; it seems to be the cries and prayers of centuries that have worn and beaten themselves out in tears and lamentation. It .is the questioning of generations Upon generations of pale exist- ences seeking Still to discover the why and the .wherefore of their. being—the call of suffer- ing creation to the Creator. " And the door through which the hearts and untaught minds of these poor Polish people hope to pass into the presence of Him whom no man hath seen or can see, is through the jewelled frame of the little picture of human love which hangs here behind this silver and gold curtain; Out, and up, and over all rises the glorious and now .triumph- ant chanting. Sops and choking sighs bear it company. Passionate, tear-filled Slav eyes are fixed on the high altar, now dimly seen through silvery clouds of incense smoke.. Amongst the scarcely breathing throng there is a sudden movement, a ripple of intense excitement, then, absolute stillness, for the music ceases. A tiny, bell tinkles. The heavy curtains part asunder, and the picture becomes visible."

THE GATE OF HEAVEN. Yes, crude and pitiful as it appears to us, that jewelled picture frame is the Gate of Heaven to the innocent Slav and the church that holds it is the Holy of Holies. Only for five minutes are the pilgrims allowed to look upon the treasure, though they may have travelled for weeks through snow and wind to do it rever- ence. The curtain closes, the organ peals out, and they pass out to make room for other worshippers to enter. So it has been for generations. The little painting holds a place unchallenged in Poland's heart. THE FATE CF THE PICTURE.

• Not long ago Germany sought the help of the Poles against Russia. There was pub; fished a statement that the Virgin and Clulu had appeared to the Kaiser in a vision, and with tears implored him to rescue the shrill° from the Russians. It was promised that the wish Of the Virgin should be done, and the help of the people was asked for. Part of their reward was to he that fresh and rare jewels should be added to the shrine at Czenstoehowa.

The Poles tore up the proclamation in the streets and the Kaiser was told that " the people nor their religion was for 01'7' - Angry at their refusal, Germany took a shoals- ful revenge. The town was sacked and 45 People outraged. The picture was torn 1113111 its frame and sent to Germany, and in its place a portrait of the Kaiser in uniform was placed amid lights on the dismantled altar. The. people—such as had not fled the place were daily driven at the point of the bayonet to kneel before the picture. How deeply this piece of brutality has pierced the soul of the nation may perhaps some day be discovered • to the vulgar German mind. But there are 'limits even to the obtusenegs of the Prussian, and a copy of the fainolis painting has been placed upon the altar. The ancient picture of the Virgin and Child forms part of the loot which has ,gone to en- rich Berlin. The city shows its pride in the achievement by the issue of a picture post- card, and meanwhile her professors write fur- ther articles to enlarge uponV German en- lightenment and express a mild surprise that their nation is not better loved.

MUNITIONS COURT.

First Local Sitting Of New

Tribunal.

TWO CASES DEALT WITH.

The first sitting of the recently constituted Munitions Court for the Warrington area was held an Monday afternoon at the Labour Ex- change, Sankey-street„ when there were but two -c,ases for decision and the proceedings lasted but a few minutes. Mr. T. J. Ridgway (the Registrar of the local County Court) sat as president of the Court, and he had the assistance of two assessors—Mr. T. Atherton, representing the employers, and Mr. J. Appleton (of the Steam Engine-Makers' Society, Earlestown Branch) representing the men.

WHY HE WANTED TO LEAVE.

Court Refuse Applicant a Certificate.

- - - There first appeared before the Court William Lamb, of ,-24, Priory-streeti Warringtpn, who made a Complaint that 'the Vulcan Foundry Company had unreasonably withheld his certifi- cate. In a written statement in support of his con- tention, Lamb argued that he was not employed on munitions at the Vulcan Works, and said that be wanted his discharge. In answer to the President of the Tribunal, Lamb said that the statement he had made was a true and accurate one; he was not working on munitions, but was engaged on locomotives. The President: At engineering work?

Lamb: Yes, sir! And don't you know that engineering work comes within the terms of the Act ?—Lamb gave a negative reply and added that the reason why he desired a change was that ..he Vulcan Works were rather a long way from his home, and that he wanted employment a bit nearer. ' In answer to further questions Lamb said that he had been working at the Vulcan. Works for about seven or eight weeks, having pre- viously worked for Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co. • The President: Why did you leave Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co.? Lamb: I went after another job that I thought would be better, but I didn't fetch it off. So I had a look round an d heard about a berth going at the Vulcan Works, and so I went there

Why The Firm Objected. Mr.' G. F. F. White (who acts as clerk to the Court) said he had received a communication from Mr. W. Collingwood, the managing direc- for the Vulcan Foundry, Ltd. Lamb said that „lie wished to go drilling at a Warrington works. Remarking that the man him-elf Lad r dmittej that he was engaged on engineering work, and that such work most distinctly came within the terms of the Order which that Court was appointed to carry out, the President announced that ,the certificate sought would not be granted.

WHITECROSS WORKER FINED. Turned Up Late and Drunk.

Percy Baxter, of 16, David-street, Warrington, appeared to answer a complaint made against him by the Whitecross Company to the effect that on September 13th he failed to comply with the regulations of that company by not present- ing himself for duty at 5.30 a.m., and by com- ing at 7.30 in a state of intoxication and unfit for work. The man admitted the accuracy of the firm's complaint. Mr. Milner, for the Whitecross Company, said his company had felt compelled to bring the case before the Court and, although they did not press the case very severely, they wanted the man to be made an example of. The firm had had considerable difficulty of late owing to the men not obeying the rules and turning into their work regularly and punctually; but, in- stead, stopping off "on the drink." - "A Very Serious Charge." •

The President said that Baxter had pleaded guilty to what was, in these times, a very serious charge, seeing that the firm by which he was employed were engaged on important Government work. He was afraid it was not generally known that a man placed himself in a very serious position indeed by not attending to his duty. However, as that was, locally, the first case of its kind under the Munitions Act, and as the Man's employers had said that they did not press for a heavy penalty, he proposed to deal with the delinquent very lightly. But he must know, and so must other workers, that in eases like that the Court had power to fine up to £5O. He did not propose to impose penalty like that, however, but the man would have to pay a fine of ss. A day was allowed for payment of the penalty.

Not Quite Decided. The centenarian was being eagerly interviewed by reporters, and was asked among other things to what he attributed his long life and good health. "Well," the old man replied, slowly, "I'm not in a position to say right now. Yoh see, I've been bargaining with two or _three of them patent medicine concerns for a couple of weeks, but I ain't quite decided yet which I'll give a certificate to."

IN THE COURTS.

DISAPPEARING STUMPS. Mystery 01 Farm Fence Solved By Police Search.

Patrick McCarthy—a single man, of Callands Cottage, Burtonwood—was charged before Mr. W. Furness at an Occasional Court on Monday morning with stealing on September 26th, part of a rail fence, valued at one shilling, the property of Herbert Warburton, Callands Farm. It was stated that portions of the fence had been missing from time to time, fifty stumps altogether having been taken.,. The part which defendant was alleged to have stolen was found in his house in the course of a police search. The fine was £l, or 14 days' imprisonment.

"DISGUSTING IN HIGHEST DEGREE."

Councillor Roberts's Scathing ,Criti-

cisms of Woman's Drunkenness.

At Tuesday's Borough Police Court, before Councillor .H. Roberts (in the chair), W. Fur- ness, C. Jackson Holmes mid J. Rymer ,Young, Jane Samson, of 1; Getnett's=yard—wife of Arthur Samson, a soldier—was charged with having in Mersey-street the previous day been drunk in charge of her twelve months' old child.

The case having been proved, it transpired that the woman had five children. The Chief Constable suggested that if the case was adjourned Mr. Laws, the Police Co&t Missioner, might give the woman some assist- ance.

The Chairman (to prisoner): I don't know whether it is any use speaking to you, but really a case like this is disgusting in the highest degree. Here is your husband gone away; and yart, because you have more money than usual. spend it in this besotted way, and endanger the life of your youngest child. , 1 don't know whether you, are past all sense of shame. We will adjourn the case for four weeks to see if there is any reformatkon. I hope there will be !

BEER.. IN RAILWAY VAN.

Two Robbers Who "Might Have

Thought It Was Venegar."

At Tuesday's Borough Police Court, John Deegan and John Mulligan---sheeters, respec- tively, of John-street and 132, Forster-street-- were charged with having the previous day stolen a quantity of beer from a barrel in a van on the Cheshire Lines Railway at the Central Station. The Chief Constable said the men were seen by P.C. Joynson, of the Cheshire Lines Police, to go into a van on the railway in which they had no right whatever to be. Mulligan was observed to take the bung out of a barrel of beer, and the other man followed, and both had a drink. "I don't know whether they thought it was vinegar, or what excuse they have got," continued Mr. Nicholls, " but I would suggest an adjournment of seven days in order that the Cheshire Lines Committee might be. repre- sented."

The adjournment was granted, each prisoner being allowed bail with surety of £5.

JUVENILE SHOPBIIEAKERS.

Sunday Afternoon Raid On Three Premises.

Four ;boys—one aged 12, two aged 11, and the other aged ten—were before a special sitting of the Children's Court at Warrington on Wed- nesday afternoon, charged (a) with, on Sunday, breaking and entering the lock-up shop of the Argenta Dairy Co.• (b) with on the same day breaking and entering the bakehouse of Messrs. Thos. Astill and Co., and stealing there- from cakes to the value of 35.; and (c) with on the same day breaking and entering the lock-up workshop of the Velvet Cutters' Association, Cartwright-street, with-intent to steal. How The Boys Were Caught.

Detective Skelton stated that, in consequence of a report having been received as to the three premises being broken into, he and Detective Turner made inquiries ,on Tuesday, with the .tesult ,thitteertain 'information came to ,their knowledge, in consequence of which they inter- viewed one of the juvenile prisoners. Asked as to where he was on Sunday, he at once admitted that he had broken into the three shops mentioned in the charge, adding that the other three prisoners were in his company. He was taken to the Police Station and witness and his companion then went in search of the other three lads, who were soon found, and were also, taken to the station: They, too,. admitted the offence, adding that a number of younger boys —too Young to proceeoragainst—were with them at the time. Whit in the Argenta Co.'s shop the lads 'threw butter all over the plkce; in fact, they even took some of it from the ft.op and threw it into the backyard. On the premises they found some paint, and with it they ,had painted a door and a number of butter plattei-s, &c. The place was turned upside down, and a great deal of damage had been done. The bakehouse was entered by one of the lads climbing through the window and then opening the door and letting the others in. Entry was also obtained to the velvet-cutting shop by a.uans of a window.

Once in the shop the prisoners got the cutting- knive's and apparently had a try at cutting •,,the pile, the result' 'being 'that • they made several holes in a length of material. They also spoiled a quantity of other velvet by walking over it with their muddy boots. In consequence of the /damage they did' in the velvet-cutting sin sreral WOMB, had had to lose a day's work, and, consequently, a day's pay. The damage in this shop alone was put at £4, it being stated that that was the Moat modest possible estimate.. Further questioned the prisoners admitted to witness and his colleague that ,they had been into the premises named in the -charge on 'other,occaions,•,evecially Detective Turner gavo".corteboratiire evidence. The lads were each ardeiett six- strokes- of .the birch-rod, and were cautioned that if they came before the Court again they would be sent to reformatory schools.

RYLANDS'S RECS. v. ST. THOMAS'. Well-Fought Game Ends in Draw.

Though, of course, it bore no comparison to the crowds which were wont in days of peace to flock to Wilderspool, there was nevertheless quite a pleasing-sized " gate " at the well-known football-ground on Saturday, when a couple of Intermediate League matches were decided. Tho spectatorate was in the main made up of lads and youths, but one noticed hero and there the face of an old football enthusiast.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
Admirably Matched Opponents.

The ,first of the two matches was between Rylands's ,Recs. and St. Thomas', Stockton Heath. It was an interesting gaMenroughout, the teams being admirably matched and giving a spirited display of the science of the game. Early on the " Hefahens " were called upon to defend? the Recs, putting in some telling work and coming near to scoring before the game was many minutes -oldi A fine clearance by the Thomas' full-back' brought easement, but only for a short time; for Rylands's were soon press- ing again and the ball was forced over the line by Lee, who, however, failed to touch down. This narrow shave made the " Saints " more wary, and they managed to confine play to somewhere about midfield for a considerable time, until, seeing their opportunity and seizing it, their left-wilugers forced the ball up the field and one of them' nipped over the line, only to be ruled off-side. Just before half-time, which arrived . with a blank Score sheet, there was . , some, desperate scrummaging in dangeroug proximity to Rylands's line, but danger was ;averted tn. the nick of time.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
'Posperate Struggle for Points.

The second half opened with a 'first-class 'tussle near the Stockton Heath line—a ding-dong piece of work that won the unmistakable approbation of the onlookers. Rylawls'i I.,ave plenty of evidence that they meant business this half, and the " Heathens " had plenty of opportunities of exhibiting their soundness as defenders. For the second time in the game the ball was got over the line, but the try was not completed, the leather Trolling dead. A visit paid by the " Heathens " to Rylands's goal promised fair, but it did not get beyond the promising stage, for at the crucial moment Archer effected a clearance with a beautiful _drive. The Stockton Heath men were, soon back, however, and all but rushed the ball over twice in quick succession, and they were making a third desperate attempt when Archer again distinguished himself by sending the ball into touch about a third of the way down the field. During the last few minutes both teams strove desperately for points, but none came, and the game ended in a scoreless draw, which result was, perhaps, just about representative of the respective merits of the combatants.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
Over Eager.

The team p were evenly matched, and good play was spoiled :a few times by over eagerness. The play of the St. Thomas' custodian was a feature. of the game. Time after time he cleared his lines in grand style. Archer, too, cleared his lines in grand style. Rylands's were best repie- rented by Archer, Ratcliffe and Dumbell, while the visitors' full-back was the saviour of his side.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====

PAROCHIAL OLD BOYS v. WHITECROSS. Works' Lads Suffer Overwhelming Defeat.

Victoria Park was on Saturday afternoon the scene of an extraordinarily one-sided Inter- mediate League game, the Parochial Old Boys' team administering defeat to the Whitecross representatives by the crushing. margin of 60 points to nil. The victors' points were, made up of 16 tries and 6 goals. Twenty Points in the First Half - _

6 The game was too hopelessly one-sided to be of any great interest. ,The Old Boys won the • toss and elected ‘to start with the breeze— quite a helpful asset, by the way—at their backs. They soon made it pretty evident that they were altogether too good for the Whitecross lads, and at half-time they enjoyed a twenty point lead.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
And Forty in the Second.

Despite the fact that the wind was in their teeth in the second half, the Parochial lads maintained the fast pace which they had ex- hibited in the opening' moiety, and, as the phrase goes, they were "all over" their opponents. who, however, stuck it doggedly. Another forty points were totted up by the Old Boys 'ere the final whistle blew.

The scorers during the match were :—Tries: IL Breas (3), W. Brandwood (3). B. Brown (3), D. Gibbs (2). W. Broadhurst, H. Malkin, H. Curtis, J. Litherland and S. Stretford (1 each); goals: Brown (4) and Wilcocks (2). The Conquering Thirteen.

EMMANUEL INSTITUTE v. SANKEY ATHLETIC.

Played at Victoria Park. The visitors won the toss, opened briskly, and soon became dan- gerous. The home team quickly discovered that Sankey take a lot of holding. After several exciting bits of work in close proximity to the visitors' goal, the home team forced a corner. The resultant kick was well placed, but it came to nothing. The home team continued to have the best of play, and-after 20 minutes' play Wm. Eaton opened the scoring with a fine "'AMC Sankey made several fine bursts, but found the home backs too good for them. They were feed- ing the forwards well, but could not again penetrate the visitors' defence. Sankey made a good rally and were unlucky not to equalise. W. Taylor hitting the croiiilaar. Half-time:— Emmanuel, 1 goal; Sankey Athletic, 0. The second half became more evenly con- tested and Sankey were showing better form. Both goalkeepers were tested in turn, but both kept out shot after shot. Emmanuel was for a time put on the defensive. After about 25 minutes' play in the second half Sankey for- wards combined well together, and after a hot melee in the home goalmouth, Kenyon secured, and with a fast shot which was too strong for Knight, the home goalkeeper, scored the equal- ising goal. Aroused by this, both sides were playing in determined style, but neither could make any headway. Towards the end both teams held their own. When the whistle sounded the genie stood drawn: one goal each; proving a reflex of a good clear, game. The pick of Sankey were J. Hands, G. Kenyon and W. Taylor, while Crozier and Oakes played well for Emmanuel.

CAIRO-STREET UNITARIANS V. PENKETH

SCHOOL.

Cairo-street Unitarians were in opposition to the Friends' School, on Saturday, at Penketh. Losing the toss, the visitors kicked-off and soon made tracks for the horn:: goal. They kept up the pressure for some time, but Brown relieved the home team. Penketh then attacked the visitors' goal, but were easily repulsed, and some pretty combination by the Unitarians ended in G. Bailey opening the score. Shortly afterwards Chatterton added a second. The Unitarians slackened down after this dual success, and taking advantage, the School scored through Mr. Baker., They continued to attack, and faulty play by the visiting backs enabled Mr. Baker to equalise. This reverse roused the visitors and they assailed the home goal, which had several narrow escapes. Half-time:— Unitarians, 2 goals; Frienas', 2 goals. On resuming the. Unitarians took play to the home quarters, and a pretty pass from the right-wing was converted by Chatterton. Encouraged by this goal, the visitors kept up the pace, and Hayes scored a fourth, Cairo- street winning by 4 goals to 2 goals. For the Unitarians .J. 'Whittaker and G. Bailey made a capital right-wing, while A. Ellesraore played his usual sound game. Mr. Brunyee and Mr. Baker were the prominent players on the school team.

FARM PIND GARDEN.

"SCHEDULE B."

It seems clear that• the proportionate increase in taxation is heavier upon the farmer than upon any other section of the community so far as the payment of income tax is concerned, says " Farm and Home."

Hitherto he, has been in a somewhat favoured position, the explanation of which it is hardly necessary at this stage to enter upon. One reason no doubt has tedntthe difficulty of ascer- taining the actual profits of the farmer owing to the methods in which the business is carried out, but however this may be, he has hitherto been dealt with on. the free and easy assumption that his profits from farming equal one-third of his annual rent. On this basis a, man whose rent is not more than ..{4BO ,a year has of late been exempt, and one who paid .1600 was assumed to be in receipt of £2OO a year, had an exemption of ..1.160, and paid on £4O. On the poundage of is. 6d. his total income-tax pay- ment would be .1.3. Under the new proposals his income will be reckoned at £6OO, and he will be allowed an abatement of .1100, so that his assessment will be on .1500, which, at the in- creased rate of (roughly) 2s. Id. in the .1, will mean an income-tax payment of £52 10s., or .mare- tham.saventeen.tirnes as much-as before: Fortunately for him, and for 'farmers- on a smaller scale, there still remains the option of electing to he assessed under Schedule D—that is on the actual profit?. . . . It will, there- fore, be the business of each to considef-whether he should not now claim the right to be placed n7rder Schedule- D. In. that ellt,e, he must be prepared to satisfy the assessors as to his actual profits, and farmers as a body, unless they are prepared to suffer, will have to give more atten- tion to account keeping than they have hitherto been supposed to devote to it.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE HOME-GROWN FOOD.

A memoranduiiron "The Maintenance or In- crease of Food Supplies in the United King- dom," which has been specially prepared by the committee of the Farmers' Club to elucidate the position of the agricultural community in view of the Government's decision not to fix a mini- mum price.for wheat has been ptgAished. This decision was conveyed; to a conferenco repre- sentative of all the great agricultural bodies, held at the. House of Lords on August 26th, by Lord Selborne, President of the Board of Agri- culture, who intimated that the Government did not propose to adopt the recommendations of the Interim Report of Lord Miler's Committee in this sense.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
Ploughing up Grass.

The question of ploughing up grass, says the memorandum, at the present time must be de- cided by each individuar farmer. If it is likely to benefit the pocket of the individual farmer it will, at this juncture, benefit the State. But much bad and undrained land is safer in grass than in arable. The same consideration does not necessarily apply to much land which has been laid down, or has tumbled down to grass since 1879. It is not anticipated that owners would object to relaxation of restrictive covenants as to breaking up where necessary.

The Avoidance of Waste. The memoranduha admits that in normal times there is a good deal of waste in agricul- ture, and that it behoves farmers to avoid.waste, both commissive and permissive, during the war. The biggest t waste in agriculture, the committee Write, is caused by weeds. As a rule, weeds are permitted by bad farmers only, and a determined attempt, notwithstanding all diffi- culties, should be made to get rid of the weeds ,so that the yield per acre of the crops we grow may attain the highest standard. There are plenty of good farmers who wish to extend their operations, and it is almost the duty of land- lords and agents to see that those who cannot farm their land properly should make room ?or those who can. At any rate, bad farmers should be warned of the risk they run.

IMMUNITY FROM SWINE FEVER. An Alternative To Wholesale Slaughter.

In view of the need fer husbanding the food supply of the country, the Board of Agriculture and Fisheries decided last August that the slaughter of pigs for the purpose merely of reducing the -risk,of the spread of swine fever should be resorted to as little as possible, and that the slaughter of breeding sows or partially matured animals purely as a matter of pre- caution should be avoided. The Board has therefore relied on isolation combined with the slaughter .of all swine distinctly sick of swine fever at the time of diagnosis. Meanwhile, investigations into tho efficacy of the injection of anti-swine-fever serum, which were being carried out before the war began, have been continued, and the results obtained, together with the evidence accumulated as to its use in other countries, show that if proper pro. cautions are taken immunity from swine fever can be established by serum treatment. The Board are now in a position to offer serum treatment of pigs free of cost in the early stages of an outbreak of swine fever, and -they wish to impress on all owners that to obtain the best results from this treatment notification of the suspected existence of swine fever on their premises should be made at the earliest possible date. Any delay in notifying not only renders the owner liable •to prosecution for failing to comply with the provisions of Article 1 (1) of the Swine Fever Order of 1908, but also tends to increase his loss owing. to more pigs becoming affected before serum treatment is carried out.

SHOULD MORE POTATOES BE GROWN? An extra million tons of potatoes would be an

immense asset to the country, for if they were not all wanted for human food, they would help to keep bread prices down, and the surplus would all be converted into pork. We have often argued that good ware potatoes werb worth 50s. to 60s. per ton for pig food, and that W:IN when pork was worth 2d. per lb. less than it is now. Potatoes are also excellent food for inikh cows in, winter,. and for quickly converting the rougher classes of cattle into second-clam beef. But if farmers have a fine crop of potatoes, they must let it go freely into consumption, and net hold it over as a speculation until next year'is new .crop.. is -coming ,in, and then howl for Government intervention to make the Army and Navy provide the market which their own folly has missed.--'f. Agricultural Economirt and .Horticultural Review."

IN THE GARDEN.

ZONAL PELARGONiUMS.

Those who wish to preserve any of these fer another season should lose no time in liftins, for, if the tiptt, of the growths- are injured `though but slightly by frost, in all-probability the shoots will continne tit decay until the whole plant has perished. After lifting, priek off all the large leaves (for thes&only die away and very often rot the stem) and put into the smallest pots the roots can. be got into. Do /HA cut any of the shoots away, and onky trim off the longest of the roots. After potting, give a-glood soaking of water, and stand them in a frame, where they can be kept close for a few days. when they may be given plenty'of air and kept rather on the dry side at the roots. During the winter they should be kept on shelves near the glass in a temperature of about 4.5 degrees. These plants, if all goes well, will yield a fine lot of;cuttings -in the spring and bloom during the following season far more freely than one- year-old plants.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0255.txt | included=true | included =====
QUINCES.

Now that the planting season will soon be with us, it may be well to call attention to the dual beauty of the quince—when in flower in spring. and when bearing its handsome fruits, Bo favoured by the housewife for jelly or to flavour apple tarts. The value of the quince is empha- sised by the fact that it will thrive in situations quite unsuitable for the majority of fruit trees. By pond margins and in marshy and swampy places the quince is at home. The pea-r-ehaped variety is, I think, the best.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0256.txt | included=true | included =====
APPLE GRENADIER.

This should receive attention on account of its free cropping and its good cooking qualities. it belongs to the Codlin section, and does well in either bush or cordon form. It may also bo grown as a standard, but the fruits, owing to their large size, are liable to injury when trees of it are grown in this form. For the amateur's and cottager's garden it is well suited, as it comes early into bearing, the fruits from Vl:mg trees being very large and handsome..

===== 0002601_19151002_art0257.txt | included=true | included =====
OUTDOOR TOMATOES.

Those who have any movable lights may profit- ably utilise them to assist in the ripening of the fruit by fixing them in a slanting position te the wall so as to keep the tomatoes dry. If kept dry the fruits will continue to swell for some time yet, and as the dry autumn has in some degree made amends for the wet summer, every ray of sunshine should be utilised to the utmost by the use of glass, so as to ripen off as inuell of the late fruit as possible.—Wick in Faint and Home."

===== 0002601_19151002_art0258.txt | included=true | included =====
Be Your Own Minister of Munitions!

Good food, well digested, is as essential to your physical and mental efficiency as are shells and machine guns to the success of our gallant army. , The enemies of your body— and- they are, legion—have you at a great disadvantage unless in times of stress" and strain, when your powers of resistance are -taxed to the utmost, you can fall back on ample reserves of strength, and vitality, built up by the thorough digestion of the food you eat. Indigestion and sluggish action of the liver and bowels—these are your labour troubles, and they call for sympathetic, well- considered treatment. Don't' try to force them back to work by drastic rneasures—pick-me- ups, violent purgatives and the like for along that way lies open rebellion. Coax and help:them back to willing healthy activity and efficiency by giving them the gentle bracing -help of a good digestive tonic and liver invigorator, such as Mother Seigel's Syrup. Then note how, day by day, your supply of strength and vital energy increases. until at length it is fully equal to every demand that is made upon it.

A Memory Test. Charlie: "Don't you remember? It was that day you borrowed five shillings of me." ;lack (hastily): "I don't recollect anything of the sort." Charlie: "But you paid it back Neat week." Jack: "Oh, yes; I remember that pey- fectly."

Chaplain's Kindly Note.

A chaplain writing for Private Hawthorn, says:—

"I am sorry to tell you that I have been very badly wounded with a bullet in the back._ It has, unfortunately, hit my spine. ant hoping for the best' as I am being very well eared for here. I will let you know how Igo on, but meanwhile try not to worry."
