===== 0002601_19151002_art0270.txt | included=true | included =====
Twenty Army and Navy Relatives.

Thirteen months ago he enlisted, and it is how three Months since he went abroad' with cheer- ful spirit. It is interesting to note that Private Granger has no less than twenty relatives in the Army

At 11.30 in the morning all the troops in the town will assemble opposite the Town Hall, when the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) will address to them a few words of encouragement and thanks. Five minute's, or so, later the 12th South Lancs. Regiment—the Bantams— headed by the band of the 2nd South Lancs. Regiment, will march to Orford Barracks by a devious route, which will include Sankey- street, Liverpool-road, Lovely-lane, Bewsey- street, Winwick-street. Lythgoes-lane, Marsh House-lane, Sze. At the same .time the mem- bers of the 4th South Lancs. Regiment in the town, headed by the 2/4th's band, will make their way to the 'Drill Hall, via Buttermarket- street, Dial-street, School Brow, Church-street, Howley-lane, Mersey-street, Bridge-street and Sankey-street, Sm. At 1.30 the 4th South Lancs. will parade at the Drill Hall, and headed by the 2/4th's band, will march to the Wilderspool Football Ground, arriving there at 2.30. At 1.50 the Bantams, with the 2nd South Lancs. Band lead- ing, will set out for the Football Ground from the Barracks, reaching their destination at 2.30. _ _

The gymkhana and sports on the Football Ground will commence at 2.30 prompt, and at the dose the prizes will be presented by the Mayoress (Mrs. Peter Peacock). A full after- noon's Sport has been arranged, the pro- gramme including many specially • attractive items, such as a Victoria Cross race, a Munition Workers' race, a Boy Scouts race, and races for ,soldiers' boys and girls; in additon to the more purely regimental and athletic events. In the evening—at 8 p.m.—will come the tattoo, which will take place in front of the Town Hall. to which place the bands will march—that of the 2/4th South Lancs. with the members of the 4th South Lancs. carrying torches, from the Drill Hall, and that of the 2nd South Lanes., with the Bantams, from the Barracks. Features of the tattoo will be a "display of fiery figures" by the Bantams, a short speech by the Mayor, and a performance Of Tschaikowsky's famous overture. "1812," by the combined bands, assisted by firing parties. On Sunday afternoon, at 3 o'clock, a drum- head service will be held in front of the Town 'Tall. The Mayor and Corporation will be in attendance, and , in addition to the troops quartered in the town. the Civilian Defence Corps, the Boy Scouts, the Cadet Corps and the Church Lads' Brigade will parade. The Rev. Canon Willis (the rector of Warrington) will give the address. and the singing will be led by the Parish •Church Choir and the band of the 2nd South Lancs.

===== 0002601_19151002_art0272.txt | included=true | included =====
TO-DAY'S IVI I LITARY GYMKHANA.

Full Arrangements For The Big Recruiting Attraction.

To-day another recruiting effort is to be put forth in connection with the Prince of Wales Volunteers, .South Lancs. Regiment, but this time it will not take the form of fervid Oratory at the street corners and in other public places, but will be in the guise of a parade and route marches, a, grand military gymkhana and a night tattoo, in which two military bands will take part, the idea being this time 'to appeal through the khaki-clad lads themselves to those who are not yet in khaki, but who, failing sufficient excuse, should make good the omission. _ _ .

===== 0002601_19151002_art0273.txt | included=true | included =====
OVER TIIE FOOTLIGHTS.

Call To Arms Sounded In Theatres. At. ;the second performance of "The Belle Of New York at, the Royal Court Theatre on Monday night, the curtain had just dropped on the sprightly ensemble with which the first act :closes, when there stepped before it Capt. Erie : Fairclough, who made a brief appeal to the young men in the audience seriously to put the question to themselves: "Am I doing my duty to my' country by not joining up ?' "

===== 0002601_19151002_art0274.txt | included=true | included =====
WEST LANCS. BIG PART.

In the victory which had been won by the Allies during the Week-end, the Captain said a battalion of West Lancashire men .had taken a big part. For the last month or six weeks the men of that battalion had been on what was known as pioneer work. ' In other words they had been consolidating the pOsitikns .*on from the Germans-=-(applause)-,:and the bomb throwers and machine gunners had been at- tached to that battalion, whilst they had been doing their Work. He mentioned that matter just to show what valuable service some Lanca- shire men had been rendering to .their country, and because he wanted to say to every young man in the audience who was not on munitions work and who in his heart of hearts knew that 'he was fit and was a proper person to join the Army.; -""Go home to-night and consider the natter, reealling the fact that thousands bf the men of Warrington-L-among, mayhaps, you have a brother, .an uncle, a cousin!—have been out at the Front for months fighting for you; and .then ask yourself whether ,you do not Con- sider that your place is by their side, helping them in their great and *glorious task. * (Hear, hear.) _ _ .

===== 0002601_19151002_art0275.txt | included=true | included =====
IS IT NOT TIME ----?

"For,monthEl—in the case or Warrington's own Territorials, since last February—your relatives, friends and workmates, who have crossed over to France, have rarely, if ever, been out of the range of the German guns, and have rarely known what it. is .to have a decent meal or to sleep in a comfortable bed. All this and much more they 'have been enduring for you; is it not time some more of you went • out and lent them a helping hand.? I ask all the fit young men whether they, conscientiously, feel, that they are doing right to let matters go on is they have been going and not to go out and 'back up -their relatives and friends? That is .the question I want to put before you. • It is for you to frame the answer!" The Captain's brief remarks were warmly applauded.' ,

TELLING WILD WEST SKETCH AT THE HIPPODROME.

Chief among the many attractions which this week's programme at the Palace and Hippo- drome affords is the performance by Mr. John F. Traynor and his company of a . forcibly written and appealing tabloid:drama ,of "the wild and woolly i West "—a trifle which is as well acted as it s written, and which is a great favourite at every performance. In quite another Vein. and, of its ordei; clever and at- tractive, is the turn provided by Gipsy Woolf and her "Futurist Dancing Girls," whilst Shirley and Ransome fairly "fetch the house" twice nightly with their recruiting sketch, "Ono of Kitchener's "—a playlet that is calculated to put the finishing touch to the speeches of the recruiters, and the "appeals of the recruiting posters. There are several other decidedly good

thing's in what is quite one of the best variety programmes ever vouchsafed to Warrington, in- cluded among them being the clever;dancing of Little Lucie Linda; the marvellous equilibristic work of Hector and Lolletta, and the funny songs of Harry Taft and Billy Rogers. Alto- gether the show is quite one of the best of the many good ones recently submitted.

" Tromboneers " and Physical Culturists Next Week.

What promises to be a specially attractive bill for next week is headed by Michael Richardina Troope, in what is billed as "the greatest dis- play of physical culture of the present time." Sharp's Tromboneers—" the original trombone band "—should afford a novel turn, and Murray Leonard and company in "The Fool "—" a rip roaring sketch "—guarantees side-splitting laughter. Other turns booked include The Avolos (wonderful double-somersault throwers), Miss Josephine Richards and Co. in "A Baby's Shoe," and Gordon and Lassie, "the Scottish Pbaritone and a pianist."

===== 0002601_19151002_art0279.txt | included=true | included =====
" RUPERT OF 11ENTZAU " AT STAR KINEMA HOUSE.

This week at the Star Kinema the first three days were occupied by the following pro- grFame "The Franco-African Cavalry At The Front," a well-filmed military item. To last week's- "Prisoner Of •Zenda," "Rupert Of Hentzau " • was the sequel, and of everybody fulfilled the fondest expectations. The story being taken from Anthony Hope's famous novel, a series of brilliant and forceful scenes were woven round one of the most daring plots in modern fiction. The culmination—which, when a blank wall appeared to have been reached, was unexpeqedly hastened and the problem thereby solved by ." Fate," was. majestio and moving. One could wish there were more ‘Zende, films on the market. " Pathes' Animated Gazette" gave a variety of war incidents in all parts, the NOrthern Edition depicting work of the Volunteers: A' really good " comic " was "/Wanted, A Chaperone; in the absence of Chaplin the Great it left little to be desired. To-day's bills of fare warrant a visit. Therein the film principal is ".Whose Diggeth A Pit," .1 powerful drama with a powerful moral. ,

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

OAVEN-DAVIES.

WEAVER-MILLS.

The marriage took place on Tuesday afternoo* at Our Lady's Church, Latchford, of John Deals Weaver. son of Mr. and Mrs. Weaver, DerbY Place. Hoole, Chester. and Ethel Mills, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Mills, tobacconist, Mersel- street. The Rev. Father Tallon performed till ceramony. The bride was attired in ivory corded silk,, with tulle ,veil and orange blossom,. and carried a sheaf of lilies. The bridesmaids were the Misses Hilda and Lily Mills, sisters of the bride, and the duties of best man were car- ried out by Mr. Harold Mills, brother of th• bride. The honeynioon is being spent at Col- wyn Bay. A number of handsome presents wer• received.

WILDU-WOOLLEY. At the Bold-street Wesleyan Church tll, marriage was solemnized between Mr. Harold K. Wilde, third son of Mr. and Mrs. Tone Wilde, of 96, Hood-lane, and Lillie, third daughter of the late Mr. James T. Woolley and Mrs Woolley, of 21, Thynne-street. The officiating minister was the Rev. P. Lifson. A reception was held and breakfast was serves at the bride's residence. Afterwards the hapPY couple left for New Brighton, where the honey- moon is being spent. Many valuable and use- ful presents were received, amongst them being two from the Sunday School Class, which the bride has attended for some years.

THE LATE MR. A't ILIA %NI LONG.

In Memoriam.

"Sacred to the memory of William Long. born November sth. 1834, died February 13th, 1915. He worshipped for many years in this chapel, following the example of a long line of ancestors, who were amongst the earliest members of the congregation. He was true te his convictions, and faithful in the dis- charge of all his public duties. 'The steps f a good

man are ordered by the Lord.'"

Thus reads the inscription on a brass memorial tablet—the • work of Messrs. T. Williams' and Son—vhich has, been placed in the Cairo-street Unitarian Church to thit memory of the late Mr. William Long, J.P., of Gra,ppenhall, who for many years was associated with the church. The tablet has been erected by the deceased gentleman's family, and it was unveiled without ceremony.

COUNTY PALATINE OF• CHESTER. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that the next GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER will be holden at the COURT- HOUSE, KNUTSFORD; in and for the said County, on MONDAY, the 18th day of OCTOBER, 1915, at 10.30 o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury -will be empannelled.. • The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Altrincham, Buck- low, Dukinfield, Middlewioli, Sandbach, 'Congle- ton, Northwich, Prestbury, and Stockport, form- ing the Knutsford district, and the trials of-all prisoners committed from such Divisions. will take place at such Sessions. , The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knutsford District must be in attendance at the COURT HOUSE, KNUTS- FORD, at the opening of the Court on MONDAY, the 18th day of OCTOBER NEXT, at 10.30 o'clock in the Forenoon. THE COURT will be Adjourned to the CASTLE OF CHESTER on WEDNESDAY, the 20th day of OCTOBER, 1915; at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when another Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Broxten, Chester Castle, Daresbury, Eddisbury, Nantwich, Run- corn, Winsford, and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners eom- mitted from the last-mentioned Divisionp, will take place at suoh Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries- for the Chester District must be in attendance at' the CASTLE OF CHESTER at the opening of the Court on WEDNESDAY, the 20th day of OCTOBER, 1915, at Eleven o'clock in the Foremen: , REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County Palatine of Chester. Chester,, 28th September, 1915.

SECOND-LIEUT. D. STEW.IIt T- SMITH. First Man Of His Battalion To Enter -German Trenches. Second-Lieutenant Dudley Stewart-Smith, of the 2nd Battalion Black Watch—and elder son of Vice-Chancellor Stewart-Smith, K.C., of Lymm Hall, Cheshire—participated on Saturday in the great advance, and now lies in the Duchess •of ,Westminster's hospital in France, gassed and wounded in the head, arm and hand., It was a most gallant part which be played. .Charging with his battalion, he was the first man to enter the German trenches, and thus earns for Lymm another distinction in the Euro- pean War. After being wounded he continued fighting for six hours. Twice Previously Wounded. Aged 20 years, Second-Lieutenant Stewart- Smith was first educated at Winchester, and now

LOSSES TO LOCAL CATHOLICISM. FATHER SAA'ARBRECK• DEAD. Rector Of New St. Benedict's ; Formerly Of St. Alban's. . At a time when already Warrington Cotholic- ism is losing two of its stalwarts by transfer- ence, it has sustained a further and unexpected loss. • The Rev. Father Thomas 'Oswald 'Swat.- breck, we regret to announce, passed away with tragic suddenness at two o'clock on Thursday afternoon from pneumonia. It it only a week ago this morning since he took to his bed and came uncle!' the' care of Dr. McKee. His age was about 50 years. , The funeral will take place on Monday after- neon at the Warrington Cemetery. The preceding service will be conducted in St. Benedict's Church at 11.15, and the funeral cor- tege should reach the graveside about noon. One of the twenty-two children of the latzb Mr. and Mrs. Charles McCartney Swarbrenk, of Thirsk, Yorkshire, Father Swarbreck formerly held the procuratorship of Ampleforth College.

Postcard Tells 07 Wounding Of Bomb Thrower. Mrs. Smith, of Green-street, Warrington, on Wednesday morning learned that her son, Corporal William Smith, a bomb thrower at- tached to the Warrington Territorials, had been wound whilst in action. A Red Cress postcard, posted from a Chichester 'Hospital, conveyed the intelligence, but gave no deails. Another Defender From Crosfield's. Corporal Smith, who is twenty years of age, as a bey attended the 'Holy Trinity Day School. Prior to volunteering fer active service , —he had been in the Tr,rritoriA-; three ears before the outbreak of' war—he was employed in the export department of Messrs. J. Cros- field and Son's Soap Works; indeed, he had been employed there ever since leaving school. He went to the front on February 13th. . Corporal Smith is a married man, and he and his, wife make their home with his mother. For three years Corporal Smith played foot- ball for the Orford St. John's Club. OTHER REGIMENTS. PRIVATE 'JOIN BROADHURST. Vernon's Tannery Employe AVounded In Shoulder. . Private John Broadhurst, of 31, Wellington- street, an employe at Messrs. Vernon's Tan- nery, Mersey-street, who is in a Highland regi- ment, was wounded in the great battle on Satur- day., A letter from the chaplain in the West Riding Clearing Casualty Station, France, says Private Broadhurst was hit in the shoulder with shrapnel. "I 'am confident he will get on all right—he was quite bright in spite. of it all," is the chaplain's cheery .closing remark. A Patriotic Family. Private John Broadhurst joined the colours on September 3rd, 1914, when he was employed at Messrs. Vernon's Tannery, Mersey-street. Son of Mr. Joseph Bradshaw (a labourer for Messrs. Crosfield), and Mrs. Bradshaw, the Private is aged 24 and unmarried. Born at Warrington, he attended as a lad the National School, afterwards going to work for Mr. Gill. butcher, Buttermarket-street, and more recently on similar work for Mr. Everall, Crossley- street. In the 2nd. South I ancaslii res he enlisted just thirteen months ago, and it is now vight weeks since he lett for Prance. KILLED. CORPORAL DICK ROBERTS. "Died Doing His Duty Bravely," Says Officer!' • Through the medium of two letters, one from a comrade, and the other from the Major of his regiment, Mrs. Roberts, of 5, School-street, Latchford, has received news that her husband, Corporal Dick Roberts. of the Bth Battalion of the King's Royal Rifles, has been killed in action. Killed Instantly and Felt Nothing. The letter from -Major C. Seymour ran as follows:—" It is with very deep regret that I have to inform you that your husband, torporal Roberts, was killed in the, trenches by a shell on September 22nd. He was killed instantly and could have felt nothing. He was buried in a little graveyard being our lines. Your husband had only lately come to my company, and he died doing his duty bravely and well. PRIVATE JAMES BERRY. Former Warrington Footballer Among The Slain. Writing to a friend from "The Trenches," under date September 9th, Priirate P. Cliffe, of the Rifle Brigade, said:— • "1 am sorry to say that we have this week lost one of the Vitrrington chaps from our battalion. His name was Berry and I. believe he used to play with the old town football club. He was a very nice, quiet chap I saw them digging his grave, but I could not get to see him buried." Serving with the Rifle Brigade was Private James Berry, of Albert-terrace, Howley, and as he was an old.Warrington footballer of some note there would seem to be but little doubt that the • writer referred to him, .though some fortnight went by .before the PrivAte's wife received any further tidings concerning her husband. Volunteered At 47! Private James Berry joined the Rifle Brigade soon after the,outbreak- of war—in September

port vessel bound for England. The ship's chaplain also writes to Mr. Bailey that he buried the Private at sea; his arm had been amputated, but it was so badly shattered that had the operation not been performed he could hardly have lived. Son of the late Mr. Napoleon Bailey (black- smith, of Albert-terrace), Private John Bailey was .bornvin Wfirringbop 34 years ago, and as a lad attendeethe National School. Upon leav- ing, he entered in turn the employ of various local tanners, and also spent some time with the Firth Company. More recently he had worked at the Corporation Electricity Department. After the watkoutbreak he joined the 6th South Lancashires, 'and - lest visited his home during Christmas leave. Thence he returned to train- ing at Blackdown Camp until being drafted to the Dardanelles in early April.

recently went to live at Sheffield, has received intimation that her husband, Private Edward Vanes, dird from dysentery, on the, 28th August, whilst being brought on board a hos- pital ship from the Dardanelles, after taking part in the fighting there, along with his regi- ment, the sth Battalion of the- Connaught Rangers. / His First And Last Letter From The Front. The first and, as it . proved to be, the last letter which Mrs. Vanes received from her hus- band since he went on foreign service was written two months ago, and in it he said he was then in the best of health, and his regi- ment were looking forward to landing at their destination. Private Vanes, who was 35 years of ago,,,was, whilst residing in Warrington, employed by Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, Ltd. He enlisted -in September of last year. Fond of angling, he was a member of a local fishing club. Five Relatives Serving. Private Vane's brother and three brothers-in- law are serving in the Army. whilst Corporal John Rose, who recently died of wounds, was another of his brothers-in-law. Comrade Says Private Vane Was Wounded. Mrs. Vane yesterday received a letter from Private P. Hawley saying that Private Vane was reported wounded. "We made our first attack," he says, "Ted and Me and a few others. I did not notice him drop, as it was like raining through smoke and we had not time to look round. But next morning, when the roll was called, Ted was put down as wounded. His wound was very bad. . . . He has done his duty. Thank God there are fellows like him to do it. He was always a good chap and we liked him very muck. It hurt me when I heard of his death."

ly an Englishwoman in Germany and addressed l-kn Own writer, who answers it in the second THE ANSWER. (By an Englishwoman in Her Own Country). In the deep woods of green England There are bluebells of such blue That your heart might stop its beating, Could I send one bloom for greeting From oui. beloved land, One bloom for you. In the hedgerows of your England The dog rose softly lies, And the honeysuckle creeping Would- move you in your weeping To lift a heavy hand To dry your eyes. The children in our England • Are laughing in the lane; They have heard the ctickno calling And summer raindrops falling Great hopes we planned, Dear, some remain. High freedom lives in England, Though we dreamed that she was dead; We found her in the fighting, Mid the shouting and the smiting, That you in alien land May lift your head. England, our holy England, Dear Mother of me and mine, Still for liberty is pleading, Through her very heart is bleeding And running in the sand. Blood, spilt like wine. Yet she is gay, our England, Her back no sorrows bend, Her warm arms ever lolding, Her children in their holding. Erect and firm to stand, Abides the end. —Frances Chesterto

A single man, aged 22, the Sapper attended St. James' Church—of the choir of which he is a Member: PRIVATE GEORGE NEWALL. Wounded A Second Time In His Third Battle. Private George Newall, of the Rifle Brigade, is a son of Mrs. Newall, of 18, Miller-street; and on Thursday morning that lady received a Red Cross card saying that he had just arrived at Cottesbrooke Hospital, Northampton. From him since, there has also arrived a letter say- ing he is very comfortable and will write later on to tell more about how he was wounded. This is the third battle he has been through

RIFLEMAN G. NEWALL Wounded.

and does not mark the first time Private Newall has figured in the casualty list; for in the battle of Neuve Chapelle he was •wounded in the shoulder—as we then reported. 116 had only been out in France since January, having enlisted four months previdusly. Aged twenty-three, unmarried, and a native of Warrington, Private Newall was educated at St. James' Schools, and now belongs to St. James' Church. Previous to enlisting he was employed by Messrs. Richmond as a moulder. At one time he belonged to the Junior Con- servative Club. Two .elder brothers of his are with the colours—lsaac, of the R.A.M.C., and William, of the Rifle Brigade. • • PRIVATE ALBERT HAWTHORN. Badly Wounded In The Back By • Bullet. Driver Isaiah Elwell, 172, Liverpool-road, who is in the Royal Field Artillery, recently wrote to his wife saying that he was about to set sail from Egypt for the fighting area. Ile added that the next time she heard'from him he would be in the firing line; but on Wednesday she

DRIVER ISAIAH ELWELL. Died. received a note from the War Office, accom- panied by an expression .0 sympathy from the King, saying that her 'husband had died of disease at a place not stated. Driver Elwell is the brother of Mr. Joseph Elwell. He was 38 years of age, and leaves a widow and three children, the oldest of whom is fourteen years of age. lila was very fond of home -life, and did litie outside work except to assist the Liberal party .at election times. At the outbreak of war he was working for Messrs. Monks. Hall and Co., and he enlisted in the first month. SHOT WHILE ASLEEP. Private Robert Wakefield's Unique Experience. Private Robert Wakefield, of the Warrington Territorials, brother of Mrs. Isaiah Elwell, who has this week lost her husband, was wounded in the knee last Saturday week—a week before the great advance began. The casualty occurred so early as five o'clock in the morning, and while Private Wakefield was asleep. Somehow a bullet penetrated his

"Patten Arms" Employe Seriously Wounded At Dardanelles. Mrs. Daniels, of 29, Gas-street, has received word that her son, Private William Granger, of the 3/4th South Lancashires (Kitchener's Army), has been seriously wounded at the Dar- danelles. This news arrived oh Wednesday morning officially from the Shrewsbury Records Office, and stated that the casualty took place on Monday, September 20th; place not men- tioned. A GREA'f HIT AT THE ROYAL , • COURT. "The Belle of New York" looks like earning a title to a place amortst the musical plays that never die: It must be getting near :a decade and a half ago since it first crossed over the Atlantic to enchant the ears and tickle the risible 'faculties of the British playgoer, and, without intermission, it has been doing its good work somewhere up and down this old country of ours ever since. In compressed form—though not seriously so; for all the salient charms of the piece are retained in the boiled down version—the play has this week been .delight- ing crowded houses, twice nightly, at the Royal Court Theatre, and it's a sure winner to pre- dict that the man or woman who wants to see the old favourite to-night and is not On.rly in his or her place in the queue will get left at the pay-box. The piece is being ' presented by a most capable company, who extract full measure of melody from Gustave Kerker's intensely tune- ful music, as well as an .imple tuocd of fun from the whimsical "book," with its delight- fully farcical- situations. - A- special word of praise must, be -accorded Miss Edith Drayson for her sympathetic treatment of the role of the Salvation Army lass made famous lay Miss Edna May; and M. J. Schofield, jun., is tip- top as "the mixed-ale pugilist"; -whilst Messrs. C. Gibb McLaughlin and Peter H. Gardner are extreniely good as Ichabod Bronson and the polite lunatic respectively. Super-Flevue Next Week. . . ' An unusually magnificent production, Francis Laidler's Super-Revue, entitled "The Silver Lining," .will be the attraction. next week at the Royal Court Theatre, Warrington. "The Silver Lining" centains many of the elements Of a popular pantomime, together with every- thing that goes to make an up-to-date revue. Throughout "The Silver Lining" the bright ~ide of the 'cloud is Shown. There is no male
